	<item>
		<title>Adaptive agentic intelligence framework using Strands Agents</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/adaptive-agentic-intelligence-framework-using-strands-agents/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Nitin Eusebius]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 21 Oct 2026 21:23:26 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">bc3c26fa1b9f32217f78b6ea7dade55d7572f14d</guid>

					<description>The Adaptive Agentic Intelligence Framework (AAIF), built with Strands Agents, handles routine tasks locally with small language models and routes complex requests to cloud models through Amazon Bedrock. This post walks through building AAIF with local SLMs through Ollama and Amazon Bedrock, using an Internet of Things monitoring scenario.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Organizations building AI agents face a common challenge: Every request, whether a routine status check or complex analysis, typically gets routed to a single large, expensive model. This one-size-fits-all approach can drive up API costs, introduce unpredictable latency, and remove the possibility of offline operation. Even routine queries that could resolve locally end up requiring a full network round-trip to a cloud-hosted large language model (LLM).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Adaptive Agentic Intelligence Framework (AAIF), built with Strands Agents, takes a different approach. Instead of routing all requests to a single large model, AAIF uses small language models (SLMs) to handle routine tasks locally. More complex requests are routed to cloud-hosted models through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. This approach builds on the research position from &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02153" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Belcak et al. at NVIDIA&lt;/a&gt;. They argue that small language models are “sufficiently powerful, inherently more suitable, and necessarily more economical for many invocations in agentic systems.” Built on Strands Agents, the framework demonstrates how intelligent model orchestration can help reduce costs while maintaining low latency for local operations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through the key components of AAIF using Strands Agents with local SLMs through Ollama and cloud models through Amazon Bedrock. We use an Internet of Things (IoT) monitoring scenario as a reference implementation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AAIF organizes AI processing into three layers, each optimized for different task types, as Figure 1 shows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Smart orchestration uses a lightweight SLM (llama3.2:1b) as the decision-making layer. This orchestrator classifies incoming requests and routes them to the appropriate processing layer without hardcoded rules.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Local processing handles routine tasks like device telemetry retrieval and image analysis using SLMs running on local hardware through &lt;a href="https://ollama.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ollama&lt;/a&gt;. These tasks complete with low latency and no cloud dependency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Cloud processing handles complex pattern analysis and strategic recommendations through frontier models in Amazon Bedrock. These requests need broader context and multi-step reasoning that SLMs cannot reliably provide.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-19699-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-19699-1.png" alt="AAIF three-layer architecture with an SLM orchestrator routing requests to local processing tools or to Amazon Bedrock cloud processing" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The three AAIF processing layers for smart orchestration, local processing, and cloud processing&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows the complete AAIF system architecture. At the center sits the Strands agent, which connects to an Ollama runtime hosting both the 1B-parameter orchestrator model and the vision model. The agent exposes three tools: get_device_telemetry for local sensor data retrieval, analyze_camera_feed for on-device vision processing through llama3.2-vision, and get_expert_insights which calls Amazon Bedrock through boto3 for complex analysis. Incoming user requests flow through the SLM orchestrator, which classifies each request and dispatches it to either a local tool or the cloud endpoint based on task complexity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-19699-2.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-19699-2.jpg" alt="Strands agent connected to an Ollama runtime and three tools, with get_expert_insights calling Amazon Bedrock" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Complete AAIF system architecture showing the Strands agent, Ollama runtime, tool integrations, and Amazon Bedrock connectivity&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The framework adapts to your deployment constraints rather than requiring you to restructure your infrastructure around it. This architecture runs on edge devices like a Raspberry Pi with 8 GB of memory, in full cloud deployments where all inference hits Amazon Bedrock, or in hybrid configurations that combine local speed with cloud reasoning. The flexibility comes from separating the routing logic (the SLM orchestrator) from the processing tools, which you can swap or reconfigure independently depending on available hardware and network connectivity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For this walkthrough, you should have the following prerequisites:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://portal.aws.amazon.com/gp/aws/developer/registration/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS account&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; model access configured in the required Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.python.org/downloads/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Python 3.10+&lt;/a&gt; installed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://ollama.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ollama&lt;/a&gt; installed with the following models pulled: 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;ollama pull llama3.2:1b&lt;/code&gt; (orchestrator).&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;ollama pull llama3.2-vision&lt;/code&gt; (vision analysis).&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents&lt;/a&gt; installed: &lt;code&gt;pip install strands-agents strands-agents-tools&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)&lt;/a&gt; configured with appropriate credentials.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://boto3.amazonaws.com/v1/documentation/api/latest/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Boto3&lt;/a&gt; installed: &lt;code&gt;pip install boto3&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="set-up-the-environment"&gt;Set up the environment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create a project directory and start the Ollama server:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;mkdir aaif-demo &amp;amp;&amp;amp; cd aaif-demo
ollama serve&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pull the required models:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;ollama pull llama3.2:1b
ollama pull llama3.2-vision&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create a file called &lt;code&gt;aaif_agent.py&lt;/code&gt; and add the following components.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implement-local-processing-tools"&gt;Implement local processing tools&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Strands Agents uses the &lt;code&gt;@tool&lt;/code&gt; decorator to define capabilities that agents can invoke. Start by creating tools for local processing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first tool retrieves device telemetry data. In a production environment, this would connect to actual IoT sensors. For this walkthrough, it uses simulated device data:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from strands import Agent, tool
import random

DEVICE_TELEMETRY = {
    "sensor-001": {
        "device_type": "Temperature Sensor",
        "location": "Building A - Floor 2",
        "temperature": 72.5,
        "humidity": 45.2,
        "status": "normal"
    },
    "camera-001": {
        "device_type": "Security Camera",
        "location": "Main Entrance",
        "fps": 30,
        "resolution": "1080p",
        "status": "recording"
    },
    "hvac-001": {
        "device_type": "HVAC Controller",
        "location": "Building A - Central",
        "set_temp": 71.0,
        "current_temp": 72.5,
        "mode": "cooling",
        "status": "active"
    }
}

@tool
def get_device_telemetry() -&amp;gt; str:
    """Retrieve current telemetry data from connected IoT devices."""
    device_id = random.choice(list(DEVICE_TELEMETRY.keys()))
    device_data = DEVICE_TELEMETRY[device_id]

    report = f"Device: {device_id} ({device_data['device_type']})\n"
    report += f"Location: {device_data['location']}\n"
    report += f"Status: {device_data['status']}\n"
    for key, value in device_data.items():
        if key not in ['device_type', 'location', 'status']:
            report += f"{key}: {value}\n"
    return report&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The second tool performs vision analysis using a local SLM. The llama3.2-vision model runs entirely on local hardware, which means sensitive camera feeds don’t leave the device:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import ollama

@tool
def analyze_camera_feed(image_path: str) -&amp;gt; str:
    """Analyze visual data from cameras using local vision model."""
    stream = ollama.chat(
        model='llama3.2-vision',
        messages=[{
            'role': 'user',
            'content': 'Describe this image in one sentence. '
            'What are the 3 most important things you see?',
            'images': [image_path]
        }],
        stream=True,
    )

    full_response = ""
    for chunk in stream:
        chunk_content = chunk['message']['content']
        print(chunk_content, end='', flush=True)
        full_response += chunk_content

    return f"Object Detection Report:\n{full_response}"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implement-cloud-processing"&gt;Implement cloud processing&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For complex analysis that requires broader reasoning, create a tool that calls Amazon Bedrock. This tool is invoked only when the orchestrator determines that local models can’t handle the request:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

@tool
def get_expert_insights(context_data: str, question: str) -&amp;gt; str:
    """Get advanced analysis and expert recommendations
    for complex questions via Amazon Bedrock."""
    client = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")

    conversation = [
        {
            "role": "user",
            "content": [
                {"text": f"Context: {context_data}\n\nQuestion: {question}"}
            ],
        }
    ]

    response = client.converse(
        modelId="&amp;lt;&amp;lt;model_id&amp;gt;", //Refer model id [details](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/foundation-models-reference.html)
        messages=conversation,
        inferenceConfig={
            "maxTokens": 512,
            "temperature": 0.5,
            "topP": 0.9
        }
    )

    response_text = response["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"]
    return f"Expert Analysis:\n{response_text}"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="configure-the-strands-agent-with-smart-orchestration"&gt;Configure the Strands agent with smart orchestration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The core of AAIF is the Strands agent configuration. The agent uses a local SLM (llama3.2:1b) as its orchestrator. Strands Agents supports multiple model providers, and here we use the Ollama provider to run the orchestrator locally:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from strands.models.ollama import OllamaModel

model = OllamaModel(
    model_id="llama3.2:1b",
    host="http://localhost:11434",
    params={
        "max_tokens": 300,
        "temperature": 0.3,
    },
)

system_prompt = """You are a monitoring assistant for operational facilities.
You have access to device telemetry tools, analysis capabilities,
and expert insights to help monitor operations effectively.

When users ask for:
- Device status or telemetry: use get_device_telemetry
- Image or data analysis: use analyze_camera_feed
- Expert advice or complex questions: use get_expert_insights

Always return the complete tool results directly to the user."""

agent = Agent(
    model=model,
    system_prompt=system_prompt,
    tools=[
        get_device_telemetry,
        analyze_camera_feed,
        get_expert_insights,
    ],
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The SLM-powered orchestrator analyzes each request and routes it to the appropriate tool without hardcoded keyword matching. The routing happens through the model’s understanding of the request:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Request&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Routing&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Latency&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“Check device status”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Local SLM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Low (local inference)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“Analyze this image”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Local vision AI&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Low (local inference)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“What optimization strategies do you recommend?”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cloud (Amazon Bedrock)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Higher (network round-trip)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Table 1: Request, routing, and latency estimates for local compared to cloud inference&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="run-the-agent"&gt;Run the agent&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the agent configured, you can interact with it directly:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Local processing - device telemetry
response = agent("What's the current status of our devices?")
print(response)

# Local processing - vision analysis
response = agent("Analyze the camera feed from camera-001")
print(response)

# Cloud processing - complex analysis
response = agent(
    "Based on current telemetry patterns, "
    "what optimization strategies do you recommend?"
)
print(response)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent automatically classifies each request and routes it to the appropriate processing layer. No explicit routing rules are needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="adapt-to-different-deployment-scenarios"&gt;Adapt to different deployment scenarios&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AAIF architecture adapts to different infrastructure requirements by changing the model configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When your workload runs entirely in the cloud and no edge hardware is available, replace the Ollama model configuration with Amazon Bedrock for all processing. This routes every request through managed cloud inference, giving you auto scaling without requiring local GPU resources. Teams with reliable low-latency connectivity and no data residency constraints benefit most from this configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from strands.models import BedrockModel

model = BedrockModel(
    model_id="&amp;lt;&amp;lt;model_id&amp;gt;", //Refer model id [details](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/foundation-models-reference.html)
    region_name="us-east-1"
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The hybrid configuration keeps the local SLM orchestrator intact while adding cloud models for requests that exceed local model capabilities. This is the default AAIF configuration shown in this walkthrough. It suits environments where most requests are routine enough to resolve locally, but some require the broader reasoning of a larger model. Cost savings can come from the roughly 80 percent of traffic that doesn’t hit a paid API endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For environments that are fully air-gapped or have strict data sovereignty requirements, replace the cloud tool with a larger local model and run all processing on-device. A 3B-parameter model running on hardware with adequate memory can handle moderately complex tasks that the 1B orchestrator would otherwise escalate. The trade-off is reduced reasoning depth on the hardest queries, but the benefit is data not leaving the premises:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;model = OllamaModel(
    model_id="llama3.2:3b", # Larger local model for complex tasks
    host="http://localhost:11434",
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="looking-ahead-centralized-tool-calling-with-mcp-servers"&gt;Looking ahead: Centralized tool calling with MCP servers&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The implementation in this post uses local tool calls defined directly in the agent code. For production deployments with multiple agents sharing tools across services, expose these tools as Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers. MCP provides a standardized interface for tool discovery and invocation, so you can centralize tool management and reuse tools across different agents and environments. In Figure 3, we show how to convert AAIF tools into MCP servers for distributed, centralized tool calling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the distributed configuration as shown in Figure 3, Device 1 acts as the orchestrating hub, running the Strands agent with the SLM router and a local telemetry tool. It connects through the MCP protocol to Device 2, which runs an edge vision processing server with llama3.2-vision. Device 3 serves as a cloud analytics gateway connected to Amazon Bedrock. From the orchestrator’s perspective, all remote tools appear as local function calls because the MCP transport layer handles serialization and network communication transparently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-19699-3.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-19699-3.jpg" alt="Distributed AAIF deployment with a Device 1 orchestrator connected through MCP to a Device 2 vision server and a Device 3 Amazon Bedrock analytics gateway" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Distributed intelligence with MCP servers&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cleaning-up"&gt;Cleaning up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid incurring future charges, complete the following cleanup steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Disable Amazon Bedrock model access: Open the Amazon Bedrock console, choose &lt;strong&gt;Model access&lt;/strong&gt;, and revoke access for Amazon Nova Lite. For detailed steps, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-access.html#model-access-modify" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Manage model access&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Stop the Ollama server and remove models to free local disk space (approximately 2 GB): 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;ollama stop
ollama rm llama3.2:1b
ollama rm llama3.2-vision&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the project directory if you no longer need the sample code: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;rm -rf aaif-demo&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We showed how to build cost-effective AI agents using the Adaptive Agentic Intelligence Framework with Strands Agents. By routing routine requests to local SLMs and reserving cloud models on Amazon Bedrock for complex analysis, AAIF can help reduce API costs while maintaining low latency for routine operations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you run this pattern in production, add responsible AI controls to the cloud inference path, since those requests carry tool outputs, retrieved data, and user input. You can attach &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; to the Strands BedrockModel with a guardrail ID and version. It then applies content filters for harmful categories, prompt injection filters, and contextual grounding checks that block responses not supported by the source data. To apply the same policies to the local SLM tier, call the standalone ApplyGuardrail API on its inputs and outputs. This way, every request follows one safety standard no matter which model handles it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This architectural pattern applies beyond IoT monitoring. A healthcare organization could run patient monitoring locally while routing diagnosis support to cloud models. A financial services firm could process transactions on-premises and reserve cloud capacity for risk analysis. A manufacturing team could run quality control vision models at the edge while sending optimization requests to the cloud.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about building agents with Strands Agents, visit the &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/docs/user-guide/quickstart/overview/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents documentation&lt;/a&gt;. For information about available foundation models, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

  &lt;div class="blog-author-image" style="margin-right: 1.0em"&gt;
   &lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-19699-5.jpg" alt="Mani Khanuja" width="100" height="133"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Train Qwen3 on AWS Trainium with torchtitan</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/train-qwen3-on-aws-trainium-with-torchtitan/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Josh Longenecker]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 21 Oct 2026 20:00:22 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Trainium]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">54e8299bd0c441c1532fc17ed8f18dacc33ccc0d</guid>

					<description>Train Qwen3 on AWS Trainium with torchtitan three ways on a single trn2.48xlarge: the Hugging Face Transformers v5 modeling backend, torchtitan's native Qwen3, and NKI mixture-of-experts kernels. The same torchrun launcher and config structure work unchanged across all three paths, including loading and saving published Hugging Face safetensors.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Using torchtitan on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/trainium/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Trainium&lt;/a&gt; means using it the way you always have. TorchNeuron registers Trainium as a native PyTorch device, which lets torchtitan use it through standard PyTorch interfaces. You can use these configurations to pre-train from random initialization or fine-tune published weights. You can also customize the model implementation and kernels for your workload. In this post, you train Qwen3 three ways on a single &lt;code&gt;trn2.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first method uses Transformers v5 as a modeling backend in torchtitan. It runs Hub models through &lt;code&gt;AutoModelForCausalLM&lt;/code&gt; and reads and writes published safetensors directly. The second method switches to torchtitan’s native Qwen3 by changing &lt;code&gt;config.model_spec&lt;/code&gt;. The third adds Neuron Kernel Interface (NKI) mixture-of-experts (MoE) kernels from the torchtitan-neuron recipes and then uses torchtitan’s override registry to attach your own kernel. All three configurations use the same &lt;code&gt;torchrun&lt;/code&gt; launch pattern and config structure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow this walkthrough, use a single &lt;code&gt;trn2.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; with either the AWS Deep Learning AMI for Neuron or a Neuron PyTorch training container. Both environments provide the Neuron driver, runtime, and compiler required to train on Trainium.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you install &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-neuron/torchtitan-neuron" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;torchtitan-neuron&lt;/a&gt;, check whether your environment already includes it. The following command prints the installed version and package path:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python3 -c "import torchtitan, torchtitan_neuron; print(torchtitan.__version__, torchtitan_neuron.__file__)"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This walkthrough uses the versions in the following table. To reproduce the examples, pin these versions. The Transformers v5 integration and Trainium-specific override modules can change between torchtitan releases.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Component&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Version&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Instance&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;trn2.48xlarge, 16 Trainium2 accelerators, 128 physical NeuronCores&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Logical NeuronCores&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;64 at LNC=2&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;torch, torch_neuronx&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2.14.0, 2.14.3&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;transformers&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5.16.0&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;torchtitan-neuron&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;release-2.33 (0.2.2+neuron.1)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When &lt;code&gt;torch_neuronx&lt;/code&gt; is installed, TorchNeuron registers Trainium as the &lt;code&gt;neuron&lt;/code&gt; device and torchtitan detects it at startup. In eager mode, Neuron compiles operations as they execute. With &lt;code&gt;torch.compile&lt;/code&gt;, Neuron compiles the captured graph. This same launch pattern works across all three configurations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the versions used in this walkthrough, install torchtitan-neuron and torchtitan’s remaining dependencies with &lt;code&gt;--no-deps&lt;/code&gt; to preserve the Neuron build of torch:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-neuron/torchtitan-neuron
cd torchtitan-neuron
pip install -r requirements.txt&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you start training, get the Qwen3 model artifacts from Hugging Face Hub. Store them in a local directory that you can pass to the training config:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;hf download Qwen/Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 \
  --local-dir "$PWD/assets/hf/Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each training configuration is a Python config function. The three examples start from a base config that you update for each step.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="train-with-hugging-face-transformers-v5"&gt;Train with Hugging Face Transformers v5&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the first configuration, Transformers v5 builds Qwen3 through &lt;code&gt;AutoModelForCausalLM&lt;/code&gt;. The Hub model definition supplies the implementation used for the forward and backward passes. You select the model by setting its Hub ID in the config.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start with &lt;code&gt;transformers_modeling_backend_full()&lt;/code&gt;. It leaves the fields that map to Hugging Face configuration keys unset, so &lt;code&gt;AutoConfig.from_pretrained()&lt;/code&gt; loads the model architecture from the Hub.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create &lt;code&gt;neuron_qwen3.py&lt;/code&gt; in the torchtitan-neuron repository root, where you run the training command. Add the imports, model artifact path, and base config:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import os
from torchtitan.experiments.transformers_modeling_backend.config_registry import (
    transformers_modeling_backend_full,
)
from torchtitan.experiments.transformers_modeling_backend.configs import (
    TransformersBackendConfig,
)

ASSETS = os.path.abspath("./assets/hf/Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507")

def v5_qwen3_4b() -&amp;gt; TransformersBackendConfig:
    config = transformers_modeling_backend_full()
    config.hf_model = "Qwen/Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507"
    config.hf_assets_path = ASSETS
    config.dataloader.dataset = "c4_test"
    config.training.steps = 10
    config.training.local_batch_size = 1
    config.parallelism.data_parallel_shard_degree = -1
    return config&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;torchrun --nproc_per_node=64 --rdzv_backend c10d --rdzv_endpoint localhost:0 \
  --local-ranks-filter 0 --role rank --tee 3 \
  -m torchtitan.train --module neuron_qwen3 --config v5_qwen3_4b&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This command launches 64 training processes, one for each logical NeuronCore. &lt;code&gt;--module neuron_qwen3&lt;/code&gt; imports the local config module. &lt;code&gt;--config v5_qwen3_4b&lt;/code&gt; selects the configuration function. The data parallel setting shards the model across all 64 ranks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="load-and-save-hugging-face-checkpoints"&gt;Load and save Hugging Face checkpoints&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To fine-tune from published weights, enable checkpoint loading and point &lt;code&gt;initial_load_path&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;ASSETS&lt;/code&gt;. The state-dictionary adapter adjusts the leading &lt;code&gt;model.&lt;/code&gt; prefix and handles tied embeddings. It saves the final checkpoint as Hugging Face safetensors.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def v5_qwen3_4b_finetune() -&amp;gt; TransformersBackendConfig:
    config = v5_qwen3_4b()
    config.checkpoint.enable = True
    config.checkpoint.folder = "checkpoint_qwen3_4b_ft"
    config.checkpoint.initial_load_path = ASSETS # absolute path required
    config.checkpoint.initial_load_in_hf = True
    config.checkpoint.initial_load_model_only = True
    config.checkpoint.last_save_in_hf = True
    config.checkpoint.last_save_model_only = True
    return config&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use the first training-step loss as a quick check that torchtitan loaded the published weights. For &lt;code&gt;Qwen/Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507&lt;/code&gt;, the verified run produced a step 1 loss of 6.54 from published weights and 12.43 from random initialization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After training, load the exported checkpoint with &lt;code&gt;AutoModelForCausalLM&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;load_state_dict&lt;/code&gt;. A successful round trip reports no missing or unexpected keys other than &lt;code&gt;lm_head.weight&lt;/code&gt;, which Qwen3-4B ties to the token embeddings. This confirms that the files remain a standard Hugging Face checkpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-22120-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-22120-1.png" alt="Training output showing a step 1 loss of 6.54 and the exported checkpoint loading with no missing or unexpected keys" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The published checkpoint starts with a loss of 6.54, and the exported weights load without missing or unexpected keys&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="train-with-torchtitans-native-models"&gt;Train with torchtitan’s native models&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the second configuration, torchtitan builds its native &lt;code&gt;Qwen3Model&lt;/code&gt;. Add the native Qwen3 imports and config function to &lt;code&gt;neuron_qwen3.py&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This example starts from &lt;code&gt;qwen3_32b()&lt;/code&gt; because the tested torchtitan build does not provide a generic &lt;code&gt;qwen3_4b()&lt;/code&gt; config builder. The next line replaces the 32B model specification with the 4B specification. The remaining fields reproduce the tested run.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from torchtitan.models.qwen3 import model_registry
from torchtitan.models.qwen3.config_registry import qwen3_32b
from torchtitan.trainer import Trainer

def native_qwen3_4b() -&amp;gt; Trainer.Config:
    config = qwen3_32b()
    config.model_spec = model_registry("4B", attn_backend="sdpa")
    config.hf_assets_path = ASSETS
    config.dataloader.dataset = "c4_test"
    config.training.steps = 10
    config.training.local_batch_size = 1
    config.training.global_batch_size = 64
    config.parallelism.data_parallel_shard_degree = -1
    return config&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;torchrun --nproc_per_node=64 --rdzv_backend c10d --rdzv_endpoint localhost:0 \
  --local-ranks-filter 0 --role rank --tee 3 \
  -m torchtitan.train --module neuron_qwen3 --config native_qwen3_4b&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;config.model_spec&lt;/code&gt; selects the model class and defines how torchtitan shards the model and maps its checkpoint keys. Here, &lt;code&gt;model_registry("4B", attn_backend="sdpa")&lt;/code&gt; selects torchtitan’s native Qwen3 implementation with scaled dot product attention (SDPA).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="add-nki-kernels-for-mixture-of-experts"&gt;Add NKI kernels for mixture of experts&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the third configuration, torchtitan runs mixture-of-experts layers with kernels written in NKI, the Neuron kernel language. Add the required imports and Qwen3 MoE config function to &lt;code&gt;neuron_qwen3.py&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from torchtitan.config import CompileConfig
from torchtitan_neuron.models.qwen3.config_registry import qwen3_nki_moe_config

def nki_qwen3_30b_a3b() -&amp;gt; Trainer.Config:
    config = qwen3_nki_moe_config(
        "30B-A3B",
        compile_config=CompileConfig(enable=False, backend="neuron"),
        n_layers=4,
        num_experts=64,
        top_k=4,
    )
    config.hf_assets_path = ASSETS
    return config&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;torchrun --nproc_per_node=64 --rdzv_backend c10d --rdzv_endpoint localhost:0 \
  --local-ranks-filter 0 --role rank --tee 3 \
  -m torchtitan.train --module neuron_qwen3 --config nki_qwen3_30b_a3b&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This configuration uses data parallel sharding across 64 ranks with a tensor parallel degree of 1. It does not use expert parallelism. &lt;code&gt;num_experts=64&lt;/code&gt; sets the number of experts in the model, not the expert parallel degree.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Setting &lt;code&gt;CompileConfig(enable=False)&lt;/code&gt; runs this example in eager mode. TorchNeuron supports registered NKI operators in both eager execution and &lt;code&gt;torch.compile&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the torchtitan build used for this walkthrough, &lt;code&gt;qwen3_nki_moe_config()&lt;/code&gt; replaces the MoE block with the NKI grouped-experts implementation. It also selects SDPA and the gather-free rotary embedding. When you enable &lt;code&gt;torch.compile&lt;/code&gt;, add &lt;code&gt;"grad_clip"&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;CompileConfig.components&lt;/code&gt; to compile gradient-norm clipping.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NKI kernels enter training as PyTorch operators. &lt;code&gt;wrap_nki&lt;/code&gt; makes an NKI kernel callable from PyTorch, and &lt;code&gt;nki_op&lt;/code&gt; registers the operator. &lt;code&gt;register_autograd&lt;/code&gt; attaches its backward pass using routing metadata saved during the forward pass.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The example reduces Qwen3-30B-A3B to 4 layers and 64 experts so it fits on one &lt;code&gt;trn2.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt;. It routes each token to 4 experts with &lt;code&gt;top_k=4&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="attach-your-own-nki-kernel"&gt;Attach your own NKI kernel&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In some cases, you might want to optimize an operation for the tensor shapes in your model. A specialized kernel can tailor its tiling and data movement to those shapes. It can also replace a performance-critical sequence of PyTorch operations with one optimized operator. You can write the kernel yourself or start with one from &lt;code&gt;nkilib&lt;/code&gt;. This example uses &lt;code&gt;rope_hf&lt;/code&gt; from &lt;code&gt;nkilib.core.embeddings&lt;/code&gt;, which expects the Hugging Face tensor layout and supports its own backward mode. This example demonstrates kernel registration and autograd integration, not a performance improvement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create &lt;code&gt;my_neuron_kernels.py&lt;/code&gt; in the repository root, where you launch training. torchtitan stores query and key as &lt;code&gt;[batch, seq, heads, head_dim]&lt;/code&gt;, whereas &lt;code&gt;rope_hf&lt;/code&gt; expects &lt;code&gt;[batch, heads, seq, head_dim]&lt;/code&gt;. Convert the layout inside the operator and allocate the output tensors with &lt;code&gt;torch.empty_like()&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import torch
from nkilib.core.embeddings.rope_hf import rope_hf
from torch_neuronx import nki_op
from torch_neuronx.nki_hop import wrap_nki
from torch_neuronx.utils import get_logical_neuron_cores

LNC = (int(get_logical_neuron_cores()),)
wrapped_rope = wrap_nki(rope_hf)

@nki_op("my_kernels::rope_fwd")
def rope_fwd(query, key, rope_cache):
    q = query.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous()
    k = key.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous()
    cache = rope_cache.reshape(-1, rope_cache.shape[-1])
    q_out, k_out = wrapped_rope[LNC](
        q,
        k,
        torch.empty_like(q),
        torch.empty_like(k),
        rope_cache=cache,
    )
    return (
        q_out.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous(),
        k_out.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous(),
    )&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;rope_hf&lt;/code&gt; computes gradients when you call it with &lt;code&gt;backward=True&lt;/code&gt;. Register a second operator for that mode. Save the rotary cache during the forward pass so the backward pass can reuse it. Return &lt;code&gt;None&lt;/code&gt; for the cache gradient because the cache is not differentiable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;@nki_op("my_kernels::rope_bwd")
def rope_bwd(grad_query, grad_key, rope_cache):
    gq = grad_query.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous()
    gk = grad_key.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous()
    cache = rope_cache.reshape(-1, rope_cache.shape[-1])
    out_q, out_k = wrapped_rope[LNC](
        gq,
        gk,
        torch.empty_like(gq),
        torch.empty_like(gk),
        rope_cache=cache,
        backward=True,
    )
    return (
        out_q.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous(),
        out_k.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous(),
    )&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def _setup_context(ctx, inputs, output):
    ctx.save_for_backward(inputs[2])

def _backward(ctx, grad_query, grad_key):
    (rope_cache,) = ctx.saved_tensors
    grad_q, grad_k = rope_bwd(grad_query, grad_key, rope_cache)
    return grad_q, grad_k, None

rope_fwd.register_autograd(_backward, setup_context=_setup_context)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because &lt;code&gt;rope_hf&lt;/code&gt; requires a supported sequence length, validate that constraint before calling the kernel. Subclass &lt;code&gt;CosSinRoPE&lt;/code&gt; and call &lt;code&gt;rope_fwd&lt;/code&gt; from &lt;code&gt;apply_rotary_emb&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from dataclasses import dataclass
from torchtitan.config import derive, override
from torchtitan.models.common.rope import CosSinRoPE

class NKIRoPE(CosSinRoPE):
    @dataclass(kw_only=True, slots=True)
    class Config(CosSinRoPE.Config):
        pass

    @staticmethod
    def apply_rotary_emb(query, key, rope_cache):
        stride = 128 * LNC[0]
        if query.shape[1] % stride:
            raise ValueError(
                f"rope_hf requires a sequence length that is a multiple of "
                f"128 * LNC={stride}, got {query.shape[1]}"
            )
        q_out, k_out = rope_fwd(query, key, rope_cache)
        return q_out.type_as(query), k_out.type_as(key)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Register &lt;code&gt;NKIRoPE&lt;/code&gt; as the replacement for &lt;code&gt;CosSinRoPE&lt;/code&gt;. &lt;code&gt;derive()&lt;/code&gt; copies the shared configuration fields into the replacement config:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;@override(
    "nki_rope",
    target=CosSinRoPE.Config,
    exact=True,
    description="nkilib rope_hf kernel in place of the PyTorch rotation",
)
def _nki_rope(cfg: CosSinRoPE.Config) -&amp;gt; NKIRoPE.Config:
    return derive(cfg, NKIRoPE.Config)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To load the custom override, pass its module name to &lt;code&gt;--override.imports&lt;/code&gt;. torchtitan imports the registry entry before it builds the model:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;torchrun --nproc_per_node=64 --rdzv_backend c10d --rdzv_endpoint localhost:0 \
  --local-ranks-filter 0 --role rank --tee 3 \
  -m torchtitan.train --module neuron_qwen3 --config native_qwen3_4b \
  --override.imports my_neuron_kernels&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before training starts, torchtitan logs each config node that the override replaces. Confirm that the output contains one &lt;code&gt;nki_rope&lt;/code&gt; entry for each Qwen3 layer:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;[Override] nki_rope: model_spec.model.layers.0.attention.rope CosSinRoPEConfig -&amp;gt; NKIRoPE.Config
[Override] nki_rope: model_spec.model.layers.1.attention.rope CosSinRoPEConfig -&amp;gt; NKIRoPE.Config&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set the sequence length to a multiple of &lt;code&gt;128 * LNC&lt;/code&gt;, which is 256 when LNC is 2. Reshape the rotary cache to &lt;code&gt;[seq, head_dim * 2]&lt;/code&gt;, with cosine values in the first half and sine values in the second. The kernel validates these constraints before training begins.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a complete torchtitan example, see &lt;code&gt;torchtitan_neuron/models/gpt_oss/nki_attention.py&lt;/code&gt;. It connects &lt;code&gt;nkilib&lt;/code&gt; flash-attention kernels through the same operator and override mechanisms. It also handles &lt;code&gt;DTensor&lt;/code&gt; inputs for tensor parallelism.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you train with a custom kernel, compare its outputs with the implementation it replaces. In the numerical test with float32 inputs, &lt;code&gt;rope_hf&lt;/code&gt; and torchtitan’s PyTorch rotary embedding produced a maximum absolute difference of 0.0 for both query and key.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you register multiple overrides, limit each one to the intended module instances with &lt;code&gt;fqns&lt;/code&gt;. For example, &lt;code&gt;fqns=["layers.1[0-9].attention.rope"]&lt;/code&gt; targets the rotary embedding in layers 10 through 19. torchtitan stops when two overrides claim the same node and warns when an override matches no nodes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="review-the-training-output"&gt;Review the training output&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At startup, confirm that torchtitan reports 64 ranks and the expected model. The first step includes compilation and takes longer than later steps. Each training step reports the loss, gradient norm, high-bandwidth memory (HBM) usage, throughput, and model FLOPs utilization (MFU). For the checkpoint run, confirm that the first-step loss is near 6.54. For the custom kernel run, confirm that the &lt;code&gt;nki_rope&lt;/code&gt; override appears once for each Qwen3 layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scale-across-the-instance"&gt;Scale across the instance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same parallelism settings apply to the Transformers and native model configurations. A &lt;code&gt;trn2.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; exposes 64 logical NeuronCores at LNC=2. Set &lt;code&gt;data_parallel_shard_degree = -1&lt;/code&gt; to shard the model parameters, gradients, and optimizer state across every rank that &lt;code&gt;torchrun&lt;/code&gt; starts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set &lt;code&gt;config.parallelism.tensor_parallel_degree&lt;/code&gt; to shard each layer’s weights across a group of ranks. Each rank computes a slice of every matrix multiplication. With sequence parallelism enabled by default, normalization layers run on activations sharded along the sequence dimension. torchtitan combines tensor parallelism with Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP) on a multidimensional &lt;code&gt;DeviceMesh&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Watch per-rank HBM as you increase the model size, sequence length, or local batch size. These values determine how much memory each rank requires.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you want to keep your checkpoints, copy them to persistent storage before you clean up. If you plan to reuse the instance, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/instance-stop-methods.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;stop it&lt;/a&gt;. When you no longer need the instance, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/terminating-instances.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;terminate it&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/trainium/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Trainium&lt;/a&gt; works as a supported torchtitan device across all three configurations in this walkthrough. You can train Qwen3 through Hugging Face Transformers v5 and load or save published safetensors directly. You can also train with torchtitan’s native Qwen3 implementation by setting &lt;code&gt;config.model_spec&lt;/code&gt;. For mixture-of-experts models, use the torchtitan-neuron NKI recipes. For custom kernels, use the override registry to attach NKI operators through the same launch flow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For setup guidance, see the &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Neuron documentation&lt;/a&gt;. For current source and training recipes, see the torchtitan repository.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How U.S. Hunger connects leaders and data using Amazon Quick</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-u-s-hunger-connects-leaders-and-data-using-amazon-quick/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rick Whitted]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 21 Oct 2026 19:19:50 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">3041691fe51fb842c8451055f54e6f1d4b96e4bf</guid>

					<description>U.S. Hunger, a nonprofit addressing food insecurity, uses Amazon Quick to connect organizational data with leadership decisions. Leaders explore applicant data directly, share KPI definitions and financial context in Quick Spaces, and investigate policy questions with Quick Research.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;For U.S. Hunger, feeding families takes more than delivering food to them. It takes understanding the people behind each request, the pressures they face, and the business that makes the mission possible. That means connecting information across the organization. Its mission is to feed families today and unite them to a healthier tomorrow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;U.S. Hunger reports delivering more than 250 million meals. Its work has reached 59 countries historically and is now concentrated primarily in the United States. The organization reports approximately 500 percent growth over four years, compared with only 15–20 percent headcount growth. For a lean team at that scale, every hour and every decision matters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt;, U.S. Hunger can explore organizational data directly, colleagues can work from shared definitions, and leaders can examine financial information alongside the circumstances families report. U.S. Hunger’s experience shows how those capabilities fit into the daily work of a lean nonprofit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="from-banking-to-feeding-families"&gt;From banking to feeding families&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Rick Whitted spent 30 years in banking before making the leap to lead U.S. Hunger. His connection to food insecurity, however, began long before that career change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When Rick and his wife were in their mid-twenties, he left his career to start a business. They lost everything. Even though he went to work every day, they relied on help from family to get by. He remembers coming home to find a pantry box on the table, left by aunts or grandparents. Seeing that box broke him.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;More than 20 years later, when the board chair asked him to consider leading U.S. Hunger, his wife reminded him of those days, when their own family needed help putting food on the table. To her, leading U.S. Hunger was a chance to help families going through what they had experienced themselves. Rick cried, called the chair back, and accepted.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“The families we serve today, their stories are my story.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Rick Whitted, President and CEO, U.S. Hunger&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;More than six years into the role, that personal connection remains at the heart of Rick’s leadership.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="from-data-requests-to-direct-exploration"&gt;From data requests to direct exploration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;U.S. Hunger first learned about Quick through an &lt;a href="https://startups.aws.com/programs" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Social Entrepreneur Accelerator&lt;/a&gt; event led by Kat Esser. Rick saw an opportunity to explore the organization’s data directly, following questions as they arose without asking the technology team for a new data pull each time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Chief technology officer (CTO) Ezekiel Brooks helped make that possible by giving Rick access to core datasets from Full Cart, U.S. Hunger’s food-assistance program. Full Cart delivers donor-funded food directly to households in need. Quick gave Rick room to explore the information at his own pace and ask follow-up questions. The organization’s data became his sandbox.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The data comes from Impact Manager, U.S. Hunger’s proprietary cloud-based data platform supporting its food-assistance work. Quick pulls data from SQL Server into its in-memory engine, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/spice.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SPICE&lt;/a&gt;, through scheduled refresh jobs at 2 AM ET each night. The technology team and a colleague working with research data prepare the datasets and provide definitions to help Rick interpret the information.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-1.png" alt="Current architecture: SQL Server data refreshes into SPICE at 2 AM ET each night for analysis in Amazon Quick." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: U.S. Hunger refreshes SQL Server data into SPICE at 2 AM ET each night for analysis in Amazon Quick&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Direct exploration was the starting point, but the moment when everything clicked came with a different discovery: &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/spaces/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Quick Spaces&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-moment-quick-became-an-enterprise-tool"&gt;The moment Quick became an enterprise tool&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Rick, Spaces changed the role Quick could play in the organization. A Space gives colleagues a shared place to work with documents, definitions, and analyses relevant to a particular area of the business.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That matters when leaders need to understand one another’s work and make decisions together. Shared definitions of key performance indicators (KPIs) help them agree on what a measure means. Financial documents and planning tools provide context for discussions about the organization’s resources. Rick sees this as his a-ha moment: using Quick to help the leadership team run the organization together.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Quick provides a live connection to how leadership thinks through problems. I can pick up where our CEO left off in a financial model or see how he’s framing a KPI, without scheduling another meeting.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Sarah Aranguibel, Chief of Staff, U.S. Hunger&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The leadership team brings together a shared document of KPIs, developed over more than a year while working across NetSuite, Salesforce, Impact Manager, and its payroll system. The document records definitions, where measures come from, and goals. Rick also uses Quick to develop definitions for client tiers and shares them in the same Space.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“With all the knowledge and data we have, Space reduces as much noise as possible and narrows my discussion.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Rick Whitted, President and CEO, U.S. Hunger&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Selecting the Space relevant to a question keeps the conversation focused on the right documents. The financial Space is shared with the CTO, chief operating officer (COO), and finance leadership, giving colleagues access to the tools and business context behind an analysis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Together, these materials show colleagues what the organization measures and the business logic behind it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-quick-does-for-the-team-day-to-day"&gt;What Quick does for the team, day to day&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;U.S. Hunger has given 17 colleagues access to Quick across its executive leadership, technology team, Full Cart program, Finance, Compliance, and supporting functions. For its CEO, chat is the primary day-to-day artificial intelligence (AI) workspace, and Spaces is the capability he uses most.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="improving-call-reporting"&gt;Improving call reporting&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Using a Space containing Salesforce call reports, Rick is drafting instructions for the technology team: which measures to include, where the data comes from, and what each measure means. In Quick, he can comment directly on the generated draft and regenerate it with multiple changes incorporated. He can review and refine the instructions alongside the source reports, without spending time moving documents and comments between Word and chat.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After completion, the instructions will guide reporting improvements for the member-engagement team, helping the program’s operational leaders and COO monitor the team’s work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="research-and-policy"&gt;Research and policy&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;U.S. Hunger uses Amazon &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/research/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Quick Research&lt;/a&gt; to investigate current events, inform communications, and connect policy questions with its survey data. The CEO is also exploring how that data could serve a wider audience. With the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/research-versions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;version history&lt;/a&gt; in Quick Research, he can revisit earlier versions as he refines an investigation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Understanding food insecurity requires information about who is struggling and the circumstances behind that struggle. In September 2025, the U.S. Department of Agriculture (USDA) &lt;a href="https://www.fna.usda.gov/newsroom/usda-0219.25" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;announced&lt;/a&gt; that it would end future Household Food Security Reports. With that longstanding reporting series ending, access to other sources of evidence becomes especially important.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;U.S. Hunger wants to make the information it collects available beyond its own organization. It envisions a national hunger-data resource where researchers and the public can explore the experiences of families seeking assistance. Alongside that access, the organization plans an enterprise offering that companies can purchase, helping fund its mission.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As Rick puts it, the goal is for “&lt;em&gt;the very data we’re using to help people&lt;/em&gt;” to become “&lt;em&gt;the flywheel that feeds the very people we’re trying to help&lt;/em&gt;.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="executive-insight-and-financial-planning"&gt;Executive insight and financial planning&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The national dashboard brings food insecurity together with income, education, health, and social needs. Filters help leaders examine different populations and use those findings in conversations with prospective partners about why families need help.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For financial planning, Rick uses a Space containing audits, cash-flow information, and board budget views. A debt-service calculator and nonprofit financial-health worksheet help him share his banking experience with the COO. The financial documents provide context for drafting audit footnotes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With over a decade of financial documents available in the Space, Rick can ask Quick to compare patterns over time and explore questions about current operations. His familiarity with the source material helps him check the answers and challenge conclusions the evidence doesn’t support.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Together, these workflows help Rick use Quick to explore both sides of the organization’s mission: understanding the families who need support and planning the finances that help U.S. Hunger provide it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="technology-as-social-impact"&gt;Technology as social impact&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Putting food in an empty pantry meets an immediate need. Understanding what keeps that pantry empty means looking at the circumstances inside the home and the pressures around it, including income, housing, employment, and health.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;U.S. Hunger combines structured survey responses with narrative testimony collected since 2020. Its records include more than 300,000 applicants. Behind those records are lives, lived experiences, and families.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-2.png" alt="Two dashboard cards side by side. Left: Total Applicants, Since 2020, From ALL_APPLICANTS_UNFILTERED, 304,348. Right: Applicants who Skip Meals, 91.2 percent. State, county, and ZIP code filters are set to ALL." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: U.S. Hunger records 304,348 applicants since 2020 (left) and 91.2 percent of applicants report skipping meals (right)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Affordability is a central theme. Among respondents to the questions shown, 90.1 percent say they sometimes or often can’t afford balanced meals. Separately, 97 percent report difficulty paying for basics such as food, housing, medical care, and heating.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-3.png" alt="Four dashboard panels arranged in a 2 by 2 grid: food insecurity frequency, ability to afford balanced meals, worry about food running out, and difficulty paying for basics. The last panel shows 55 percent Very Hard and 42 percent Somewhat Hard, totaling 97 percent." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Food insecurity frequency (top left), ability to afford balanced meals (top right), worry about food running out (bottom left), and difficulty paying for basics (bottom right)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“In reality, it’s about affordability.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Rick Whitted, President and CEO, U.S. Hunger&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team supplies business definitions alongside the data. U.S. Hunger uses classification codes known as Z codes to categorize reported social and economic circumstances, including food insecurity, low income, and housing difficulties. Together, these views form what Rick calls “&lt;em&gt;an EKG reading on hunger.&lt;/em&gt;”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Food insecurity is the most frequently recorded Z code in this view, followed by low income. The other leading categories concern housing or financial circumstances. Leaders can examine the pressures accompanying requests for food, rather than looking at food needs in isolation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-4.png" alt="Ranked Z codes: food insecurity 67.4 percent. Low income 28.3 percent. Other housing and economic problems 17.8 percent. Financial insecurity 11.8 percent. Inadequate housing environmental temperature 10.6 percent. The adjacent descriptions appear in a different order." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Food insecurity, low income, and housing or financial circumstances are the leading Z-code categories in this view. Applicants can have multiple codes, so the percentages are not mutually exclusive and need not total 100 percent&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Another view shows 44.7 percent of all applicants reporting illness and 79.1 percent enrolled in health insurance. In the employment view, 64.4 percent say they don’t need or want help finding or keeping work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These findings show why requests for food assistance need to be understood alongside families’ wider circumstances. Having health insurance doesn’t necessarily remove financial pressure, and help finding work is not what every applicant is seeking.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-5.png" alt="Live with illness 44.7 percent, illness in household 25.8 percent, enrolled in health insurance 79.1 percent. These are separate measures and do not establish overlap or causality." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Separate dashboard measures show reported illness and health insurance enrollment&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;U.S. Hunger has been working to organize its data to better understand the pressures families face. Quick helps the team visualize patterns in that information. Rick describes one pattern as an early warning signal: periodic difficulty obtaining enough food, before families reach daily or weekly food insecurity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“We were on that journey, but [Quick] really helped us visualize that and lay that out much faster.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Rick Whitted, President and CEO, U.S. Hunger&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Rick, this is where technology becomes a form of social impact. Quick helps U.S. Hunger look past a request for groceries to the pressures behind it. Feeding a family meets today’s need. Understanding those pressures is part of helping that family toward a healthier tomorrow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="a-message-to-other-nonprofits"&gt;A message to other nonprofits&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“501C is a tax designation. You’re still a corporation, which means you’re a business. And if you’re a business not embracing technology, you will get left behind – or your mission will cost you more.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Rick Whitted, President and CEO, U.S. Hunger&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A nonprofit still has a business to run. Its leaders need to understand cash flow, agree on what performance measures mean, and connect operational decisions to their mission. U.S. Hunger uses Quick to bring that work together. Financial documents give Rick context for planning. Shared KPI definitions give colleagues a common reference. Dashboards help leaders examine the circumstances behind requests for food.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Getting started didn’t require every system to be connected. Historical financial documents and questions Rick already needed to answer gave him a practical starting point. Other nonprofits can begin with a question that matters to their work and relevant information they have available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The people who know the organization remain the subject matter experts. They supply the context, check the answers, and decide what action makes sense. Quick helps them examine the evidence and bring it into those decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“This is not about bringing humans into the AI loop. It’s about bringing AI into the human loop.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Rick Whitted, President and CEO, U.S. Hunger&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-comes-next-for-u.s.-hunger-and-amazon-quick"&gt;What comes next for U.S. Hunger and Amazon Quick&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;U.S. Hunger is developing an enterprise KPI framework during Q4 2026. Boomi already connects NetSuite, Salesforce, and Impact Manager. The organization plans to use Boomi to bring research data, historical analytics, and enterprise KPIs into &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/redshift/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Redshift&lt;/a&gt;, which will supply data to Quick through scheduled SPICE jobs. Figure 6 shows the architecture supporting that planned expansion.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/21/ML-22201-6.png" alt="Planned architecture: data flows from business systems through Boomi into Amazon Redshift, then to Amazon Quick through scheduled SPICE jobs" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: U.S. Hunger’s planned enterprise KPI architecture brings data from business systems through Boomi into Amazon Redshift for analysis in Amazon Quick&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The diagram shows a planned pipeline bringing information from Impact Manager, NetSuite, Salesforce, Paylocity, ThriveLink, and iQpay through Boomi into Amazon Redshift. Scheduled SPICE jobs then supply the data to Quick.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The next step for U.S. Hunger is to explore how its hunger data could become a national resource for researchers, the public, and companies. Rick has asked AWS for help shaping that work. The ambition extends the role of technology beyond internal analysis: Knowledge gathered while helping families could support research and generate revenue to help feed more families.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;U.S. Hunger’s goal remains to feed families today and unite them to a healthier tomorrow. Direct data exploration, shared executive context, research, and financial analysis help its leaders understand both the organization they run and the families at the center of its mission.&lt;/p&gt;
