			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Beyond the KV cache: Training and serving i14’s Laplace 3B linear attention model on AWS Trainium2 with native PyTorch</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/beyond-the-kv-cache-training-and-serving-i14s-laplace-3b-linear-attention-model-on-aws-trainium2-with-native-pytorch/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Kevin Shabahang]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 20 Oct 2026 21:14:10 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Trainium]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">733fa955e4db373fd2ec88938f4d9a6aa8ad0a49</guid>

					<description>Learn how i14 trained and served Laplace 3B, a linear attention model, on AWS Trainium2 using native PyTorch. Its recurrent state stays fixed at 29.5 MB per sequence regardless of context length, avoiding the growing key-value cache of transformer models and keeping serving memory predictable.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Most large language models (LLMs) use transformer architectures with softmax attention. During inference, their key-value (KV) cache grows with the conversation or document context. This can reduce concurrency, increase serving cost, and force agentic applications to discard useful history.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://i14.ai" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;i14&lt;/a&gt; designed Laplace 3B as a linear attention alternative. Its recurrent state stays at 29.5 MB per sequence, independent of context length. Working with AWS, i14 used &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/frameworks/torch/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;native PyTorch support through TorchNeuron Native&lt;/a&gt; to bring this custom architecture to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/trainium/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Trainium2&lt;/a&gt;. This preserved its Gated Linear Attention (GLA) design and Muon optimizer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On one trn2.48xlarge instance, Laplace 3B trained at about 120,000 tokens per second. The model served 11,544 output tokens per second across 1,280 concurrent sequences. This post explains the model design, the changes i14 made for Trainium2, and the training and inference results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="about-i14"&gt;About i14&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;i14 is an AI research and technology company that develops model architectures, components, and runtimes. Its work draws on AI, computational cognitive science, and research into how biological systems learn over time. i14 applies these ideas to models that can retain long-range context without allowing serving state to grow with every token.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-linear-attention-models"&gt;Why linear attention models?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Transformer softmax attention compares tokens across the context. During training, this computation grows quadratically with sequence length. During inference, the KV cache grows as the model processes more tokens.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Linear attention reorganizes the computation around a recurrent state that is updated for each token. Laplace 3B uses GLA to compress prior context into a fixed-size matrix state. This changes training attention from O(&lt;em&gt;T&lt;/em&gt;2) to O(&lt;em&gt;T&lt;/em&gt;) and keeps inference state fixed at 29.5 MB per sequence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Consideration&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Transformer softmax attention&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Laplace 3B with GLA&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Training attention&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Builds pairwise token scores with O(&lt;em&gt;T&lt;/em&gt;2) scaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Updates chunked recurrent state with O(&lt;em&gt;T&lt;/em&gt;) scaling&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Inference memory&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;KV cache grows with context&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Recurrent state remains fixed in size&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Longer contexts&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Each sequence consumes more accelerator memory&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Context length does not increase recurrent-state allocation&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Implementation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Uses widely optimized attention kernels&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Requires architecture-specific kernels and compiler tuning&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Linear attention compresses history rather than retaining every key and value. You must therefore evaluate retrieval quality for your workloads. The fixed state can benefit long-running agents, large-document analysis, and applications that need persistent memory with predictable serving requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-i14-adapted-laplace-3b-for-aws-trainium2"&gt;How i14 adapted Laplace 3B for AWS Trainium2&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The original implementation of Laplace 3B used GPU-optimized Triton kernels. The AWS Generative AI Innovation Center (GenAIIC) team first rewrote the GLA kernel with standard PyTorch operators for Trainium2. After comparing both approaches, the team chose the beta version of &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/frameworks/torch/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;TorchNeuron Native, the native PyTorch backend for AWS Trainium&lt;/a&gt;, because it performed better.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The implementation required three changes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Using native PyTorch for the GLA attention kernel.&lt;/strong&gt; The team replaced the GPU-specific fused_chunk_simple_gla Triton kernel with standard PyTorch operators, using 16 chunks of 128 tokens to match the 128 partitions of the NeuronCore on-chip buffer.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Redesigning how Muon communicates.&lt;/strong&gt; Two all_to_all collectives in MuonFSDP2 cut per-rank traffic by 24 times and raised throughput by 1.63 times.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compiling the sequential backward pass.&lt;/strong&gt; The team statically unrolled the 16 chunk steps of the GLA recurrence to fit the graph-size and on-chip memory limits of the compiler.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The engineering challenges section describes each change in detail.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-aws-trainium-accelerators"&gt;Why AWS Trainium accelerators?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/trainium/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Trainium&lt;/a&gt; is a family of AWS machine learning (ML) accelerators designed for model training and inference. A trn2.48xlarge instance combines 16 Trainium2 chips, 64 logical NeuronCores, high-bandwidth memory, and NeuronLink-v3 connectivity. These resources support the distributed training and inference configurations described in this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three properties of Trainium2 shaped the implementation: software-managed on-chip memory (Challenge 1), a fast chip-to-chip interconnect (Challenge 2), and a static compiler (Challenge 3). The compiler traces the GLA recurrence as a fixed graph.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Software-managed on-chip memory.&lt;/strong&gt; Each NeuronCore has a 28 MiB State Buffer (SBUF) with 128 partitions and a 2 MiB partial-sum buffer (PSUM). The PSUM is wired to a 128×128 systolic Tensor Engine, alongside separate vector and scalar units. The SBUF is not a cache: the compiler schedules every &lt;code&gt;HBM-to-SBUF&lt;/code&gt; transfer ahead of time, so the data is in place with no evictions or misses (Figure 1).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-1.png" alt="NeuronCore-v3 internal architecture" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: NeuronCore-v3 internal architecture. Software-managed SBUF (28 MiB, 128 partitions), Tensor Engine (128×128 systolic array), Vector Engine, Scalar Engine, and GPSIMD cores.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="i14-and-the-laplace-3b-architecture"&gt;i14 and the Laplace 3B architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Laplace 3B is the 24-layer i14 decoder, where every layer replaces softmax attention with Simple GLA. Figure 2 follows one layer from the input &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;x&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt; at the top to the output &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;x&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt; + 1&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt; at the bottom. This section explains each module: normalization and convolution, the QKVG projection, the GLA mixer, and the feed-forward channel mixer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Normalization and convolution.&lt;/strong&gt; Each token’s residual-stream vector &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;x&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt; ∈ ℝ&lt;sup&gt;&lt;em&gt;D&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt; (&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;D&lt;/em&gt; = 3, 072&lt;/span&gt;) passes through a LayerNorm and a depth-wise causal Conv1d of width &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;k&lt;/em&gt; = 4&lt;/span&gt; with SiLU. This gives every position a 4-gram summary before the mixer. The GLA mixer then does content-based retrieval over the whole history through its decaying state.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;QKVG projection.&lt;/strong&gt; The post-conv output goes through one fused matrix &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;W&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;q&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;k&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;v&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;g&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt; ∈ ℝ&lt;sup&gt;9, 216 × 3, 072&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt;, where &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;9, 216 = &lt;em&gt;H&lt;/em&gt; × (2&lt;em&gt;d&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;k&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt; + 2&lt;em&gt;d&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;v&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;)&lt;/span&gt;. It splits into four per-token streams: queries &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;q&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt;, keys &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;k&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt;, values &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;v&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt;, and a gate &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;g&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt; (&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;H&lt;/em&gt; = 16&lt;/span&gt;, &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;d&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;k&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt; = 96&lt;/span&gt;, &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;d&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;v&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt; = 192&lt;/span&gt;). The gate is used after the mixer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 510px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-2.png" alt="Laplace decoder layer architecture" width="500"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Laplace decoder layer architecture. Green = fixed (no params), gray = learned, yellow = recurrent state, blue = projection.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GLA mixer.&lt;/strong&gt; Each head &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;h&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/span&gt; keeps a matrix state &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;S&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;sup&gt;(&lt;em&gt;h&lt;/em&gt;)&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt; of size &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;d&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;k&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt; × &lt;em&gt;d&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;v&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt; (96 × 192), a compressed, decaying record of past key-value pairs. At each token, it updates as:&lt;br&gt; &lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 250px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/01-gla-recurrent-state-update.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/01-gla-recurrent-state-update.png" alt="GLA recurrent-state update equation"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;GLA recurrent-state update equation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;br&gt; Here 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;λ&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;h&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt; is the fixed per-head decay, and 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;k&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;strong&gt;v&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;sup&gt;⊤&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt; is the rank-1 outer product that writes the current key-value pair into the state. The readout queries the state:
&lt;br&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 195px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/02-gla-state-readout.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/02-gla-state-readout.png" alt="GLA state-readout equation"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;GLA state-readout equation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;br&gt; Next, a self-augmentation step adds a gated copy of the value to each head’s output:
&lt;br&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 215px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/03-gla-self-augmentation.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/03-gla-self-augmentation.png" alt="GLA self-augmentation equation"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;GLA self-augmentation equation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;br&gt; Here 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;σ&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/span&gt; is the sigmoid and 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;⊙&lt;/span&gt; is the element-wise product. The gate is the query dotted with a learned per-channel rescaling of the key, where 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;a&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/span&gt; is the learned scale (aug_balance). At initialization, 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;a&lt;/strong&gt; = 0&lt;/span&gt;, which gives a half-strength skip of the value. The per-head outputs pass through a Head RMSNorm within each decay tier, are concatenated, gated by 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;S&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;i&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;L&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;U&lt;/em&gt;(&lt;strong&gt;g&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;)&lt;/span&gt;, and projected by 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;W&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;o&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;u&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;t&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt; ∈ ℝ&lt;sup&gt;&lt;em&gt;D&lt;/em&gt; × &lt;em&gt;D&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt; back to the residual stream.
&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Decay schedule.&lt;/strong&gt; The per-head decay rate &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;λ&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;h&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt; controls how fast a head forgets past tokens. It is fixed at initialization (not learned) and set by a geometric schedule. The schedule gives the 16 heads a spread of memory lengths and defines each head’s log-decay rate &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;γ&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;h&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt;:&lt;br&gt; &lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 210px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/04-gla-decay-schedule.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/04-gla-decay-schedule.png" alt="GLA decay schedule equations" width="696"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;GLA decay schedule equations&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;br&gt; 
&lt;strong&gt;Decay schedule’s reach.&lt;/strong&gt; For this model, 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;T&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;min&lt;/sub&gt; = 1&lt;/span&gt; and 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;T&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;max&lt;/sub&gt; = 2, 048&lt;/span&gt;. Head 0 therefore has 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;λ&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;0&lt;/sub&gt; = 0.5&lt;/span&gt; (half-life of one token), and head 15 has 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;λ&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;15&lt;/sub&gt; ≈ 0.9997&lt;/span&gt; (half-life of 2,048 tokens). Heads are grouped into tiers, each sharing a Head RMSNorm.
&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Channel mixer (FFN).&lt;/strong&gt; After the attention block and its residual, a second LayerNorm feeds a GLU (SiLU) FFN with inner dimension 8,192. Its three matrices are two up-projections &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;W&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;1&lt;/sub&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;W&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;2&lt;/sub&gt; ∈ ℝ&lt;sup&gt;8, 192 × &lt;em&gt;D&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt; and one down-projection &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;W&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sub&gt;3&lt;/sub&gt; ∈ ℝ&lt;sup&gt;&lt;em&gt;D&lt;/em&gt; × 8, 192&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt;. A second residual follows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The state &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;S&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sup&gt;(&lt;em&gt;h&lt;/em&gt;)&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt; is fixed at &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;d&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;k&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt; × &lt;em&gt;d&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;v&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt; = 96 × 192&lt;/span&gt; per head regardless of sequence length. As a result, inference is &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;O&lt;/em&gt;(1)&lt;/span&gt; per token, and training is &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;O&lt;/em&gt;(&lt;em&gt;T&lt;/em&gt;)&lt;/span&gt; rather than &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;O&lt;/em&gt;(&lt;em&gt;T&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sup&gt;2&lt;/sup&gt;)&lt;/span&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Parameter&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Parameters&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Heads&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

&lt;h2 id="engineering-challenges"&gt;Engineering challenges&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each of the three changes raised its own engineering challenge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="challenge-1-using-native-pytorch-on-trainium-for-gla"&gt;Challenge 1: Using native PyTorch on Trainium for GLA&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On GPU, GLA runs as the fused Triton kernel fused_chunk_simple_gla from the flash-linear-attention library. The GenAIIC team re-expressed it with standard PyTorch operators running through &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/frameworks/torch/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;TorchNeuron Native&lt;/a&gt;, as explicit loops over fixed-size chunks. The chunk count is fixed at compile time and sets the backward-unroll depth. The team uses fla_num_chunks = 16, so each chunk holds &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;C&lt;/em&gt; = 128&lt;/span&gt; tokens.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Within each chunk &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;c&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/span&gt;, the intra-chunk output is masked linear attention, and the running state &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;S&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/span&gt; adds earlier context:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 215px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/05-chunked-gla-intra-chunk-output.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/05-chunked-gla-intra-chunk-output.png" alt="Chunked GLA intra-chunk output" width="508"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Chunked GLA intra-chunk output&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The state then advances to the next chunk:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 175px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/06-chunked-gla-state-update.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/06-chunked-gla-state-update.png" alt="Chunked GLA state update"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Chunked GLA state update&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;λ&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;em&gt;C&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt; is the per-head decay compounded over the chunk. The 16 intra-chunk computations run in parallel, and only the state update is sequential (Figure 3).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-7.png" alt="GLA chunked recurrence decomposition" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: GLA chunked recurrence with parallel intra-chunk attention and a sequential state chain.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the SBUF has 128 partitions, each 128-token chunk maps one token per partition, with no padding or cross-partition movement. This alignment increased measured throughput by 14 percent (Figure 4).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-8.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-8.png" alt="GLA chunk alignment with SBUF partitions" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Chunk size C = 128 maps one-to-one onto the 128 SBUF partitions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="challenge-2-distributed-muon-orthogonalization-under-fsdp2"&gt;Challenge 2: Distributed Muon orthogonalization under FSDP2&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Muon orthogonalizes each gradient before the update. For each of the 96 two-dimensional weight matrices (2.718 billion parameters), it runs five Newton–Schulz iterations (Algorithm 1).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 628px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/07-newton-schulz-algorithm.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/07-newton-schulz-algorithm.png" alt="The Newton-Schulz algorithm"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;The Newton-Schulz algorithm&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The distributed problem.&lt;/strong&gt; The Gram matrix &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A&lt;/strong&gt; = &lt;strong&gt;X&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;strong&gt;X&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sup&gt;⊤&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt; correlates every row of the weight with every other row. Under Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP2), each of the 64 ranks holds only 1/64 of the rows. A shard-local &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;X&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;strong&gt;X&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;sup&gt;⊤&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt; therefore yields only a diagonal block and drops every cross-shard term (Figure 5).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-9.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-9.png" alt="Newton-Schulz communication patterns under FSDP2" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Newton–Schulz under FSDP2: the shard-local Gram block, and gather/broadcast compared with all_to_all.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Naive approach: Gather and broadcast.&lt;/strong&gt; Every rank gathers the full gradient (5.44 GB), one rank runs Newton–Schulz, and it broadcasts the result. This moves 10.87 GB per step while the other 63 ranks wait.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Production approach: all_to_all transposition.&lt;/strong&gt; In MuonFSDP2, one all_to_all gives each rank a few complete matrices, all 64 ranks run Newton–Schulz in parallel, and a second all_to_all returns the results. Per-rank traffic drops to 0.45 GB, 24 times less. Throughput rises from 73,378 to 119,934 tokens per second, 1.63 times higher, with a bit-identical loss curve (Figure 6).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-10.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-10.png" alt="Communication volume per training step" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Per-step communication volume: 10.87 GB with all_gather compared with 0.45 GB with all_to_all.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Communication cost.&lt;/strong&gt; Newton–Schulz is only about 2 percent of the FLOPs in a training step (&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;1.95 × 10&lt;sup&gt;14&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt; of &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;9.53 × 10&lt;sup&gt;15&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/span&gt;), so communication was the bottleneck. Over NeuronLink-v3 at 1.28 TB/s, the 0.45 GB exchange takes under 0.4 ms of the roughly 4.4-second step.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="challenge-3-backward-pass-unrolling-for-a-sequential-recurrence"&gt;Challenge 3: Backward-pass unrolling for a sequential recurrence&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The backward pass differentiates through all 16 sequential state updates. On GPU, autograd builds this graph at runtime. On Trainium2, the compiler must see the whole unrolled 16-step graph in advance, which creates three constraints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Graph depth grows with chunk count: at 32 chunks (&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;C&lt;/em&gt; = 64&lt;/span&gt;), the graph overflowed the scheduler. Per-chunk memory grows with chunk size: at 8 chunks (&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;C&lt;/em&gt; = 256&lt;/span&gt;), intermediates spilled into HBM. Every shape is fixed at trace time, so each loop intermediate needs its own static allocation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Sixteen chunks (&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;C&lt;/em&gt; = 128&lt;/span&gt;) satisfy all three: the unroll fits the scheduler, intermediates stay on chip, and tiles align with the 128 SBUF partitions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="training-results"&gt;Training results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The GenAIIC team trained Laplace 3B on one trn2.48xlarge instance using all 64 logical NeuronCores and the all_to_all Muon path. The full recipe is 190,735 steps (100 billion FineWeb-Edu tokens). The following results are through step 84,210, about 44 billion tokens.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput.&lt;/strong&gt; After compilation warmup, throughput settles at roughly 120,000 tokens per second and holds there through step 84,210.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Loss and gradient stability.&lt;/strong&gt; The loss (Figure 7a) drops smoothly from about 11.4 to 2.3729 at step 84,210. The gradient norm (Figure 7b) shows the expected early spike then a steady decline to about 0.05, with no post-warmup spikes, divergence, or NaN/Inf values.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-11.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-11.png" alt="Laplace 3B training loss and gradient norm" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Training dynamics. (a) Loss 11.4 → 2.3729. (b) Gradient norm from TensorBoard data.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="inference-optimization-on-trainium2"&gt;Inference optimization on Trainium2&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Through an unmodified vLLM engine, the GenAIIC team served 11,544 output tokens per second at 1,280 concurrent sequences on one trn2.48xlarge instance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Constant serving state.&lt;/strong&gt; The 29.5 MB recurrent state per sequence is roughly 82 times smaller than a comparable KV cache at 8,192 tokens. Increasing the prompt from 2,048 to 8,192 tokens added only 0.5% to the cost per output token.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Unmodified vLLM.&lt;/strong&gt; A wrapper replaces one attribute per decoder layer, the token mixer, to read and write the recurrent state in the vLLM pool. Because the model declares itself attention-free, vLLM allocates one fixed page per sequence. Outputs matched the i14 reference token for token (48/48 steps on 3/3 prompts in BF16).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Finding the bottleneck.&lt;/strong&gt; A linear fit of decode time against the number of decoder layers &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;L&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/span&gt; gives:&lt;br&gt; &lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 240px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/08-decode-time-linear-fit.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/08-decode-time-linear-fit.png" alt="Decode-time linear fit equation" width="762"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
 &lt;figcaption aria-hidden="true"&gt;
  Decode-time linear fit equation
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
 &lt;/figcaption&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;br&gt; At 
&lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;L&lt;/em&gt; = 24&lt;/span&gt;, that is 168.1 ms per step, 96.9 percent of it inside the decoder layers. The large projections cost 13.27 ms, near the 12 ms HBM-streaming floor. Most time went to small operators dispatched one at a time under eager PyTorch.
&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scaling to a full instance.&lt;/strong&gt; Without tensor parallelism, throughput scales through replicas, with one engine and 20 sequences per logical NeuronCore. Eager throughput grew from 133 output tokens per second on one replica to 6,772 on 64, where host CPU usage reached 82 percent. Compiling the decode step removed per-operator dispatch and raised throughput 1.7 times, to 11,544 (Figure 8).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-13.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21817-13.png" alt="Whole-instance inference throughput" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: Whole-instance inference throughput. (a) Replica scaling on eager and compiled decode. (b) Compiled decode lifts throughput from 6,772 to 11,544 output tokens per second.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What fixed-batch benchmarks hide.&lt;/strong&gt; Under real traffic, each new decode batch width is a separately compiled shape whose first use costs about 11 seconds. These steps were only 1.1 percent of the total but consumed 47.5 percent of decode time. Padding to fixed widths removed this cost with bit-exact results. Compiled decode then improved per-replica throughput 3.52 times under staggered arrivals, compared with 1.46 times on fixed batches. Because BF16 reassociation makes compiled output differ slightly from eager, use eager decode when exact reproducibility matters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="key-takeaways"&gt;Key takeaways&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The project produced lessons across both training and inference:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Native PyTorch can support custom architectures on Trainium2.&lt;/strong&gt; i14 retained the GLA architecture and used TorchNeuron Native for the final implementation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Communication design matters for custom optimizers.&lt;/strong&gt; The Muon all_to_all design reduced per-rank traffic by 24 times and increased training throughput by 1.63 times.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fixed state keeps inference memory predictable.&lt;/strong&gt; Each sequence uses 29.5 MB regardless of context length.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Profile before compiling.&lt;/strong&gt; Operator dispatch, rather than arithmetic or HBM bandwidth, accounted for most decode time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Benchmark realistic arrival patterns.&lt;/strong&gt; Fixed batches hid the cost of compiling new shapes for changing batch widths.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Laplace 3B shows that AWS Trainium2 can support model architectures beyond standard transformers. Using native PyTorch through TorchNeuron Native and the AWS Neuron SDK, i14 trained and served its GLA-based model while preserving a fixed recurrent state.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For agentic systems, this changes a basic serving constraint. Longer histories don’t require a larger per-sequence state, which can help teams retain more context while keeping memory use predictable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If growing context is increasing serving cost, reducing concurrency, or limiting agent memory, try &lt;a href="https://i14.ai" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;i14&lt;/a&gt; or contact your AWS account team to evaluate Laplace 3B on AWS Trainium2.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="resources"&gt;Resources&lt;/h2&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Work management context for AI agents with Smartsheet MCP on AWS</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/work-management-context-for-ai-agents-with-smartsheet-mcp-on-aws/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Galina Jordanowa]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 20 Oct 2026 17:59:07 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Kiro]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">47fdf366d1959d8569ea858c6045c42e45b756f5</guid>

					<description>The Smartsheet Model Context Protocol (MCP) Server lets AI agents read, update, and act on live work management data. This post shows two ways to connect it on AWS: Amazon Quick for business teams and Kiro for developers, so your agents can work with Smartsheet without switching tools.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;AI agents are most useful when they can act on the real work happening across your organization. Work management involves complex, cross-functional tasks such as updating project timelines, reassigning tasks, and surfacing status. AI agents can support you with these without anyone switching tools. &lt;a href="https://www.smartsheet.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Smartsheet&lt;/a&gt;, the work management platform for managing projects, portfolios, and operations, has launched a generally available Model Context Protocol (MCP) Server.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With &lt;a href="https://developers.smartsheet.com/ai-mcp/smartsheet/mcp-server" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;the Smartsheet MCP Server&lt;/a&gt;, AI assistants can read, write, and manage Smartsheet data through a standardized protocol. The protocol spans more than 40 tools for exploring, creating, and managing sheets, rows, columns, and automations. You can update task statuses, create rows, and modify project structure directly through natural language.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through two ways you can connect it to your existing workflows. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt; is an agentic AI workspace built for business users, project managers, and operations teams. They can ask questions, update sheets, and build dashboards through natural language without writing code. &lt;a href="https://kiro.dev/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kiro&lt;/a&gt; is an agentic IDE where developers connect to Smartsheet through MCP to reference project specs, update task status, and track dependencies without leaving their coding workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-overview"&gt;Architecture overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows how the Smartsheet MCP Server sits as the central integration layer connecting to AWS services. Amazon Quick and Kiro connect directly as MCP clients.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick serves knowledge workers, project managers, and operations teams who want to query, update, and automate Smartsheet through natural language. With Kiro, developers can reference project specs, update task status, and track dependencies without leaving their coding workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following architecture diagram illustrates these integration paths: the Smartsheet MCP Server in the center, with Amazon Quick and Kiro as MCP clients on the left, and Smartsheet’s API and data layer on the right.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram: the Smartsheet MCP Server connecting Amazon Quick and Kiro clients to the Smartsheet API and data layer" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Smartsheet MCP Server architecture showing MCP client connections from Amazon Quick and Kiro through the server to the Smartsheet API layer&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="sprint-management-with-amazon-quick-and-kiro"&gt;Sprint management with Amazon Quick and Kiro&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider a software engineering team that tracks their sprint in Smartsheet. Tasks, owners, statuses, and blockers all live in a project sheet. Here’s what each path looks like for the same team:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a user such as a project manager wants to check which sprint tasks are blocked this week, they can ask Amazon Quick through natural language. The Amazon Quick chat agent queries the Smartsheet MCP Server, pulls the relevant rows, and surfaces a summary with owner names and due dates. The response is generated from live Smartsheet data without building a report or dashboard.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For developers who are fixing bugs or working through features, they can ask Kiro to pull the current sprint sheet and show them the open backend tasks assigned to them. Kiro queries Smartsheet through MCP and surfaces the list inline. When the fix is committed, a Kiro hook automatically updates the corresponding row status to Complete.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Smartsheet MCP Server delivers precisely structured context, including row relationships, column types, cell history, and comments. This produces token-efficient payload sizes rather than returning raw API payloads. This engineering decision compounds into reduced cost and improved latency at scale when running hundreds of AI-assisted queries across a project portfolio. To learn how Smartsheet built their remote MCP server on AWS, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-smartsheet-built-a-remote-mcp-server-on-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How Smartsheet built a remote MCP server on AWS&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before connecting the Smartsheet MCP Server to Amazon Quick and Kiro, verify that you have the following in place:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An active AWS account with appropriate AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions for the service you’re connecting (Amazon Quick, or Kiro)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A Smartsheet account on a Business, Enterprise, or AWM plan.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A Smartsheet Personal Access Token, or OAuth credentials if your client supports the OAuth flow.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Amazon Quick: Author or higher permissions in your Amazon Quick account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Kiro: Kiro IDE installed from &lt;a href="https://kiro.dev" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;kiro.dev&lt;/a&gt; with &lt;code&gt;Node.js&lt;/code&gt; available in your environment.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Smartsheet MCP Server is available at three regional endpoints. It uses Streamable HTTP transport (MCP spec versions 2025-06-18 and 2025-03-26) and is authenticated with either OAuth 2.0 or a Personal Access Token.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Commercial US: https://mcp.smartsheet.com.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;EU: https://mcp.smartsheet.eu.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AU: https://mcp.smartsheet.au.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both options expose the same Smartsheet toolset. The right choice depends on where your work gets done:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choose Amazon Quick if you need a fully managed, organization-wide solution your whole team can use: no app registration, only pre-built OAuth, and it scales across knowledge workers in your enterprise.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choose Kiro if you’re a developer who wants something lightweight: no infrastructure, only a config file and a personal access token, scoped to your own workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="amazon-quick-integration"&gt;Amazon Quick integration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick includes Smartsheet as a pre-built connector in its directory. That means reading project status, updating rows, creating discussions, and surfacing risks, all in the same interface your business users already work in.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick makes that integration available across the organization, from project managers and operations teams to business users who manage work in Smartsheet.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To add the Quick integration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksuite/latest/userguide/mcp-integration.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick console&lt;/a&gt;, navigate to &lt;strong&gt;Connectors&lt;/strong&gt; and find Smartsheet in the directory.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-2.png" alt="Amazon Quick Connectors directory with the Smartsheet connector tile" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The Smartsheet connector in the Amazon Quick Connectors directory&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="2" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select the &lt;strong&gt;Smartsheet connector&lt;/strong&gt; tile.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter the following integration details: 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Name&lt;/strong&gt; – Descriptive name for your Smartsheet integration.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Keep the &lt;strong&gt;Connection type&lt;/strong&gt; and &lt;strong&gt;Auth configuration&lt;/strong&gt; settings as is.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Base URL&lt;/strong&gt; – &lt;a class="uri" href="https://mcp.smartsheet.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;https://mcp.smartsheet.com&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Client ID&lt;/strong&gt; – App client ID from your Smartsheet Developer Tools app registration.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Client Secret&lt;/strong&gt; – App secret from your Smartsheet Developer Tools app registration.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Token URL&lt;/strong&gt; – &lt;a class="uri" href="https://api.smartsheet.com/2.0/token" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;https://api.smartsheet.com/2.0/token&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Auth URL&lt;/strong&gt; – &lt;a class="uri" href="https://app.smartsheet.com/b/authorize" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;https://app.smartsheet.com/b/authorize&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-3.png" alt="Smartsheet connection details form in Amazon Quick with base URL, client ID, client secret, token URL, and auth URL fields" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Entering the Smartsheet connection details in Amazon Quick&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Next&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the &lt;strong&gt;Manage Tools &amp;amp; Permissions&lt;/strong&gt; page, you see a list of available tools. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Next&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-4.png" alt="Manage Tools and Permissions page listing the available Smartsheet tools in Amazon Quick" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: The Manage Tools and Permissions page for the Smartsheet connector&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="6" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the &lt;strong&gt;Publish&lt;/strong&gt; page, choose who can access this connection, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Publish&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-5.png" alt="Amazon Quick Publish page for choosing who can access the Smartsheet connection" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Choosing who can access the Smartsheet connection on the Publish page&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="7" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Next&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After you’re connected, your Amazon Quick chat agent can work with Smartsheet data through natural language:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;“What projects in our portfolio are currently At Risk?”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Update the status of the authentication task in the Backend API sheet to Complete.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Create a summary of all overdue items across the Q3 Roadmap and send it to the team.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-6.png" alt="Amazon Quick chat response summarizing overdue tasks from a Smartsheet project sheet with recommended actions" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Amazon Quick chat response summarizing overdue tasks from a Smartsheet project sheet with key observations and recommended actions&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="kiro-integration"&gt;Kiro integration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://kiro.dev/docs/mcp/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kiro&lt;/a&gt; is the right path when developers want Smartsheet project context available directly in their development environment, alongside their code, without context-switching.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Kiro is built around spec-driven development. It turns high-level prompts into structured specs, then into working code, docs, and tests. MCP servers are how Kiro gets real-world context by connecting to documentation, databases, APIs, and enterprise systems to ground its agent in live data rather than training-time knowledge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A Kiro feature called powers is a one-click installable bundle that packages a curated MCP server with steering files and hooks. It gives the Kiro coding agent a new capability out of the box. For Smartsheet, configuring the connection takes a single JSON configuration block and from that point, the Kiro agent can reference live project data as part of a development workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To connect the Smartsheet MCP Server to Kiro, add it to your Kiro MCP configuration (&lt;code&gt;.kiro/settings/mcp.json&lt;/code&gt; for workspace scope). For a full walkthrough, see &lt;a href="https://repost.aws/articles/ARuX8rkojgSx-TYCc65JyAOw/getting-started-with-kiro-and-mcp-servers-connect-your-ai-ide-to-real-world-tools" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting Started with Kiro and MCP Servers&lt;/a&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "mcpServers": {
    "smartsheet-us": {
      "type": "http",
      "url": "https://mcp.smartsheet.com",
      "headers": {
        "Authorization": "Bearer &amp;lt;SMARTSHEET_API_TOKEN&amp;gt;"
      }
    }
  }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This configuration uses the Personal Access Token (API key) approach. For production deployments, OAuth is recommended.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set &lt;code&gt;SMARTSHEET_API_TOKEN&lt;/code&gt; to your Smartsheet Personal Access Token in your environment. Kiro loads MCP servers as background processes on startup. After it’s initialized, Smartsheet tools are available to the Kiro agent across all modes: chat, spec planning, and hooks-based automation. Full details in the &lt;a href="https://developers.smartsheet.com/api/smartsheet/ai-integration/mcp-server" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Smartsheet MCP Server Documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From there, Kiro can reference Smartsheet data as part of development workflows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Check the Backend API project sheet for any open bugs and generate a prioritized fix list.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-7.png" alt="Kiro generating a prioritized fix list from open bugs in the Backend API Smartsheet project sheet" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Kiro generating a prioritized fix list from open bugs in the Backend API project sheet&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Pull the Q3 Roadmap sheet and create a spec for the features marked In Progress.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-8.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21001-8.png" alt="Kiro creating a spec from the in-progress features in the Q3 Roadmap Smartsheet sheet" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: Kiro creating a spec from the in-progress features in the Q3 Roadmap sheet&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="enterprise-governance"&gt;Enterprise governance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For builders deploying in regulated or enterprise environments, the Smartsheet MCP Server security model is built on the same infrastructure and permissions layer as the Smartsheet REST API. It’s not a new trust boundary:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;OAuth 2.0 and Personal Access Token authentication&lt;/strong&gt;: choose the auth method supported by your MCP client.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;User-level permission enforcement&lt;/strong&gt;: agents can only access data the authenticated user has permission to see. No privilege escalation through natural language prompts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Organization-level AI controls&lt;/strong&gt;: system administrators can turn on or off AI features globally. Write-tool access is configurable by user group.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Streamable HTTP transport&lt;/strong&gt;: firewall allowlisting is done by fully qualified domain name (FQDN) (mcp.smartsheet.com), not IP address.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For compliance architects, the MCP Server doesn’t store prompts, and prompt content stays between the AI client and the model. Smartsheet sees only the structured tool calls and their results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you followed this walkthrough and want to remove the integration afterward, here’s how to undo each path:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-quick"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Amazon Quick console, navigate to &lt;strong&gt;Connectors&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select your Smartsheet connector.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Delete&lt;/strong&gt; to remove the connection. This revokes the OAuth token and stops agent access to Smartsheet data.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="kiro"&gt;Kiro&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open your MCP configuration file (&lt;code&gt;.kiro/settings/mcp.json&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove the &lt;code&gt;smartsheet-us&lt;/code&gt; entry from the &lt;code&gt;mcpServers&lt;/code&gt; object.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Restart Kiro to unload the server.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="next-steps"&gt;Next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For background on how the server works, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-smartsheet-built-a-remote-mcp-server-on-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How Smartsheet built a remote MCP server on AWS&lt;/a&gt;. After you’ve connected the Smartsheet MCP Server to your system of choice, here are four actions we recommend trying:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can ask the chat agent to monitor your active project sheets, identify rows marked Blocked or At Risk. You can also ask to annotate projects with a discussion comment, and deliver a structured summary to your team through your preferred channel. This would be an example workflow that chains &lt;code&gt;get_sheet_summary&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;search&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;create_discussion_on_row&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent can also retrieve data through the Smartsheet MCP Server to reconcile task statuses between your issue tracker and your Smartsheet project plan. It can flag drift between the two, and update the affected rows, using &lt;code&gt;get_sheet_summary&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;get_columns&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;update_rows&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;create_discussion_on_row&lt;/code&gt; in sequence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also try to aggregate project health signals across your reports and dashboards. You can use it to produce a structured briefing without manual data gathering, drawing on &lt;code&gt;list_reports&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;get_report&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;list_dashboards&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;get_dashboard&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;search&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Lastly, you can use the agent to follow every write with a discussion comment explaining what changed and why. This creates a human-readable audit trail inside Smartsheet through the combination of &lt;code&gt;update_rows&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;create_discussion_on_row&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All four patterns work across the preceding paths: Amazon Quick for business-user-facing workflows, and Kiro for developer-initiated actions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Building something with Smartsheet MCP and AWS? Share what you’re working on in the comments, or connect with us in the &lt;a href="https://community.smartsheet.com/categories/api-developers" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Smartsheet Developer Community&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>From data to decisions: Conversational supply chain intelligence with Amazon Quick and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/from-data-to-decisions-conversational-supply-chain-intelligence-with-amazon-quick-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Vineet Kachhawaha]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 20 Oct 2026 17:52:50 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">4732e547b3ba87197c5a5faf5d2f273ac726daf1</guid>

					<description>Enterprise supply chains are data-rich but insight-poor, with the data to decide scattered across ERP, monitoring, procurement, and email. This post shows how Amazon Bedrock AgentCore exposes those systems as a governed tool layer, and how Amazon Quick turns them into one conversational experience for business users, with a deploy-it-yourself walkthrough.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Giving supply chain teams direct access to the systems behind their decisions.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every supply chain generates constant signals: orders in approval, shifting lead times, slowing ports, drifting prices, disruptions. The problem isn’t scarce data. It’s the distance between the data and the decision-maker.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Answering “which top-revenue SKUs are exposed to the current port closure, and what will it cost?” used to mean knowing which system held each piece and stitching them together by hand while the domain expert waited.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Could that manager instead ask in plain language and get an actionable, cross-system answer in seconds? Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is the intelligence layer, exposing enterprise Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers as a composable tool layer. Amazon Quick turns that into action for personas with no setup. Answers come from live systems of record and grounded documents, not model recall, and every change waits for a named human. In this post, we walk through this pattern end to end, from a conversational walkthrough in Amazon Quick to the AgentCore tool layer behind it, and we show you how to deploy it yourself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-problem-data-rich-insight-poor"&gt;The problem: Data-rich, insight-poor&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-1-1.png" alt="Diagram showing domain expertise with business users while operational data is split across ERP, monitoring, procurement, and email systems" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Domain expertise sits with users while operational data is fragmented across ERP, monitoring, procurement, and email&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each question needs its own tool: inventory from the enterprise resource planning (ERP) system, disruptions from email or news, a request for quotation (RFQ) from procurement, and compliance from a checklist. Correlating is manual.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="a-day-in-the-life"&gt;A day in the life&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A manager’s morning traditionally spans the ERP (inventory exceptions), monitoring tool (alerts), procurement (alternatives), email (RFQ), and a tracker: 45–60 minutes across five systems with a dozen-plus context switches.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Conversationally it’s three prompts in one tool, “Any critical disruptions affecting my top-revenue SKUs?” → “Assess the TechComp halt and issue an emergency RFQ to approved alternatives” → “Looks good. Send it.”, in minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-2-1.png" alt="Comparison of a traditional multi-tool workflow against a single conversational thread orchestrated by AgentCore" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Traditional multi-tool flow versus a single conversational thread orchestrated by AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deep-dive-supply-chain-scenario-walkthrough"&gt;Deep dive: Supply chain scenario walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following five steps follow one disruption from detection to an approved emergency RFQ, all in a single Amazon Quick conversation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-discovery---what-tools-do-i-have"&gt;Step 1: Discovery – “What tools do I have?”&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The user asks “What supply chain tools do I have?” and Amazon Quick lists the supply chain MCP servers published in AWS Agent Registry. Because an administrator has turned on the registry integration, the four business MCP servers already appear as connector cards, with connection details pre-populated and no URLs to enter by hand. The orchestration agent is added as its own MCP connector.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-querying---show-me-active-disruptions"&gt;Step 2: Querying – “Show me active disruptions”&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;“Check active supply disruptions” calls the Disruption Alerts MCP (&lt;code&gt;assess_disruption_impact&lt;/code&gt;): three disruptions with severity, region, and SKU counts. &lt;em&gt;How we solve it: Quick routes the request through AgentCore to the MCP server, which queries backend monitoring and returns a structured result, no dashboard or export.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-drill-down---assess-the-critical-one"&gt;Step 3: Drill-down – “Assess the critical one”&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The user opens the TechComp Production Halt. &lt;code&gt;assess_disruption_impact(disruption_id="DISR-002")&lt;/code&gt; correlates order, customer, and inventory data: $750K at risk, 23 orders, three days of supply. &lt;em&gt;How we solve it: one MCP tool call fans out across systems of record, so the manager gets a consolidated impact read without touching the ERP.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-action---get-mitigation-recommendations"&gt;Step 4: Action – “Get mitigation recommendations”&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The user asks for mitigation. &lt;code&gt;recommend_mitigation(disruption_id="DISR-002")&lt;/code&gt; chains the Governance MCP (verify approved alternatives) and Quoting MCP (contract-aligned quote), one flow from one request. &lt;em&gt;How we solve it: the agent plans and sequences the calls, so a single ask spans disruption, governance, and quoting with no manual hand-offs.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-automated-follow-up"&gt;Step 5: Automated follow-up&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The user then sets up proactive monitoring (“Alert me every morning if there’s a new critical disruption”). The same flow can run unattended from an inbound supplier email. &lt;em&gt;How we solve it: the same agent and tools run on a schedule or email trigger, posting review-ready cards to the Activity Feed for a person to approve.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Put end to end, those five steps are a single runtime sequence:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/22042-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/22042-1.png" alt="Runtime sequence for the walkthrough in which every result returns to Amazon Quick for human approval" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Runtime sequence for the walkthrough, where every result returns to Amazon Quick for approval&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-architecture"&gt;Solution architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That’s the experience. Here’s how it is built: the pattern separates the intelligence layer from the interface layer, so each evolves independently, with Amazon Quick as the interface and AgentCore as the intelligence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-4-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-4-1.png" alt="End-to-end architecture connecting Amazon Quick to AgentCore gateways, runtime, identity, and policy, with AWS Agent Registry alongside" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: End-to-end architecture from Amazon Quick to AgentCore (Gateways, Runtime, Identity, Policy) to backends, with AWS Agent Registry alongside&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="amazon-bedrock-agentcore-the-intelligence-layer"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore: The intelligence layer&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is a modular agentic platform for building, deploying, and operating agents securely at scale. It offers more than we use here. Four of its services are relevant to this pattern:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Runtime – Serverless hosting for MCP servers and agents, with session isolation and built-in identity.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Gateway – Turns APIs, AWS Lambda functions, and services into MCP-compatible tools behind a secure managed endpoint, standardizing tool access. Reasoning is the calling agent’s.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Identity – Authentication and credential management, compatible with existing identity providers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Policy – Deterministic authorization rules, authored in natural language or Cedar, that intercept every tool call before execution, keeping an agent in its lane on procurement and financial data.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-mcp-protocol-how-tools-communicate"&gt;The MCP protocol: How tools communicate&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;MCP is an open standard for how AI models interact with external tools. Each server exposes tools with typed parameters:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "server_name": "sc-disruption-alert-gateway",
  "tools": [{
    "name": "assess_disruption_impact",
    "parameters": {
      "disruption_id": {"type": "string"},
      "context": {"type": "object"} // order, inventory, revenue data
    }
  }] // plus classify_severity, recommend_mitigation, get_escalation_chain
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This means:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AI clients (Quick, custom agents, other MCP apps) can consume the same servers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Backends (SAP, Oracle, custom microservices) can be wrapped as an MCP server.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Tool discovery is automatic. Clients query the registry for schemas at runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="aws-agent-registry-the-discovery-layer"&gt;AWS Agent Registry: The discovery layer&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The registry is the governed discovery layer: a publisher submits a record, a curator approves it, and only then is it discoverable through hybrid search, browsing, or the registry’s MCP endpoint. One scale reality worth noting: because schemas are discovered at runtime, treat each as a contract, versioning new alongside old for breaking changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-5-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-5-1.png" alt="Flow of publishing an MCP server record to AWS Agent Registry, curator approval, admin linking to Quick, and records becoming connector cards" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Publishing an MCP server record to AWS Agent Registry, a curator approving it, an administrator linking it to Quick, and approved records becoming connector cards&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="multi-step-orchestration"&gt;Multi-step orchestration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reasoning layer is an agent on AgentCore Runtime, an agent record at its direct invocation URL through a Custom MCP connector, with no Lambda or gateway. Here, it’s the Order Fulfillment Agent. Given one request it plans which tools to call and in what order:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;User: "A supplier just went down. Find alternatives, verify compliance, issue an emergency RFQ."
Disruption Alerts MCP -&amp;gt; identify the disruption
Governance MCP -&amp;gt; approved alternatives + compliance + budget authority
Quoting MCP -&amp;gt; contract-aligned quote
-&amp;gt; one consolidated recommendation&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="security-and-governance-architecture"&gt;Security and governance architecture&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every tool call passes through the same AgentCore control set before it reaches a backend.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-6-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-6-1.png" alt="The uniform AgentCore control set applied before the backend, including IAM auth, per-tool authorization, policy evaluation, and audit logging" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: The uniform AgentCore control set before the backend (IAM auth, per-tool authorization, input validation, policy evaluation, rate limiting, data-boundary enforcement, audit logging)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-mcp-servers-in-this-pattern"&gt;The MCP servers in this pattern&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MCP server&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Capabilities&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Example queries&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Quoting Engine&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;code&gt;sc-quoting-gateway&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Quote generation, pricing rules, validation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“Contract-aligned quote for 500 units of SKU-A”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Governance Engine&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;code&gt;sc-governance-gateway&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Supplier approval, budget authority, regulatory compliance, policy rules&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“Is SupplierCo on our approved vendor list?”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Invoice Processing&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;code&gt;sc-invoice-gateway&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Invoice approval routing, AP aging, payment schedules&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“What invoices are pending approval this week?”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Disruption Alerts&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;code&gt;sc-disruption-alert-gateway&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Active disruptions, severity, impact assessments, mitigation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“Critical disruptions affecting our APAC suppliers?”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Order Fulfillment Agent&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;code&gt;sc-order-fulfillment-agent&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Multi-step orchestration across all four business MCPs (AgentCore Runtime, direct invocation URL)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“A supplier went down, find alternatives, check compliance, issue an RFQ”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="amazon-quick-the-enterprise-ai-assistant"&gt;Amazon Quick: The enterprise AI assistant&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick puts AgentCore tools before business users through chat, agents, the Activity Feed, and automated workflows, with no technical setup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="key-components-used"&gt;Key components used&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Role in this pattern&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;How it works&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent Registry connection&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Surfaces MCP servers as connectors&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Quick Agent&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt;
