 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="automated-activity-feed-and-proactive-monitoring"&gt;Automated Activity Feed and proactive monitoring&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The&lt;em&gt; Activity Feed&lt;/em&gt; is the proactive surface of Amazon Quick, a unified inbox where agents post results, alerts, and recommendations. Feed items are:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Actionable, each carries context and next-step actions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AI-triaged, with priority indicators (Reply needed, Follow up, Deadline).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Cross-source, spanning email, schedules, agent runs, and external systems.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Human-in-the-loop, where agents propose and humans decide.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-automated-monitoring-works"&gt;How automated monitoring works&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore tools plus the proactive capabilities of Amazon Quick give two paths, time-based and email-driven, through a common trigger → agent → output pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-7-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-7-1.png" alt="Three-stage pipeline from a schedule or email trigger to an autonomous agent to a review-ready card" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Three-stage pipeline from trigger (schedule or email) to autonomous agent (assess_disruption_impact, check_supplier_approval, apply_approval_rules, validate_quote) to review-ready card&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="use-case-validate-supplier-responses-from-email"&gt;Use case: Validate supplier responses from email&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Suppliers reply to an emergency RFQ by email with pricing. The agent validates each (approved supplier? within guidelines?) and stages the result. It does not author a quote, pick a winner, or issue a purchase order (PO).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 1: Setup (one-time, approximately 5 minutes)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Connect Outlook&lt;/strong&gt;: in Amazon Quick, choose &lt;strong&gt;Settings&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Connections&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Add Outlook&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Configure Activity Feed&lt;/strong&gt;: turn on email monitoring in feed preferences.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create SC Agent&lt;/strong&gt;: build an agent with these instructions:&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;Supply chain procurement assistant. On a supplier RFQ reply with pricing: 1) extract SKU(s), quantities, pricing; 2) validate approved vendor (Governance MCP); 3) check pricing within guidelines (Quoting MCP); 4) post a review-ready card (approved-supplier, within-guidelines); do NOT draft a PO or select a supplier.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create Schedule&lt;/strong&gt;: “Every 2 hours during business hours, check for new supplier replies and validate them.”&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 2: Runtime (fully automated)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;Schedule -&amp;gt; email from "TechSupply Co" (MCU-7200, 10,000 units @ $2.45/unit) -&amp;gt; Governance MCP: approved? | Quoting MCP: within guidelines? -&amp;gt; "Supplier Response - Ready for Review" card&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The feed card carries the validation results and decision-relevant facts:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Supplier Response: Ready for Review&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Details&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Supplier&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TechSupply Co, &lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/2705.png" alt="✅" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt; approved vendor&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;SKU / Qty / Price&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MCU-7200 · 10,000 units · $2.45/unit&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Price check&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/2705.png" alt="✅" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt; within purchasing guidelines&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Actions&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Select for PO · Compare responses · Reject&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 3: Human-in-the-loop&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reviewer compares validated responses and either selects a supplier and issues the PO, or rejects with a logged reason. Selection and the PO stay with a person.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="use-case-proactive-disruption-monitoring"&gt;Use case: Proactive disruption monitoring&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Schedule-triggered, the same pattern applies: a daily 8:30 AM agent checks for new critical or high-severity disruptions, assesses impact, flags anything under seven days of supply as URGENT, and posts an alert card with an Issue Emergency RFQ action.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="where-this-fits-alongside-traditional-bi"&gt;Where this fits alongside traditional BI&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dimension&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Traditional business intelligence (BI) / dashboards&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore + Amazon Quick&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Access model&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Navigate, log in, find the dashboard/report&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Ask in natural language. The system finds the source&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Time to insight&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Minutes to hours (find → filter → export → correlate)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Seconds (ask → answer with cross-system context)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-system correlation&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Manual: 3-4 tabs, copy-paste&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Automatic: the agent orchestrates across MCP servers from one query&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Action gap&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Insight in one tool → switch → act in another&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Insight → action in the same conversation&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Setup cost&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Weeks/months (dashboard design, ETL, permissions, training)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Minutes to connect, once your MCP servers exist&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Run cost&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Fixed: pipelines cost the same whether or not anyone asks&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Usage-based: serverless scales to zero, cost tracks questions (Lambda + agent tokens)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trust &amp;amp; accuracy&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;As fresh as the last ETL load&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Computed live from systems of record and grounded docs, every write human-gated&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This isn’t a “replace everything” argument. Dashboards remain superior for trend visualization, collaborative analysis, regulatory reporting, and exploratory analysis. Use conversational AI for real-time operational decisions and BI for strategic analysis. They complement, not compete.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="personas-and-use-cases"&gt;Personas and use cases&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One Quick Agent serves all three personas: the manager maintains continuity, procurement sources with compliance-awareness, the analyst correlates signals. Persona also bounds reach, through row/column-level security and per-persona MCP-tool scoping, so procurement does not see finance data.&lt;/p&gt;

  &lt;tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tools invoked&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Outcome&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;SC Manager&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Morning disruption check&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Disruption Alerts MCP&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Disruptions, revenue at risk, days of supply&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;SC Manager&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;Quoting MCP&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;RFQ issued + responses validated&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ops Analyst&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Payment queue, audit, disruption trends&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Invoice / Governance MCP&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AP aging, audit log, patterns&lt;/td&gt;

 &lt;/tbody&gt;

&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploying the pattern takes three phases: provision the infrastructure, connect Amazon Quick to your data and the registry, and turn on monitoring.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore and AWS Agent Registry.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) v2, AWS Serverless Application Model (AWS SAM) CLI, and Docker (the agent runs as an arm64 container).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.12 or later, with Boto3 1.43.94 or later for the registry step.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Active credentials with AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions to deploy and register (&lt;code&gt;aws sts get-caller-identity&lt;/code&gt; returns the target account).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Data layer: a database (Snowflake, PostgreSQL/RDS, or a SQLAlchemy engine), an Amazon S3 bucket, optionally a Salesforce org.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Quick set up for your organization.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;(Optional) An Outlook account for email automation.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Setup uses two surfaces, so each of the following steps names where it happens: the &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Quick web console&lt;/strong&gt; in a browser (Amazon Quick Sight permissions, Agent Registry linking, connectors, and agent settings) and the &lt;strong&gt;Quick Desktop App&lt;/strong&gt; (connecting Outlook for email automation). The conversational experience in the Desktop App comes last, once setup is done.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-the-infrastructure"&gt;Deploy the infrastructure&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Everything is open source and scripted: clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-amazon-quick-suite-knowledge-hub/tree/main/examples/quick-for-supply-chain" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;quick-for-supply-chain&lt;/a&gt; example in the aws-samples/sample-amazon-quick-suite-knowledge-hub repository and follow its &lt;code&gt;README.md&lt;/code&gt;, or point a coding agent like Kiro at it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;README.md&lt;/code&gt; covers three phases. Phase 1 deploys the AgentCore stack: the identity layer (an Amazon Cognito user pool and app client, authorization-code OAuth flow, plus a test user), four business-rule MCP servers (each an AWS Lambda function fronted by its own AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, using &lt;code&gt;CUSTOM_JWT&lt;/code&gt; authorization), the gateway targets mapping the tools of each Lambda function, and &lt;code&gt;sc-order-fulfillment-agent&lt;/code&gt; on AgentCore Runtime as a container, with least-privilege IAM roles. Phase 2 uploads the synthetic dataset to Amazon S3. Phase 3 verifies gateways and runtime are Ready. Six Agent Registry records are approved.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-8-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-8-1.png" alt="AWS Agent Registry console showing six approved records: four MCP servers, one agent, and one skills record" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: AWS Agent Registry console with six approved records (four MCP Server, one Agent, one Skills)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="grant-amazon-quick-access-to-your-data"&gt;Grant Amazon Quick access to your data&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In the Amazon Quick web console (browser):&lt;/strong&gt; grant Amazon Quick access from the Amazon Quick Sight admin page (Manage Quick Sight → Security and permissions) to both the S3 bucket and Amazon Athena, since each table is served through an Athena source over an AWS Glue database. Without the Athena grant, dataset creation fails.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-9-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-9-1.png" alt="Amazon Quick Sight Security and permissions page with Amazon Athena selected alongside Amazon S3" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 9: Amazon Quick Sight Security and permissions, with Amazon Athena alongside Amazon S3&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="link-the-registry-and-create-connectors"&gt;Link the registry and create connectors&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Linking the registry is one-time and administrator-level. Creating a connector is per-user. Both happen in the Amazon Quick console. See the AWS Agent Registry integration guide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="link-the-registry-administrator-one-time"&gt;Link the registry (administrator, one-time)&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An administrator links one Agent Registry to the Quick account. &lt;strong&gt;In the Amazon Quick web console (admin):&lt;/strong&gt; go to Manage account → Permissions → AWS Agent Registry and turn on the registry (&lt;code&gt;sc-supply-chain-registry&lt;/code&gt;, one per account). Only registries in your account and Region, agent-registry namespace, AWS_IAM (SigV4) authorization, and READY status appear. The Quick-managed service role links it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-10-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-10-1.png" alt="Registry linking screen showing only READY, AWS_IAM registries as selectable" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 10: Linking the registry, where only READY, AWS_IAM registries are selectable&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="create-a-connector"&gt;Create a connector&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the registry is linked, approved MCP server records become connector cards: the four business MCPs as cards, the order-fulfillment agent added manually. At scale, federate connector authentication through your identity provider and provision connectors to groups, with the Cognito user pool as the seam.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In the Amazon Quick web console (per user):&lt;/strong&gt; the four cards appear on the Connectors page (&lt;code&gt;sc-quoting-mcp&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;sc-governance-mcp&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;sc-invoice-processing-mcp&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;sc-disruption-alert-mcp&lt;/code&gt;). Authenticate through the OAuth authorization-code flow (&lt;code&gt;OAUTH2_AUTHORIZATION_CODE&lt;/code&gt;) with client ID, secret, and token, authorization, and redirect URLs from the deploy script. Sign in as the test user, turn on tools, create and share.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-11-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-11-1.png" alt="The four business-MCP connector cards on the Connectors page" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 11: The four business-MCP connector cards, with the order-fulfillment agent added manually&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Registry-sourced connectors are Custom MCP connectors (relevant for custom permissions). Removing the link later doesn’t delete connectors already created.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 675px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-12-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-12-1.png" alt="The Authenticate step using the authorization-code OAuth flow" width="665"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 12: The Authenticate step using the authorization-code OAuth flow (OAUTH2_AUTHORIZATION_CODE)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="add-the-order-fulfillment-agent-connector"&gt;Add the order-fulfillment agent connector&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In the Amazon Quick web console:&lt;/strong&gt; because it is an agent record, create its connector manually (Connectors → Create for your team → Model Context Protocol → Create new): name it &lt;code&gt;sc-order-fulfillment-agent&lt;/code&gt;, point it at the AgentCore Runtime direct invocation URL from the deploy script, complete the same OAuth sign-in, create and share.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-13-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-22042-13-1.png" alt="Creating the sc-order-fulfillment-agent connector pointed at the AgentCore runtime invocation URL" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 13: Creating the sc-order-fulfillment-agent connector, pointed at the AgentCore runtime invocation URL&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="attach-the-connectors-to-your-agent"&gt;Attach the connectors to your agent&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The deployment creates the Supply Chain agent: a Quick Space, imported datasets and S3 knowledge base, and the agent with instructions, starter prompts, and welcome message. What remains is attaching the connectors.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In the Amazon Quick web console (agent settings):&lt;/strong&gt; open the deployment-created Supply Chain agent (default id &lt;code&gt;sc-supply-chain-agent&lt;/code&gt;) and attach all five connectors (four business MCPs plus &lt;code&gt;sc-order-fulfillment-agent&lt;/code&gt;), or pass the five connector IDs to the setup script.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="set-up-proactive-monitoring"&gt;Set up proactive monitoring&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To make it proactive, Quick runs the same agent on a schedule or on incoming email and posts results to the Activity Feed. Both paths reuse the agent and tools you configured.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In the Quick Desktop App (Settings → Connections):&lt;/strong&gt; connect Outlook and turn on email monitoring. Then, in Quick Chat, create two natural-language schedules, time-based (“Every morning, check for new critical disruptions”) and email-driven (“Every two hours in business hours, check supplier replies and validate”). The agent extracts pricing, runs the checks, and posts a card.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="try-it-in-amazon-quick"&gt;Try it in Amazon Quick&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the agent and connectors ready, ask in natural language in Amazon Quick Chat. Each prompt shows the data-then-rules pattern.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ask Amazon Quick&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What you get&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“What supply chain tools do I have?”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The five MCP connectors (quoting, governance, invoice, disruption alerts, order-fulfillment agent), with status.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“Show me active supply disruptions.”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Active disruptions with severity, region, SKU counts (Disruption Alerts MCP).&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“Assess the impact of the TechComp production halt.”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Revenue at risk, affected orders, days of supply, from your datasets.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“Is this supplier an approved vendor, and do we have budget authority for a $72,000 PO?”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An approval from the Governance MCP (vendor status, budget authority, compliance).&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“Generate a contract-aligned quote for 1,000 units for this customer.”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A contract-aligned quote from the Quoting MCP, as a branded document.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“A supplier just went down. Find alternatives, verify compliance, and issue an emergency RFQ.”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The Order Fulfillment Agent chains disruption, governance, and quoting MCPs into one recommendation.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore and Amazon Quick are a repeatable enterprise-AI pattern: separate intelligence from interface. AgentCore turns fragmented backends into a composable, governed, AI-accessible layer through standard MCP interfaces. Amazon Quick democratizes access in natural language.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gain is the collapsed decision chain: detect, research, decide, act, and log once spanned five tools, now one conversation. The AI researches, correlates, validates. The person decides. Every call is authenticated, authorized, policy-checked, and audited, and a 45-60 minute routine across five systems can collapse to minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That is the shift from data-rich, insight-poor to data-rich, decision-ready. Start with one MCP server and one persona. The registry grows and the experience stays the same.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Fine-tuning GPT-OSS-20B for multilingual reasoning on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/fine-tuning-gpt-oss-20b-for-multilingual-reasoning-on-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Mijanur Palash]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 20 Oct 2026 17:48:27 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker HyperPod]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">e95a562fb3248db86864f803ae6d83c71f277e90</guid>

					<description>Learn how we fine-tuned GPT-OSS-20B for multilingual reasoning on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod, and how to decide between supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning with GRPO. SFT took reasoning-language accuracy from 22% to 100%, and GRPO added measurable gains where headroom existed.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;GPT-OSS-20B is an open-weight reasoning model that thinks in English. Ask it to reason in French, German, Spanish, or Italian and it acknowledges the instruction, then produces its chain-of-thought in English anyway. On a 50-example multilingual reasoning test, the base model reasoned in the correct target language only 22 percent of the time. That 22 percent was almost entirely the English examples (English 100 percent, French 0 percent, German 10 percent, Spanish 0 percent, Italian 0 percent).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For teams deploying reasoning models for non-English users, that gap matters. A user who asks a question in French expects the model to reason and answer in French, not to switch to English mid-thought. Closing that gap is a fine-tuning problem. It raises a question every team faces once they start customizing open-weight models: supervised fine-tuning (SFT), reinforcement learning with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), or both?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through how we fine-tuned GPT-OSS-20B for multilingual reasoning on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/hyperpod/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod&lt;/a&gt;. We show when SFT alone was sufficient and when GRPO added measurable value on top. To understand more broadly when GRPO helps, we also ran a controlled Qwen2.5-7B experiment across two tasks with different capability gaps. The short answer: SFT does most of the work, GRPO refines when there is headroom to refine, and matching the technique to the capability gap is what decides the outcome.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section covers the multilingual reasoning task and dataset, how SFT and GRPO complement each other, and the training stack we ran on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-task-and-dataset"&gt;The task and dataset&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The dataset is &lt;code&gt;HuggingFaceH4/Multilingual-Thinking&lt;/code&gt; — a chain-of-thought reasoning dataset translated into French, Spanish, German, and Italian alongside English, roughly 949 training and 50 validation examples. The model receives a &lt;code&gt;reasoning language:&lt;/code&gt; system-prompt instruction and must both reason and answer in that target language, overriding its strong English prior. The evaluation is 50 test cases: 10 math/logic problems across 5 languages (EN/FR/DE/ES/IT).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two metrics matter:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reasoning-language correct&lt;/strong&gt; – is the chain-of-thought in the requested language?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Answer-language correct&lt;/strong&gt; – is the final answer in the requested language?&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-sft-and-grpo-fit-together"&gt;How SFT and GRPO fit together&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The clearest way to think about the two techniques: SFT teaches a behavior the model doesn’t have. GRPO optimizes a behavior the model can already sometimes produce. GRPO works by sampling a group of responses per prompt and pushing the policy toward the above-average ones. If the desired behavior has zero probability in the base distribution, there is very little for GRPO to reinforce. That single idea explains almost every result in this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-training-stack"&gt;The training stack&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We ran on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod with Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) orchestration. For the initial SFT and GRPO runs on GPT-OSS-20B, we used the PyTorch TRL library. For the Qwen control experiment, we switched to veRL as the trainer, with Ray and KubeRay for distributed orchestration and vLLM for fast rollout generation. Checkpoints and datasets lived on Amazon FSx for Lustre, and the inter-node fabric used Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA). All fine-tuning used Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), which is parameter-efficient and well-suited to both SFT and GRPO.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the training data path end-to-end. A submission from the user lands on the Amazon EKS control plane of the Amazon SageMaker HyperPod cluster. Amazon EKS schedules a Ray head pod plus worker pods that run the veRL trainer and vLLM rollout workers. Both sets of pods mount Amazon FSx for Lustre for the base model, training data, and checkpoints. Inter-node collectives use EFA. Rollouts stream from vLLM back to the veRL trainer, and the trainer writes checkpoints back to Amazon FSx for Lustre.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/08/ML-21403-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/08/ML-21403-1.png" alt="End-to-end fine-tuning architecture on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: End-to-end training data path for SFT and GRPO fine-tuning on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod with veRL, Ray, KubeRay, vLLM, and Amazon FSx for Lustre for Qwen control experiments&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following describes each hop in more detail. The submitter starts the run from Amazon SageMaker Studio or the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI). The Amazon EKS control plane on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod places pods on GPU nodes and starts a Ray head with worker pods for the veRL trainer and the vLLM rollout workers. Amazon FSx for Lustre provides shared, high-throughput storage for the base model, the training data, and the checkpoints. When training completes, the trainer writes the LoRA adapter back to Amazon FSx for Lustre for downstream evaluation or serving.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow along, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon SageMaker HyperPod cluster with Amazon EKS orchestration. GPT-OSS SFT used 4× ml.g6e.12xlarge (16× L40S 48GB). The Qwen control used 2× g6e.48xlarge (16× L40S).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon FSx for Lustre file system mounted on the cluster with a &lt;code&gt;PersistentVolumeClaim&lt;/code&gt; named &lt;code&gt;fsx-claim&lt;/code&gt; at &lt;code&gt;/fsx&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;EFA-enabled instances and a working NVIDIA Collective Communications Library (NCCL) and &lt;code&gt;aws-ofi-nccl&lt;/code&gt; stack for multi-node SFT.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A container registry such as Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) to hold the training image built from the reference repository.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A HuggingFace access token for &lt;code&gt;openai/gpt-oss-20b&lt;/code&gt; and the &lt;code&gt;HuggingFaceH4/Multilingual-Thinking&lt;/code&gt; dataset.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI), &lt;code&gt;kubectl&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;docker&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;envsubst&lt;/code&gt; available on your workstation.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="set-up-the-training-environment"&gt;Set up the training environment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All of the commands, manifests, and scripts referenced below live in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/awsome-distributed-ai" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;awsome-distributed-ai&lt;/a&gt; GitHub repository under &lt;code&gt;3.test_cases/pytorch/trl/gpt-oss-lora-grpo/&lt;/code&gt;. Clone the repository and change into the test case directory:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/awslabs/awsome-distributed-ai
cd awsome-distributed-ai/3.test_cases/pytorch/trl/gpt-oss-lora-grpo&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Copy the environment template, then edit &lt;code&gt;env_vars&lt;/code&gt; to set your ECR registry, image name, tag, and HuggingFace token:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cp env_vars.example env_vars
source env_vars&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Build the training image and push it to your registry. The image bundles PyTorch, TRL, HuggingFace Transformers, PEFT, and the training and inference scripts:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd artifacts &amp;amp;&amp;amp; ./build_push.sh &amp;amp;&amp;amp; cd ..&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Point &lt;code&gt;kubectl&lt;/code&gt; at your cluster and apply the FSx storage manager pod. The storage manager gives you a shell into the shared file system for checkpoint conversion and eval-log inspection:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws eks update-kubeconfig --name &amp;lt;YOUR_CLUSTER_NAME&amp;gt; --region us-east-2
envsubst '$REGISTRY $IMAGE $TAG $HF_TOKEN' \
  &amp;lt; kubernetes/fsx-storage-manager.yaml | kubectl apply -f -&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="fine-tune-gpt-oss-20b-with-sft-and-lora"&gt;Fine-tune GPT-OSS-20B with SFT and LoRA&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We begin by measuring the base model, then configure the SFT run and examine the results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-base-model-gap"&gt;The base-model gap&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Base GPT-OSS-20B on the 50-case eval scored &lt;strong&gt;22 percent reasoning-language correct&lt;/strong&gt; (11/50). Per-language: English 100 percent, French 0 percent, German 10 percent, Spanish 0 percent, Italian 0 percent. The model defaults to English reasoning regardless of the instruction.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="sft-configuration"&gt;SFT configuration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The SFT setup used LoRA r=8, α=16, trained for 1,000 steps on 4× ml.g6e.12xlarge (16× L40S 48GB) with Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP) full-shard, bf16 precision, and gradient checkpointing. The job runs as an elastic &lt;code&gt;HyperPodPyTorchJob&lt;/code&gt; managed by Kueue, launched with &lt;code&gt;hyperpodrun&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Apply the SFT manifest:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;envsubst '$REGISTRY $IMAGE $TAG $HF_TOKEN' \
  &amp;lt; hyperpod-eks/train-lora-hyperpod-elastic-g6e.yaml | kubectl apply -f -&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Watch training progress:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl get pods -l app=gpt-oss-lora-elastic
kubectl logs -f -l app=gpt-oss-lora-elastic&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Loss and gradient norm print each step. A healthy run looks like this:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;{'loss': 1.035, 'grad_norm': 0.163, 'learning_rate': 0.0002, 'mean_token_accuracy': 0.716, 'epoch': 3.25}
{'loss': 1.003, 'grad_norm': 0.171, 'lr': 0.0002, 'acc': 0.722, 'epoch': 3.32}
{'loss': 0.976, 'grad_norm': 0.159, 'lr': 0.0002, 'acc': 0.726, 'epoch': 3.57}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When training completes, convert the FSDP checkpoint to the Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) format that TRL’s GRPO trainer and the inference scripts expect:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl exec -it fsx-storage-manager -- python /app/convert_fsdp_checkpoint.py \
  --checkpoint /fsx/checkpoints/checkpoint-1000 \
  --output    /fsx/checkpoints/converted-peft/lora-checkpoint-1000-peft&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="sft-results-and-the-staged-learning-curve"&gt;SFT results and the staged learning curve&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After SFT, the model reasons in the correct language &lt;strong&gt;100 percent of the time&lt;/strong&gt; (50/50) across all five languages. SFT learned this in two stages:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Around step 200, the model learned to &lt;em&gt;reason&lt;/em&gt; in the target language (reasoning-language metric hits 100 percent).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Around step 1000, the model additionally learned to give the &lt;em&gt;answer&lt;/em&gt; in the target language.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Final SFT eval (step 1000):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Reasoning-language correct: &lt;strong&gt;100 percent&lt;/strong&gt; (50/50).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Answer-language correct: &lt;strong&gt;62 percent&lt;/strong&gt; (31/50).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Both correct: &lt;strong&gt;62 percent&lt;/strong&gt; (31/50).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Per-language answer accuracy: FR 60 percent, DE 60 percent, ES 30 percent, IT 60 percent, EN 100 percent.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Takeaway:&lt;/strong&gt; SFT is not optional here. The base model can’t follow the reasoning-language instruction at all, and SFT takes reasoning-language from 22 percent to 100 percent. The remaining gap is answer-language at 62 percent. That’s the harder half, because the final answer is short and the English prior is strongest there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="refine-answer-language-with-grpo"&gt;Refine answer-language with GRPO&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Answer-language was the obvious target for GRPO. SFT already produced correct reasoning 100 percent of the time, and the model &lt;em&gt;sometimes&lt;/em&gt; produced the answer in the right language, so there was a signal to reinforce.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="run-grpo-on-top-of-the-sft-checkpoint"&gt;Run GRPO on top of the SFT checkpoint&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We ran GRPO with TRL’s &lt;code&gt;GRPOTrainer&lt;/code&gt;, starting from the SFT checkpoint. The setup: load the SFT LoRA, call &lt;code&gt;merge_and_unload&lt;/code&gt;, and add a new LoRA (r=8, α=16, about 0.068 percent trainable parameters, 13.6M of 20B). Training used K=8 generations per prompt, &lt;code&gt;device_map=auto&lt;/code&gt;, learning rate 1e-6, and 10 epochs. The reward function scored answer-language (±5.0), reasoning-language (±1.5), and brevity (+0.5 or −1.0), for a combined range of −7.5 to +7.0.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Launch GRPO from the converted SFT checkpoint:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;envsubst '$REGISTRY $IMAGE $TAG $HF_TOKEN' \
  &amp;lt; kubernetes/train-grpo-singlenode.yaml | kubectl apply -f -
kubectl logs -f grpo-single&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GRPO writes checkpoints to &lt;code&gt;/fsx/checkpoints/grpo-singlenode/&lt;/code&gt;. Pick the best checkpoint against your held-out eval (the answer-language metric peaked early in this run) and convert it to PEFT format for inference and evaluation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Result (from the SFT checkpoint):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Reasoning-language correct: &lt;strong&gt;100 percent&lt;/strong&gt; (held: GRPO didn’t break what SFT taught).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Answer-language correct: &lt;strong&gt;62 to 70 percent&lt;/strong&gt; (+8 points, 35/50).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Both correct: &lt;strong&gt;70 percent&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Per-language answer: FR 70 percent, DE 80 percent, ES 60 percent, IT 40 percent, EN 100 percent.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GRPO delivered a real +8 point gain on the answer-language metric while holding reasoning at 100 percent. The gain came where there was headroom to gain. Answer-language was the sometimes-correct behavior that SFT had not fully converged on, and GRPO reinforced it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="compare-base-sft-and-grpo-inference-side-by-side"&gt;Compare base, SFT, and GRPO inference side by side&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To see the qualitative difference, deploy three inference pods that share the same base model but load different adapters:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;envsubst '$REGISTRY $IMAGE $TAG $HF_TOKEN' \
  &amp;lt; kubernetes/inference-g6e-base.yaml    | kubectl apply -f -
envsubst '$REGISTRY $IMAGE $TAG $HF_TOKEN' \
  &amp;lt; kubernetes/inference-g6e-trained.yaml | kubectl apply -f -
envsubst '$REGISTRY $IMAGE $TAG $HF_TOKEN' \
  &amp;lt; kubernetes/inference-g6e-grpo.yaml    | kubectl apply -f -&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Run the same French-reasoning prompt through each variant:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl exec -it inference-base    -- python /app/inference_g6e.py \
  --reasoning_language French

kubectl exec -it inference-trained -- python /app/inference_g6e.py \
  --checkpoint /fsx/checkpoints/converted-peft/lora-checkpoint-1000-peft \
  --reasoning_language French

kubectl exec -it inference-grpo    -- python /app/inference_grpo_new.py \
  --use_grpo \
  --grpo_checkpoint /fsx/checkpoints/converted-peft/checkpoint-1470-peft \
  --reasoning_language French&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The base pod reasons in English despite the instruction. The SFT pod reasons in French but often answers in English. The GRPO pod reasons and answers in French. To reproduce the full 50-case eval that generated the numbers earlier, run the evaluation manifest and read the result file from the FSx storage manager:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;envsubst '$REGISTRY $IMAGE $TAG $HF_TOKEN' \
  &amp;lt; kubernetes/eval-grpo.yaml | kubectl apply -f -
kubectl logs -f eval-grpo
kubectl exec fsx-storage-manager -- \
  cat /fsx/experiments/grpo_eval_checkpoint1470_*.txt&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To understand more broadly when GRPO adds value, we ran a controlled experiment on Qwen2.5-7B across two tasks with different capability gaps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="test-the-pattern-with-a-qwen2.5-7b-control"&gt;Test the pattern with a Qwen2.5-7B control&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To test the “match technique to capability gap” thesis on a different model and different tasks, we ran controlled experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. The first task is the same multilingual reasoning task (where headroom exists). The second task is GSM8K, where the model is already at peak accuracy. This is an internal experiment, and the public repo has not yet been released.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Infrastructure:&lt;/strong&gt; 2× g6e.48xlarge (16× L40S), EFA 100Gbps, FSx Lustre, Ray 2.44 + KubeRay 1.4.2, veRL v0.6.1, vLLM 0.8.4.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Arms:&lt;/strong&gt; A = base, B = SFT-only, C = SFT+GRPO.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="multilingual-reasoning-50-cases"&gt;Multilingual reasoning (50 cases)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A base&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;B SFT&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;C SFT+GRPO&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Δ GRPO&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Structured format&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;92%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;98%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+6&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reasoning language&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;96%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;98%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+2&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Answer language&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;38%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;44%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+6&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Both correct&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;38%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;44%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+6&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="gsm8k-a-task-with-no-headroom"&gt;GSM8K, a task with no headroom&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On GSM8K, base Qwen2.5-7B already scores &lt;strong&gt;90.5 percent&lt;/strong&gt; (181/200). GRPO at step 30 also scores 90.5 percent. When most sampled groups are all-correct, the group-relative advantage collapses to zero, so there is no learning signal. This is the other half of the thesis: &lt;strong&gt;GRPO needs headroom (pass@k &amp;gt;&amp;gt; pass@1) to have anything to optimize.&lt;/strong&gt; No headroom means no gain.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="best-practices"&gt;Best practices&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Start with SFT.&lt;/strong&gt; GRPO cannot teach a behavior with zero base probability. It can only reinforce responses the model already sometimes produces. SFT took reasoning-language from 22 percent to 100 percent, and every GRPO win in this work was built on that SFT checkpoint.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use LoRA for both SFT and GRPO.&lt;/strong&gt; LoRA r=8/α=16 was parameter-efficient (0.068 percent trainable), and on the Qwen control it stayed numerically stable through GRPO (grad norm 0.23, about 11.7 GB per GPU). It’s a strong default when you want SFT and GRPO to share the same adapter shape.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Match the technique to the gap.&lt;/strong&gt; Use SFT for missing behavior. Use GRPO for refining an existing, sometimes-correct behavior. GRPO’s realistic ceiling here was +6 to +8 points.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Check for reward headroom before spending GPU-hours.&lt;/strong&gt; If most groups are all-correct (GSM8K 90.5 percent base), the group-relative advantage collapses to zero and GRPO gets no gradient. Verify there is headroom (pass@k &amp;gt;&amp;gt; pass@1) before starting a GRPO run.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up-resources"&gt;Clean up resources&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the workload resources and then the underlying infrastructure:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the training and inference pods and jobs: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl delete hyperpodpytorchjob lora-hyperpod-elastic --ignore-not-found
kubectl delete pod grpo-single inference-base inference-trained inference-grpo \
  eval-grpo fsx-storage-manager --ignore-not-found&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Scale down or delete the Amazon SageMaker HyperPod cluster or its node groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Amazon FSx for Lustre file system once you have exported any checkpoints you want to keep.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete any Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) buckets or objects used for training data or checkpoint archives.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We showed how to fine-tune GPT-OSS-20B for multilingual reasoning on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod, and how to decide between SFT and GRPO. SFT did most of the work: reasoning-language went from 22 percent to 100 percent. GRPO added a real, modest refinement on top (answer 62 percent to 70 percent) where headroom existed. A parallel Qwen2.5-7B control confirmed the pattern. GRPO added +6 percent on multilingual reasoning, where there was room to improve. It added no gain on GSM8K, where the model was already at peak accuracy (90.5 percent).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Decision framework:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Behavior the model can’t do at all: use &lt;strong&gt;SFT&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Behavior it does sometimes, with headroom: use &lt;strong&gt;SFT then GRPO&lt;/strong&gt; for a few points more.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model already at peak accuracy, or target has zero base probability: &lt;strong&gt;GRPO won’t help&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To try this yourself, walk through the test cases in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/awsome-distributed-ai" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;awsome-distributed-ai&lt;/a&gt; repository. For more on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod, see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/hyperpod/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;service detail page&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod documentation&lt;/a&gt;. For a walkthrough of Ray on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod, see the related post &lt;em&gt;Running distributed training on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod with Ray and Amazon SageMaker Studio&lt;/em&gt;. The link will be added after that post is published. Share your results in the comments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Octave built a secure multi-tenant AI foundation on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-octave-built-a-secure-multi-tenant-ai-foundation-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Urvish Saraiya]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 20 Oct 2026 17:34:04 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">71eb785a1fd13603c3885408903f8aca0b20d311</guid>

					<description>Octave built a secure, multi-tenant AI foundation on Amazon Bedrock, using Cohere Embed v4 for embeddings and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore for isolated agent execution, to power a Complaint Triage Assistant that searches 2 billion regulated documents without crossing tenant boundaries.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;In quality management for regulated industries, the institutional memory that resolves a problem is almost always buried somewhere in a customer’s history. A similar defect investigated last year, a Corrective and Preventive Action (CAPA) that fixed the root cause, an audit finding that flagged the same gap. That knowledge sits in billions of documents no human can search by semantic similarity across languages and formats, so every investigation starts from scratch. Octave Reliance has helped each organization collect documents across multiple systems to help them search more easily and keep track of everything they have. The &lt;a href="https://www.octave.com/en" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Octave&lt;/a&gt; Reliance team saw unlocking this history as its single largest opportunity to help customers work faster and stay compliant. The obstacle was equally clear: complaint records, audit findings, and corrective actions carry the platform’s strictest isolation requirements, and those were exactly the records the AI would need to read. In a platform serving over 14,000 customers across medical devices, pharmaceuticals, and food manufacturing, a single inadvertent disclosure from one customer to another would carry regulatory consequences far outweighing any AI benefit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Octave operates across 180 countries, serving more than &lt;a href="https://www.octave.com/newsroom/press-releases/2026/octave-intelligence-plc-listed-on-nasdaq-new-york" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;60% of the Global Fortune 500&lt;/a&gt;. Its cloud-native quality management system, &lt;a href="https://www.octave.com/products/asset-performance-management/reliance" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Reliance&lt;/a&gt;, supports over 650 enterprise customers with over 40 applications, from complaint management and CAPA tracking to audit management and supplier quality. Everyone generates regulated data that AI could reason over. Each customer is a separate tenant and no data can cross a tenant boundary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Faced with that, the Reliance team made an unusual call: rather than shipping one AI feature, it spent its first cycle building a foundation every future feature would sit on. That foundation has since cut deployment time per use case by 50 percent. If you are bringing AI to a software as a service (SaaS) platform where the data is valuable, but isolation is non-negotiable, the reasoning travels beyond quality management. In this post, we walk through the four tensions that shaped the design and the three-layer foundation Octave built with Cohere Embed v4 on Amazon Bedrock. We then follow the pipeline that embedded 2 billion documents and the Complaint Triage Assistant that proved it in production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-a-foundation-not-a-feature"&gt;Why a foundation, not a feature&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Envisioning workshops with AWS surfaced four high-impact use cases: complaint summarization and triage, audit readiness monitoring, incident narrative generation, and predictive quality risk scoring. They also surfaced the insight that shaped everything after dozens of applications, each with different document types and workflows. Building AI for one in a silo meant rebuilding the infrastructure for the next.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;So, the team inverted the plan: build the horizontal platform first, then cut vertical feature slices into it. Complaint summarization and triage went first, to prove the architecture before the rest followed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-four-tensions"&gt;The four tensions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before selecting a single model, the team spent its time on four problems that pulled in different directions. None could be traded away.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Making data available without risking inadvertent disclosure.&lt;/strong&gt; Complaint records, CAPAs, standard operating procedures (SOPs), and audit findings belong to one tenant and must be unreachable by another. Not even an errant AI query could cross. Vector store, retrieval logic, and generation context all had to be scoped to a single tenant.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Executing agents in isolation.&lt;/strong&gt; The permissions governing an agent’s tools, data stores, and model endpoints had to be scoped to the requesting user rather than a shared service role, available only during execution, and respectful of that user’s access rights. A shared agent pool with static credentials failed all three.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reading content that cannot be trusted.&lt;/strong&gt; Agents here ingest complaints, email attachments, scanned PDFs, and images from outside the organization. The OWASP Top 10 for large language model (LLM) Applications (2025) names the threats that apply directly: prompt injection through ingested content (LLM01), sensitive information disclosure (LLM02), improper output handling (LLM05), and excessive agency (LLM06). Hidden instructions in an attached PDF are not hypothetical at this volume.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Embedding 2 billion documents without a quarter-long wait.&lt;/strong&gt; Reliance hosts more than 2 billion documents, with individual tenants storing millions of Word files, PDFs, scanned forms, spreadsheets, images, and email. The team committed to onboarding even the largest tenant within a single working day. Because these documents inform regulatory decisions, quality could not be traded for throughput. A missed complaint erodes trust in the whole system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="where-the-architecture-landed"&gt;Where the architecture landed&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Holding four tensions at once narrowed the options considerably. Amazon Bedrock addressed them as one platform: multi-model access through a single API, the AWS Region residency and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; governance the AWS account already enforces, and Guardrails as production content controls rather than something to build.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The foundation resolves into three layers, shown in Figure 1. Data preparation embeds quality documents with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/cohere/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cohere Embed v4 on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; and writes them to a per-tenant vector store on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon OpenSearch Service&lt;/a&gt;. Secure execution passes a user request through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/?" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, which applies authentication and policy before handing it to an isolated microVM session that calls an Anthropic Claude model on Amazon Bedrock. Wrapping both, content protection applies Amazon Bedrock Guardrails twice: input filtering on the way in, output filtering before anything reaches the handler. Retrieval, execution, and screening stay in separate stages, so each can be enforced and evidenced on its own.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/31/ML-21094-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/31/ML-21094-1.png" alt="High-level architecture of Octave’s AI foundation" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: High-level architecture of the Reliance AI foundation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="choosing-how-to-represent-the-data"&gt;Choosing how to represent the data&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Nothing else worked until the documents were retrievable. That meant accurately, across languages and formats, and not across a tenant boundary. The team evaluated embedding models against its own corpus rather than public leaderboards. The deciding questions were specific to this data: what a photo of a cracked housing has to do with the paragraph describing it, and a German complaint with an English CAPA.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cohere Embed v4 on Amazon Bedrock won on five counts and they weren’t independent of each other. What was decided first was how much of the pipeline was made unnecessary. Quality records carry photos of defective products, scanned inspection forms, and annotated diagrams alongside text. Embed v4 places both modalities in one vector space. A photo of a cracked housing and the paragraph describing that defect are retrievable from a single query, with no separate pipeline per modality. Native multilingual support does the same work along the other axis: documents arrive in multiple languages, often inside one tenant’s corpus, and need no translation preprocessing. Two pipelines the team never had to build, and two fewer places for a document to fall out of retrieval.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The remaining three counts settled where the vectors could live and what they would cost. Customer data can’t leave its AWS Region, even for inference, so in-Region model availability on Amazon Bedrock was a precondition rather than a convenience. Embed v4 supports in-Region inference in the US East (N. Virginia) Region (us-east-1), Europe (Ireland) Region (eu-west-1), and Asia Pacific (Tokyo) Region (ap-northeast-1), covering three of Octave’s regional compliance requirements. Model and feature availability varies by Region and changes over time. For current coverage, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Regional availability by models&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide. Within each Region, Amazon OpenSearch Service costs scale linearly with dimension count. That made Embed v4’s configurable output dimensions of 256–1,536 worth testing rather than accepting by default. Testing against representative production queries showed 512 dimensions holding target accuracy while cutting storage roughly 66 percent against the 1,536 default. The 128K token context window paid off elsewhere entirely: chunks of roughly 4K tokens pack many to a request, which is where the onboarding throughput came from.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="keeping-tenants-apart-at-runtime"&gt;Keeping tenants apart at runtime&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The harder question was where to enforce tenant separation. Application code would have worked, but it would have meant every future feature team getting it right on every code path, where one missed check risks inadvertent cross-tenant disclosure. The team pushed isolation into the runtime instead. AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, enforces separation below the application layer, and because it stays framework- and model-independent, the agent framework and models chosen for the Complaint Triage Assistant were not constrained by the isolation decision.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each user session runs in a dedicated microVM with its own CPU, memory, and filesystem, stopped and sanitized when the session ends. That is hardware-level separation designed to hold regardless of how the agent behaves. AgentCore carries the authenticated user’s identity through the whole execution chain: per-user OAuth token vaults verify the agent acts for that specific user rather than a shared service identity, and credentials are scoped per-agent and live only during the session. AgentCore Gateway authorizes at the tool, operation, and parameter level. Automated reasoning provides formal verification against the defined rules. It’s the same discipline behind IAM and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/pm/serv-s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt; policy validation. Figure 2 traces that separation across two tenants.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/31/ML-21094-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/31/ML-21094-2.png" alt="AgentCore session separation across two tenants, each microVM stopped and its memory sanitized after use" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: AgentCore session separation between tenants. Each microVM is stopped after use with memory sanitized.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A complaint submitted by a quality handler at Tenant A therefore reaches only Tenant A’s token vault, tools, and sandbox in AgentCore Code Interpreter, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Tenant B’s session has no path to any of them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="treating-ingested-content-as-untrusted"&gt;Treating ingested content as untrusted&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This tension had no clean answer. A complaint attachment is untrusted input, but it’s also the thing the agent exists to read, so suspicious content couldn’t be rejected. The team settled on defense in depth.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Octave applied &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/?" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; over the agent logic rather than inside it, at three points in the complaint workflow. The ApplyGuardrail API evaluates text extracted from attachments before embedding, retrieved documents and tool outputs before they enter the agent’s context, and the synthesized brief before the handler sees it. Attachment-derived content is passed with input tagging, marking it as data rather than instruction. That is what makes an injection buried mid-document detectable. Personally Identifiable Information (PII) is masked on the way in.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Detection alone wasn’t sufficient, so bounded agency sits underneath. Whatever an agent is asked to do, AgentCore Gateway still governs which tools it can invoke, and document extraction runs in the AgentCore Code Interpreter sandbox with no network egress and no persistence beyond the session. A payload hidden in a PDF has to defeat every layer, not one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The effect:&lt;/strong&gt; detection and masking rates are tracked per tenant, so what the screening caught is something Octave can evidence rather than assert.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-2-billion-documents-through-the-pipeline"&gt;Getting 2 billion documents through the pipeline&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ahead of the embedding model sat the harder problem: extracting usable content from Word files, spreadsheets, digital PDFs, scanned pages, photographs, and email threads. Several languages appear, sometimes inside one record. And it had to run fast enough that onboarding took a day rather than a quarter. Figure 3 traces the resulting pipeline end to end.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/31/ML-21094-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/31/ML-21094-3.png" alt="Ingestion pipeline: document events flow through Step Functions orchestration to the per-tenant OpenSearch index" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Ingestion pipeline: event-driven document processing from the application platform through Step Functions orchestration to the per-tenant OpenSearch index.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Document events fan out through Amazon Simple Notification Service (Amazon SNS) and Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS) into an AWS Step Functions state machine. That state machine orchestrates parsing, chunking, and embedding across concurrent AWS Lambda executions inside a dedicated Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC). Because parallelism comes from fanning batches across executions rather than scaling one resource, throughput grows without rearchitecting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Parsing handles the heterogeneity: semantic hierarchy preserved from Word files and spreadsheets, optical character recognition (OCR) applied to scanned PDFs lacking a text layer, embedded images treated as first-class content. Chunks carry metadata: tenant, document type, source application, language, and a content hash. That metadata drives both retrieval filtering and per-tenant namespace enforcement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The throughput came from packing. Interleaving many 4K chunks into single requests against Embed v4’s 128K context window cut the API calls for a large corpus by about an order of magnitude. In Octave’s internal testing on Amazon Bedrock, Embed v4 delivered 28 million tokens per minute. After tuning batch sizes with Cohere’s solutions architects until memory bottlenecks cleared, the pipeline sustained 1.4 million documents per 24 hours per tenant workload. That is what made the single-working-day commitment real.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-complaint-triage-assistant"&gt;The Complaint Triage Assistant&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A hospital reports defective syringes to a medical device manufacturer, and the record lands with email, photos of damaged units, and supporting documentation attached. A quality handler has to piece the context together across those formats, work out whether anything like it has been reported before, and find what resolved it. Only then can a priority be assigned, often inside a reporting window measured in days.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The assistant is built with the Strands Agents SDK, open-sourced by AWS, which composes agentic workflows as graphs, with nodes as tool invocations or model calls and edges as the data dependencies between them. AgentCore runtime runs that graph and isolates it per session, and five stages carry a complaint from raw attachments to a finished brief.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Document extraction comes first. PDFs, images, email attachments, and videos all enter AgentCore Code Interpreter, which parses each artifact into a structured intermediate representation. A Claude model on Amazon Bedrock then summarizes the record, and that summary, rather than the raw multi-thousand-token source, becomes the retrieval query. Retrieval runs it against the tenant’s OpenSearch index of Embed v4 embeddings, surfacing similar past complaints and their CAPAs, with AgentCore Gateway enforcing that only authorized namespaces are reachable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;What comes back is not trusted on similarity alone. A separate model call scores results by confidence and discards matches that share vocabulary but differ in root cause. What survives goes to synthesis, where Anthropic Claude Sonnet on Amazon Bedrock assembles the output: key facts, sentiment, urgency indicators, the closest historical records with their outcomes, and a recommended triage priority.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Completed briefs are cached in Amazon DynamoDB with a per-environment time to live (TTL), so repeated views cost nothing extra. The handler no longer starts from zero, but from institutional memory.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 4 places that engine behind a private API boundary. A portal request reaches AgentCore runtime through a private Amazon API Gateway and a Lambda function, and retrieval reads the document index through role-based access with pre-signed URLs while attachments stay in Amazon S3. Cached summaries and OpenTelemetry traces exit to the summarization table and the evaluation dashboard.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/31/ML-21094-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/31/ML-21094-4.png" alt="AgentCore summarizing engine handling document extraction, retrieval, reasoning, and synthesis inside a private API boundary" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Summarizing engine: the AgentCore-powered workflow handling document extraction, retrieval, reasoning, and synthesis within a private API boundary, with response caching and full observability.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 5 reduces the same path to a single line, with Guardrails filtering at both ends.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/31/ML-21094-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/31/ML-21094-5.png" alt="Complaint Triage Assistant pipeline from ingestion to handler output, with Guardrails filtering at both ends" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Complaint Triage Assistant pipeline from ingestion to handler output&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="proving-what-the-agent-did"&gt;Proving what the agent did&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Observability here is not only how the engineering team debugs but also how they integrate with system auditing capabilities to meet compliance requirements. An auditor can ask why a particular complaint was triaged the way it was, and “the model decided” will not survive that conversation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore captures OpenTelemetry-compatible traces across every node of the graph: each model call, retrieval, and AgentCore Code Interpreter run. Months later, Octave can show which retrieved documents and prompts produced a given brief. The model invocation logging of Amazon Bedrock adds a per-tenant record of every request and response, scoped through the IAM identity AgentCore propagates, with costs attributing to the initiating tenant in Amazon CloudWatch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Offline, AgentCore Evals reruns the workflow against expert-annotated ground truth after any change to prompts, models, or retrieval parameters, reporting summarization faithfulness, retrieval precision at K, and output completeness. A prompt that reads better but retrieves worse gets caught before users see it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Complaint Triage Assistant has been running in production for three months, and the following numbers reflect that period.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The handler who used to open an investigation with nothing now opens it with the relevant history already assembled. The 4–6 hours Octave measures per investigation is the work the pipeline absorbed: extraction across formats, the hunt for precedent, and the reading it takes to tell a genuinely similar defect from a coincidence of wording.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The foundation also changed how quickly Octave ships. By its own accounting, development cycles for new AI capabilities went from months to weeks. Shared data preparation, secure execution, and retrieval are the three layers of Figure 1. They cut deployment time per use case by 50 percent, because each new capability inherits them rather than rebuilding them. That landed semantic search in Q2, two quarters ahead of the original Q4 target.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="looking-back"&gt;Looking back&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The decision to build a foundation first was the one the team second-guessed most. Months of infrastructure work is hard to defend when stakeholders want an AI feature they can see. The next round of use cases has borne it out. Two choices carried it: isolation pushed into microVM sessions and identity propagation rather than application code, and embedding models evaluated against Octave’s own corpus, which surfaced the 512-dimension trade-off no benchmark would have shown.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team also drew a line it chose not to cross. The assistant surfaces context and precedent. The quality handler still makes the call. In an industry where that call carries regulatory weight, that boundary shaped what got automated and what did not.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-next"&gt;What’s next&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Octave is now extending the foundation across the Reliance suite. Audit readiness monitoring, incident narrative generation, and predictive quality risk scoring are next, each building on the same infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your platform faces a similar tension: valuable data behind hard isolation requirements. Octave’s experience shows the trade-offs are workable. To learn more about the services behind this story, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/cohere/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cohere on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. For related architectural guidance, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/aws-vector-solutions-build-agentic-ai-where-your-data-lives/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS vector solutions: Build agentic AI where your data lives&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/scaling-agentic-ai-enterprise-patterns-without-vendor-lock-in/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Scaling agentic AI: Enterprise patterns without vendor lock-in&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
