			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Access OpenAI GPT models on Amazon Bedrock from Canada with cross-Region inference</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/access-openai-gpt-models-on-amazon-bedrock-from-canada-with-cross-region-inference/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Roopesh Ramklass]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 20 Oct 2026 16:42:53 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">64795cdbf69a431d795a47585bec95352a07ed61</guid>

					<description>Canadian organizations can now access OpenAI GPT-5.6, GPT-6, and GPT-6.1 models on Amazon Bedrock from the Canada (Central) and Canada West (Calgary) Regions using cross-Region inference. This post covers the models and inference profiles, how to set up permissions, and how to invoke them with the Responses, Chat Completions, and Converse APIs.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Canadian organizations building generative AI applications on Amazon Bedrock often need the latest foundation models (FMs), such as OpenAI GPT-6.1, OpenAI GPT-6, and GPT-5.6, accessible from the Canadian AWS Regions. With Amazon Bedrock cross-Region inference (CRIS), you can now access these models from Canada. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/openai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenAI GPT-5.6 models (Sol, Terra, and Luna), GPT-6 (Astra, Sol, and Luna), and GPT-6.1 Sol&lt;/a&gt; are available from the Canada (Central) and Canada West (Calgary) Regions. Inference is processed in North America, with data-residency controls described later in this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cross-Region inference is a capacity mechanism. Your application calls the Amazon Bedrock Runtime endpoint in your source Region. Amazon Bedrock then routes the request to a supported destination Region for processing. With this approach, you get access to a broader capacity pool that can help your application sustain throughput during demand spikes. You don’t need to manage destination Region routing in your application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through the GPT-5.6, GPT-6, and GPT-6.1 models available from Canada and the cross-Region inference profiles that serve them. We then show how to set up permissions and invoke the models with the OpenAI Responses API, Chat Completions API, and the Amazon Bedrock Converse API. For the Responses API, we include streaming and tool-use examples. Finally, we cover how to request quota increases, review the observability signals available in your source Region, and clean up when you are done.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="models-available-from-canada"&gt;Models available from Canada&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Amazon Bedrock, you can access three families of OpenAI models in Canada through cross-Region inference. The following table summarizes each model and its positioning.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="12px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Positioning&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPT-6.1 Sol&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Strong reasoning and coding for tasks where both performance and cost matter&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPT-6 Astra&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Complex end-to-end reasoning and agentic tasks&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPT-6 Sol&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Strong reasoning and coding for recurring tasks with economics suited to regular use&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPT-6 Luna&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Fast, low-cost inference for high-volume repeated tasks with adjustable reasoning effort per request&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPT-5.6 Sol&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;High reasoning, coding, and agentic workloads&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPT-5.6&amp;nbsp;Terra&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Balanced performance and cost for everyday production&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPT-5.6 Luna&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Fast and affordable for high-volume, latency-sensitive applications&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These models accept text and image inputs, return text, and support a 1-million-token context window, reasoning mode, and tool calling. GPT-6.1 Sol supports the same context window and tool calling, with additional input and output modalities documented in its model card. GPT-5.6, GPT-6, and GPT-6.1 also support prompt caching on Amazon Bedrock (see the Prompt caching section). These models are available through the OpenAI Responses API, the OpenAI Chat Completions API, and the Amazon Bedrock Converse API on the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For pricing, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing&lt;/a&gt;. For per-model Region availability, see the model cards in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-cards-openai.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock User Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cross-region-inference-profiles-from-canada"&gt;Cross-Region inference profiles from Canada&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock inference profiles are logical identifiers you pass in place of a raw model ID. Each profile defines a model and the set of Regions to which Amazon Bedrock can route a request. Two profile types are available from Canada:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;US geographic&lt;/strong&gt; (prefixed &lt;code&gt;us.&lt;/code&gt;) routes requests to your Canadian source Region and US Regions. Choose this profile when your workload requires inference processing to remain within North America (Canada and the US).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Global&lt;/strong&gt; (prefixed &lt;code&gt;global.&lt;/code&gt;) routes requests to supported commercial AWS Regions where the model is deployed. This draws on a broader capacity pool at the time of the request.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Endpoint availability differs by model and source Region, as shown in the following table.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;US geographic profile&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Global profile&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPT-6.1 Sol&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ca-central-1, ca-west-1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ca-central-1, ca-west-1&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPT-6 Astra / Sol / Luna&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ca-central-1, ca-west-1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ca-central-1, ca-west-1&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPT-5.6 Sol / Terra / Luna&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ca-central-1, ca-west-1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ca-central-1&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Profile membership and model availability can change. The global profile for the GPT-5.6 models is available only from &lt;code&gt;ca-central-1&lt;/code&gt;. From &lt;code&gt;ca-west-1&lt;/code&gt;, use the US geographic profile for GPT-5.6. Verify the active profiles for your source Region before deployment, either with the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) or on the console (see the following section), or see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cross-region-inference-support.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cross-Region inference support documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections walk through the setup and code needed to invoke these models from your Canadian source Region.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, confirm that you have the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with the Canada (Central) &lt;code&gt;ca-central-1&lt;/code&gt; or Canada West (Calgary) &lt;code&gt;ca-west-1&lt;/code&gt; Region enabled as your source Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role or user with permissions to invoke the inference profiles (see &lt;strong&gt;Set up permissions&lt;/strong&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A service control policy (SCP) that allows the inference profiles in your source Region, if your organization enforces SCPs (see SCP considerations).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.9 or later with the &lt;code&gt;openai&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;boto3&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;aws-bedrock-token-generator&lt;/code&gt; packages installed.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="set-up-permissions"&gt;Set up permissions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For quick exploration, attach the AWS managed policy &lt;code&gt;AmazonBedrockLimitedAccess&lt;/code&gt;. For deployed workloads with stricter access controls, use a least-privilege custom policy. The following example grants access to the GPT-6 Astra global profile from &lt;code&gt;ca-central-1&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["bedrock:InvokeModel*"],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:bedrock:ca-central-1::inference-profile/global.openai.gpt-6-astra"
      ]
    },
    {
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["bedrock:InvokeModel*"],
      "Resource": ["arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/openai.gpt-6-astra"],
      "Condition": {
        "StringLike": {
          "bedrock:InferenceProfileArn": "arn:aws:bedrock:ca-central-1::inference-profile/global.openai.gpt-6-astra"
        }
      }
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you authenticate through the API-key path (bearer token for the OpenAI SDK), also grant &lt;code&gt;bedrock:CallWithBearerToken&lt;/code&gt;. For the Responses API, add &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt; on &lt;code&gt;arn:aws:bedrock:ca-central-1:*:project/default&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="scp-considerations"&gt;SCP considerations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your organization restricts Regions through SCPs, use the &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InferenceProfileArn&lt;/code&gt; condition key to allow CRIS traffic while blocking direct calls to unapproved Regions. The following SCP denies Amazon Bedrock inference outside your approved source Regions unless the request uses a CRIS inference profile:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "DenyBedrockOutsideCanadaUnlessCris",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": ["bedrock:InvokeModel*"],
      "Resource": "*",
      "Condition": {
        "StringNotEquals": {
          "aws:RequestedRegion": ["ca-central-1", "ca-west-1"]
        },
        "ArnNotLike": {
          "bedrock:InferenceProfileArn": [
            "arn:aws:bedrock:*:*:inference-profile/us.openai.*",
            "arn:aws:bedrock:*:*:inference-profile/global.openai.*"
          ]
        }
      }
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With this condition, Amazon Bedrock can route to the profile’s destination Regions even when those Regions are otherwise denied by your SCP. Every destination Region in your chosen profile must be allowed for the request to succeed. For more detail, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/inference-profiles-prereq.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Set up cross-Region inference in multi-account environments&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="verify-active-inference-profiles"&gt;Verify active inference profiles&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before invoking a model, you can verify which profiles are active in your source Region with the AWS CLI. The following command lists the active OpenAI profiles from &lt;code&gt;ca-central-1&lt;/code&gt;. To run the same check from Calgary, replace &lt;code&gt;ca-central-1&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;ca-west-1&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock list-inference-profiles \
    --region ca-central-1 \
    --type-equals SYSTEM_DEFINED \
    --query "inferenceProfileSummaries[?contains(inferenceProfileId, 'openai')].[inferenceProfileId,status]" \
    --output table&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Amazon Bedrock console offers the same view. Open the console, choose &lt;strong&gt;Canada (Central)&lt;/strong&gt; or &lt;strong&gt;Canada West (Calgary)&lt;/strong&gt; as your Region, choose &lt;strong&gt;Cross-Region inference&lt;/strong&gt; under &lt;strong&gt;Inference&lt;/strong&gt;, and review the available profiles.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the active OpenAI inference profiles listed on the Amazon Bedrock console for the Canada (Central) Region.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/16/ML-21126-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/16/ML-21126-1.png" alt="Amazon Bedrock console listing active OpenAI cross-Region inference profiles for the Canada (Central) Region" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Active OpenAI cross-Region inference profiles on the Amazon Bedrock console (Canada (Central))&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="invoke-the-models-through-amazon-bedrock-runtime"&gt;Invoke the models through Amazon Bedrock Runtime&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-5.6, GPT-6, and GPT-6.1 support three access paths on the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; endpoint: the OpenAI Responses API, the OpenAI Chat Completions API, and the Amazon Bedrock Converse API. The OpenAI-compatible APIs are called on the &lt;code&gt;/openai/v1&lt;/code&gt; paths of the endpoint. The endpoint accepts either AWS Signature Version 4 (SigV4) or an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/api-keys.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock API key&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following examples use the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/aws-bedrock-token-generator-python" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock token generator for Python&lt;/a&gt; to create a short-term Amazon Bedrock API key from your current AWS credentials, so your application does not need to store a static key.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="openai-responses-api"&gt;OpenAI Responses API&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For applications that already use the OpenAI SDK with the Responses API, point the client at the Regional Amazon Bedrock Runtime endpoint and pass the inference profile ID as the model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from aws_bedrock_token_generator import provide_token
from openai import OpenAI

region = "ca-central-1"  # Use "ca-west-1" for Calgary
client = OpenAI(
    base_url=f"https://bedrock-runtime.{region}.amazonaws.com/openai/v1",
    api_key=provide_token(region=region),
)

response = client.responses.create(
    model="global.openai.gpt-6-astra",
    input="What are the key considerations for AI governance in Canadian financial services?",
    max_output_tokens=1024,
)
print(response.output_text)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For streaming output, set &lt;code&gt;stream=True&lt;/code&gt; and iterate over the response events, printing text as it arrives:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;stream = client.responses.create(
    model="global.openai.gpt-6-luna",
    input="List five generative AI use cases for Canadian healthcare.",
    max_output_tokens=512,
    stream=True,
)
for event in stream:
    if event.type == "response.output_text.delta":
        print(event.delta, end="", flush=True)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="openai-chat-completions-api"&gt;OpenAI Chat Completions API&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The OpenAI Chat Completions format works through the same endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.chat.completions.create(
    model="global.openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "Summarize recent AI regulatory trends in Canada."}],
    max_completion_tokens=1024,
    reasoning_effort="low",
)
print(response.choices[0].message.content)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the full list of supported parameters, including reasoning effort levels, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-cards-openai.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model cards&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-bedrock-converse-api"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Converse API&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use the Converse API when your application calls Amazon Bedrock through an AWS SDK. Boto3 resolves credentials through the standard AWS credential chain.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

bedrock = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="ca-central-1")
response = bedrock.converse(
    modelId="us.openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": "Summarize recent AI regulatory trends in Canada."}]}],
    inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 1024},
)
# GPT-5.6 and GPT-6 may return a reasoningContent block alongside the text.
# Select the block that contains the text output.
text_output = next(block["text"] for block in response["output"]["message"]["content"] if "text" in block)
print(text_output)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same call works for GPT-6 Sol, GPT-6 Luna, and GPT-6.1 Sol by changing the inference profile ID. The following example uses the US geographic profile for GPT-6 Sol and the global profile for GPT-6 Luna. Both were validated from &lt;code&gt;ca-central-1&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# GPT-6 Sol, GPT-6 Luna, and GPT-6.1 Sol are called the same way. Swap the inference profile ID, and choose a us. (US geographic) or global. profile to match your data boundary.
# For example, GPT-6.1 Sol via the US geographic profile: modelId="us.openai.gpt-6.1-sol"
# GPT-6 Sol via the US geographic profile: strong reasoning and coding for complex work.
response = bedrock.converse(
    modelId="us.openai.gpt-6-sol",
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": "Summarize recent AI regulatory trends in Canada."}]}],
    inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 512},
)

# GPT-6 Luna via the global profile: fast, low-cost inference for high-volume tasks such as classification.
response = bedrock.converse(
    modelId="global.openai.gpt-6-luna",
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": "Classify this ticket as BILLING, TECHNICAL, or OTHER: ..."}]}],
    inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 256},
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tool-use"&gt;Tool use&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These models support &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/tool-use-client-side.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;client-side tool use (function calling)&lt;/a&gt;. Define tools in the &lt;code&gt;tools&lt;/code&gt; parameter, and the model returns a structured tool-call request for your application to execute.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.responses.create(
    model="global.openai.gpt-6-astra",
    input="What's the current exchange rate from CAD to USD?",
    tools=[{
        "type": "function",
        "name": "get_exchange_rate",
        "parameters": {
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {
                "from_currency": {"type": "string"},
                "to_currency": {"type": "string"}
            }
        }
    }],
)

# The model returns a tool-call request rather than text.
for item in response.output:
    if item.type == "function_call":
        print(f"Tool: {item.name}, Args: {item.arguments}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prompt-caching"&gt;Prompt caching&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-5.6, GPT-6, and GPT-6.1 support prompt caching on Amazon Bedrock to reduce the cost and latency of repeated prompt prefixes. Implicit caching requires no changes to your code: Amazon Bedrock automatically caches eligible prefixes of 1,024 tokens or more. Cached tokens read on subsequent requests are billed at a discounted rate and do not count against your tokens-per-minute quota. Cached tokens appear in the response usage object and in Amazon CloudWatch metrics so you can track usage. For cache-write pricing, minimum prefix sizes, and support for explicit cache breakpoints, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;prompt caching documentation&lt;/a&gt; and the individual &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-cards-openai.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model cards&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="quota-management"&gt;Quota management&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cross-Region inference quotas on the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; endpoint are measured in tokens per minute (TPM) per model. Each quota combines input and output tokens against a single limit. US geographic and global inference profiles have separate quota allocations, so switching between them changes the pool you draw from.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Token consumption combines input tokens, cache-write input tokens, and output tokens. Output tokens are multiplied by the model’s burndown rate, so output-heavy workloads consume quota faster than raw token counts suggest. Before a production rollout, request quota increases early and monitor quota utilization. Test with representative prompts, output lengths, streaming behavior, concurrency, and peak traffic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To request a quota increase, follow these steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/servicequotas/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Service Quotas console&lt;/a&gt; in &lt;code&gt;ca-central-1&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;ca-west-1&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/strong&gt;, then select the quota named “Cross-Region InvokeModel tokens per minute” for the model that you need.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Request increase at account level&lt;/strong&gt;, enter your target value, and submit the request.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For details on how token usage and costs are tracked, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-management.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Track usage and costs in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="observability"&gt;Observability&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because these models run through the Amazon Bedrock Runtime API, requests made through an inference profile appear in your observability tooling the same way on-demand requests do. Invocation logs and Amazon CloudWatch metrics are recorded in your source Region. This holds regardless of where the request was processed. Your observability stays in one place.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS CloudTrail&lt;/strong&gt; logs every request, recording which destination Region served it in the &lt;code&gt;additionalEventData.inferenceRegion&lt;/code&gt; field. For example: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;"additionalEventData": {
  "inferenceProfileArn": "arn:aws:bedrock:ca-central-1:111122223333:inference-profile/global.openai.gpt-6-astra",
  "inferenceRegion": "us-west-2"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon CloudWatch metrics&lt;/strong&gt; are published per inference profile, covering invocation count, token count, latency, throttles, and errors.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model invocation logging&lt;/strong&gt; delivers full request and response payloads to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) or Amazon CloudWatch Logs. To activate it on the Amazon Bedrock console, choose &lt;strong&gt;Settings&lt;/strong&gt;, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Model invocation logging&lt;/strong&gt;. You can also call the &lt;code&gt;PutModelInvocationLoggingConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; API. It is off by default.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Data Exports&lt;/strong&gt; itemizes spend by model and profile.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-cloudwatch-coding-agent-insights"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch Coding Agent Insights&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you use these models to power AI coding assistants such as OpenAI Codex, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudWatch/latest/monitoring/coding-agents-insights.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch Coding Agent Insights&lt;/a&gt; provides a dedicated dashboard. It surfaces token usage, API request counts, active users, and cost attribution across dimensions such as department, team, and environment. To activate it, configure your coding agent to export telemetry to Amazon CloudWatch. For the setup steps, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudWatch/latest/monitoring/coding-agents-codex.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Set up OpenTelemetry for OpenAI Codex&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges after testing, remove the resources you created:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn off model invocation logging. On the Amazon Bedrock console, choose &lt;strong&gt;Settings&lt;/strong&gt;, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Model invocation logging&lt;/strong&gt;, or call &lt;code&gt;DeleteModelInvocationLoggingConfiguration&lt;/code&gt;. This stops delivery of logs to Amazon S3 and Amazon CloudWatch Logs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete or detach the IAM policies and any Amazon Bedrock API keys that you created for testing.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The inference profiles themselves are system-defined and incur no cost when they aren’t in use.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-and-compliance"&gt;Security and compliance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cross-Region inference is designed to protect your data in transit and at rest. Requests travel over the AWS private backbone encrypted with TLS 1.2+ rather than the public internet. For Canadian customers, your data at rest stays in your source Region by default. This includes invocation logs, knowledge bases, and configurations. Only the transient inference computation happens in the destination Region, and by default model providers don’t have access to your prompts or completions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There’s one consideration specific to OpenAI models. Content flagged by automated abuse classifiers might be retained for up to 30 days in the destination Region for review. If you want to constrain processing to North America (Canada and the US), you can choose the US geographic profile. We recommend reviewing &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/abuse-detection.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock abuse detection&lt;/a&gt; with your compliance team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a more detailed look at Canadian data residency patterns, see &lt;a href="https://builder.aws.com/content/3EuyyJgS9hWozGI9oUJIU6nCfMk/cross-region-inference-and-canadian-data-residency-a-technical-architecture-guide" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cross-Region Inference and Canadian Data Residency: A Technical Architecture Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how to access OpenAI GPT-5.6 (Sol, Terra, and Luna), GPT-6 (Astra, Sol, and Luna), and GPT-6.1 Sol from Canada. These models are available from the Canada (Central) and Canada West (Calgary) Regions using cross-Region inference on Amazon Bedrock. We covered the available inference profiles, how to set up permissions and an example SCP, and how to invoke the models with the Responses, Chat Completions, and Converse APIs. For the Responses API, we included streaming and tool-use examples. We also showed how to request quota increases, review usage, and clean up your test resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, review the OpenAI model cards in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-cards-openai.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock User Guide&lt;/a&gt;, then open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock console&lt;/a&gt; and send a test prompt through an inference profile. For pricing, see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing page&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you have questions or feedback, leave a comment on this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Discover tools at runtime with AWS Agent Registry</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/discover-tools-at-runtime-with-aws-agent-registry/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Vibhu Pareek]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 20 Oct 2026 16:22:24 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">3a6f4d4b37bd5f432ae64ac9368553ce7e94b9ac</guid>

					<description>Hardcoded tool wiring breaks down as an agent's catalog grows from a handful of tools to hundreds. This post walks through a runtime Discover, Instantiate, Execute loop built on AWS Agent Registry, where an orchestrator discovers MCP servers and A2A agents through semantic search at runtime instead of compiling every tool at build time.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Runtime discovery with AWS Agent Registry can help address a problem that agent demos might hide. Hardcoded tools work fine for a handful, start to degrade at a few dozen, and become unmanageable beyond that. Enterprise systems expose hundreds of internal APIs for pricing, support, orders, inventory, and customer data. In these systems, the model can spend more tokens disambiguating tool names than reasoning about the user’s request. Each new capability requires a code change in the orchestrator, a redeploy, and a coordinated release across teams that would much rather ship on their own cadence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This pattern can arise across enterprise software as a service (SaaS) engagements where teams expose large internal API surfaces to agentic consumers. Build-time wiring can become a bottleneck as the catalog expands. One approach is to change how the orchestrator accesses its tools: instead of compiling every tool into the agent, the agent can discover relevant tools at runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry&lt;/a&gt; is a managed service for cataloging and discovering Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, agents, and agent skills. Publishers register protocol-specific descriptors and searchable descriptions. At runtime, an orchestrator can search approved, discoverable records and use the returned endpoint and descriptor content to construct a client. Registry search results do not return the capability’s invocation credentials or authentication scheme.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post walks through the Discover → Instantiate → Execute loop demonstrated by the refactored Python sample. The code deploys an order-management MCP server through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, two agent-to-agent (A2A) agents on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore runtime&lt;/a&gt;, a Registry, and an orchestrator. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/ach1ntya/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/discovery-and-invocation-at-runtime" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample directory&lt;/a&gt; contains the deployment script, orchestrator, helper modules, and cleanup script.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview-discover-instantiate-execute"&gt;Solution overview: discover, instantiate, execute&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The orchestrator agent in this sample has no built-in knowledge of tools or agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21108-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21108-1-1.png" alt="Comparison of hardcoded tool endpoints versus runtime discovery with AWS Agent Registry" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Without AWS Agent Registry (left), endpoints are hardcoded and adding a new agent means code changes plus redeployment. With AWS Agent Registry (right), the orchestrator discovers capabilities through semantic search and new integrations are usable after registration and approval&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="discover"&gt;Discover&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The orchestrator sends a natural-language query to the AWS Agent Registry data plane. The search response includes record identity, name, description, record type, status, version, and protocol descriptors such as MCP server metadata or an A2A agent card. It does not include invocation credentials. The application must obtain and apply the appropriate credentials through a separate, trusted configuration path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="instantiate"&gt;Instantiate&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For each returned record, the orchestrator constructs a client from the descriptor type. In this sample, the registered MCP server represents tools that were first added as targets to Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway. The MCP descriptor therefore points to the Gateway endpoint. The orchestrator fetches a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cognito OAuth&lt;/a&gt; access token separately and uses it when creating the &lt;code&gt;MCPClient&lt;/code&gt;. A2A records contain an agent card whose URL identifies an AgentCore runtime endpoint. The wrapper invokes that runtime through the &lt;a href="https://boto3.amazonaws.com/v1/documentation/api/latest/reference/services/agent-registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;boto3&lt;/a&gt; data-plane client, which signs the request with the caller’s AWS credentials. Registry discovery supplies connection metadata, not the authentication material.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="execute"&gt;Execute&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An ephemeral inner agent is built with only the dynamically-discovered tools. The large language model (LLM) picks the right one(s) for the request, and the result is synthesized back to the user. After the turn finishes, the inner agent and its clients are torn down. The next request gets a fresh search and a fresh tool set.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21108-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21108-2-1.png" alt="End-to-end architecture: the orchestrator queries AWS Agent Registry, then invokes MCP servers through AgentCore Gateway and A2A agents on AgentCore runtime" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: End-to-end architecture. A single orchestrator on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime queries AWS Agent Registry. Matched MCP servers are invoked through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway, and matched A2A agents are invoked on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reference architecture used in the sample includes &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime&lt;/a&gt;, Amazon Bedrock, Amazon Cognito, AWS Lambda, and AWS Secrets Manager.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry&lt;/a&gt; provides the governed, searchable catalog queried by the orchestrator. Only records that complete the configured approval workflow are available through the discoverable data plane.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt; exposes the order-management Lambda tools as an MCP server. The sample configures &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cognito JWT&lt;/a&gt; authorization for inbound Gateway calls and uses the Gateway AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role to invoke the Lambda target.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime&lt;/a&gt; hosts the orchestrator and the Pricing and Customer Support A2A agents. Runtime invocations use &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/introduction.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM-authorized&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://boto3.amazonaws.com/v1/documentation/api/latest/reference/services/bedrock-agentcore-control.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Boto3&lt;/a&gt; calls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; provides the foundation model (FM) used by the orchestrator and A2A agents. The sample currently configures an Anthropic Claude Sonnet model through a cross-Region model ID. See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three demo scenarios exercise the discovery loop with different capability combinations. The first is an MCP-only call (order status). The second chains MCP + A2A (pricing and discounts). The third tests a pure semantic match against ambiguous user intent: return and refund, where those words don’t appear in the matched A2A agent’s name.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="technical-implementation"&gt;Technical implementation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The linked runtime-discovery &lt;a href="https://github.com/ach1ntya/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/discovery-and-invocation-at-runtime" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample&lt;/a&gt; deploys the dependencies, creates and populates the Registry, verifies discovery, deploys the orchestrator, and runs three demos. &lt;a href="https://github.com/ach1ntya/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/discovery-and-invocation-at-runtime" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;orchestrator_agent.py&lt;/a&gt; contains the per-request discovery loop. &lt;a href="https://github.com/ach1ntya/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/discovery-and-invocation-at-runtime" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;utils.py&lt;/a&gt; parses Registry descriptors and creates MCP and A2A clients. &lt;a href="https://github.com/ach1ntya/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/discovery-and-invocation-at-runtime" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;cleanup.py&lt;/a&gt; removes resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-registration-model"&gt;1. The registration model&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A Registry record contains a searchable description plus a protocol-specific descriptor. The sample uses the Agent Registry control-plane client for Registry and record lifecycle operations and the Agent Registry data-plane client for runtime discovery.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Creating a Registry with manual approval and IAM authorization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;registry_client = session.client("agent-registry-control")
dp_client = session.client("agent-registry")
reg = registry_client.create_registry(
    name="OrderManagementRegistry",
    description=(
        "Registry for Order Management &amp;amp; Customer Service — "
        "agent discovers tools and agents via semantic search"
    ),
    approvalConfiguration={"autoApprovalRules": []},
)
REGISTRY_ARN = reg["registryArn"]
REGISTRY_ID = REGISTRY_ARN.split("/")[-1]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;recordType&lt;/code&gt; field is required when creating a record. In this sample, MCP servers use &lt;code&gt;recordType="MCP"&lt;/code&gt; and A2A-backed agents use &lt;code&gt;recordType="AGENT"&lt;/code&gt;. Protocol metadata is stored under &lt;code&gt;mcpServer&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;a2aAgentCard&lt;/code&gt; in the &lt;code&gt;descriptors&lt;/code&gt; object. An MCP descriptor contains server metadata and tool definitions. An A2A descriptor contains the agent card. The descriptors contain connection metadata but not the credentials that a consumer must present when invoking the capability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Building current public-SDK descriptor shapes for MCP and A2A records.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# MCP descriptor
mcp_descriptors = {
    "mcpServer": {
        "data": json.dumps(server_metadata),
        "dataSchemaVersion": "2025-12-11",
        "additionalData": {
            "tools": {
                "data": json.dumps({"tools": tool_schemas}),
                "dataSchemaVersion": "2025-06-18",
            }
        },
    }
}

# A2A descriptor
a2a_descriptors = {
    "a2aAgentCard": {
        "data": json.dumps(agent_card),
        "dataSchemaVersion": "0.3",
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Write descriptions in user-facing language that distinguishes the capability from neighboring records. Descriptor content supplies the protocol-specific server, tool, or agent-card data. Search quality should be evaluated with representative queries. This post does not assume that catalog growth automatically improves ranking.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Creating a Registry record with the current control-plane shape.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;descriptors = (
    mcp_descriptors
    if cfg["protocol"] == "MCP"
    else a2a_descriptors
)

response = registry_client.create_registry_record(
    registryId=REGISTRY_ID,
    name=name,
    description=cfg["description"],
    recordType=cfg["protocol"],
    descriptors=descriptors,
    recordVersion="1.0",
)
record_id = response["recordId"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="approval-workflow"&gt;2. Approval workflow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With &lt;code&gt;autoApproval&lt;/code&gt; set to false, a new record must move from &lt;code&gt;DRAFT&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;PENDING_APPROVAL&lt;/code&gt; and then &lt;code&gt;APPROVED&lt;/code&gt;. The submit and status-update actions are &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/introduction.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM-authorized&lt;/a&gt;, allowing publisher and approver permissions to be separated. Only approved records are returned by the discoverable Registry data plane.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;The two-step approval transition.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;record = registry_client.get_registry_record(
    registryId=REGISTRY_ID,
    recordId=record_id,
)
if record["status"] == "DRAFT":
    registry_client.submit_registry_record_for_approval(
        registryId=REGISTRY_ID,
        recordId=record_id,
    )
    registry_client.update_registry_record_status(
        registryId=REGISTRY_ID,
        recordId=record_id,
        status="APPROVED",
        statusReason="Approved",
    )&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-discovery-layer"&gt;3. The discovery layer&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The data-plane client supports &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;hybrid keyword and semantic search&lt;/a&gt; across discoverable records. The latest method name is &lt;a href="https://boto3.amazonaws.com/v1/documentation/api/latest/reference/services/agent-registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;search_discoverable_registry_records&lt;/a&gt;. Results expose normalized record fields and discoverable descriptors. They don’t expose the capability’s invocation credentials.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Searching approved, discoverable records across protocols.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = dp_client.search_discoverable_registry_records(
    registryIds=[REGISTRY_ARN],
    searchQuery=query,
    maxResults=3,
)
records = response.get("registryRecords", [])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the sample scenario, a return-and-refund query retrieves the Customer Support Agent because its description captures that intent. Treat this as a demo result, not a general claim that ranking improves merely because the catalog is larger.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-orchestrator"&gt;4. The orchestrator&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The orchestrator is a &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands agent&lt;/a&gt; deployed to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime&lt;/a&gt;. It exposes &lt;code&gt;discover_and_execute&lt;/code&gt;, which searches the Registry, parses each returned descriptor, creates the corresponding &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MCP&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://a2a-protocol.org/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;A2A&lt;/a&gt; client, and builds a request-scoped inner agent from only those discovered capabilities.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21108-3-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21108-3-1.png" alt="The orchestrator’s per-request flow, where an outer agent searches the Registry and assembles a request-scoped inner agent from the results" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The orchestrator’s per-request flow. The outer agent translates the user request into a Registry search. The inner agent is assembled from the search results and torn down at the end of the turn&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The core of &lt;code&gt;discover_and_execute&lt;/code&gt; fits in a single code block:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;The orchestrator’s discover/instantiate/execute loop.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-code"&gt;​​​@tool 
 def discover_and_execute(request: str) -&amp;gt; str: 
     records = dp_client.search_discoverable_registry_records( 
         registryIds=[REGISTRY_ARN], 
         searchQuery=request, 
         maxResults=5, 
     ).get("registryRecords", []) 
  
    # Registry returns descriptors, not invocation credentials. 
     oauth_token = fetch_oauth_token_from_config() 
     mcp_clients, a2a_tools = [], [] 
     for record in records: 
         metadata = parse_server_metadata(record) 
         if metadata["protocol"] == "MCP": 
             mcp_clients.append( 
                 create_mcp_client_from_metadata(metadata, oauth_token) 
             ) 
         elif metadata["protocol"] == "A2A": 
             a2a_tools.append( 
                 create_a2a_tool_from_metadata(metadata, session, REGION) 
             ) 
  
    return run_request_scoped_agent(request, mcp_clients, a2a_tools)​​ 

​​model = BedrockModel(model_id=MODEL_ID, region_name=REGION) 
​    started_clients = [] 
​    try: 
​        mcp_tools = [] 
​        for c in mcp_clients: 
​            c.start() 
​            started_clients.append(c) 
​            mcp_tools.extend(c.list_tools_sync()) 

​ 	    ##agent compiled in realtime within agent 
​        sub_agent = Agent( 
​            model=model, 
​            tools=mcp_tools + a2a_tools, 
​            system_prompt=( 
​                "You are an Order Management &amp;amp; Customer Service assistant. " 
​                "Use the RIGHT tool for each request:\\n" 
​                "- Order lookups, status, cancellations, address changes: use MCP tools\\n" 
​                "- Pricing, discounts, promo codes: use the pricing_agent tool\\n" 
​                "- Returns, refunds, complaints, escalations: use the customer_support_agent tool\\n" 
​                "Always use the most specific tool for the task." 
​            ), 
​        ) 
​        return str(sub_agent(request)) 
​    finally: 
​        for c in started_clients: 
​            try: 
​                c.stop() 
​            except Exception: 
​                pass​ &lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The sample fans out several search queries to improve recall for its order-management domain, deduplicates records, and tears down MCP clients after the request. That fan-out is a sample-specific orchestration choice, not a Registry requirement. Authentication remains outside discovery: the Gateway OAuth token is obtained from &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cognito&lt;/a&gt; configuration and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/secretsmanager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Secrets Manager&lt;/a&gt;, whereas A2A runtime calls are signed through the &lt;a href="https://boto3.amazonaws.com/v1/documentation/api/latest/reference/services/bedrock-agentcore-control.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;boto3&lt;/a&gt; session.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="running-the-three-demos"&gt;5. Running the three demos&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each demo exercises a different shape of the discovery loop.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="demo-1-order-status-mcp-only"&gt;Demo 1: Order status (MCP only)&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;A request that resolves to a single MCP tool call.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;result = invoke_orchestrator(
    "What is the current status and tracking info for order 123?",
    agentcore_client, orchestrator_arn,
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Registry returns the order management MCP server. The orchestrator calls &lt;code&gt;get_order_status&lt;/code&gt; through Gateway, the LLM synthesizes a human-readable response, and the inner agent terminates. No A2A involvement on this turn.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="demo-2-pricing-and-discounts-mcp-a2a"&gt;Demo 2: Pricing and discounts (MCP + A2A)&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;A request that resolves to a multi-protocol chain.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;result = invoke_orchestrator(
    "Order 123 has 2x Widget Pro at $99.98. What discount tiers or "
    "promo codes can reduce the price?",
    agentcore_client, orchestrator_arn,
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Registry returns the order management MCP server and the pricing A2A agent: two protocols, one natural-language query. The LLM calls &lt;code&gt;get_order_status&lt;/code&gt; to fetch line items, then calls the pricing agent on runtime for discount analysis, then synthesizes a single recommendation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="demo-3-return-and-refund-semantic-match"&gt;Demo 3: Return and refund (semantic match)&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;A request whose intent is not literally present in any tool name.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;result = invoke_orchestrator(
    "I want to return order 789 (Premium Headphones, delivered "
    "March 10). Am I eligible for a return and what is the refund "
    "amount?",
    agentcore_client, orchestrator_arn,
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This demo shows the value of semantic discovery. The phrase ‘return refund’ doesn’t appear in the Customer Support Agent’s registered name. The hybrid search ranks it highly because its description captures the intent. Without Registry, the orchestrator would need hardcoded knowledge of which agent handles returns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="observability"&gt;6. Observability&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Instrument the discovery and invocation path using Amazon Bedrock &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Observability&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudWatch/latest/monitoring/WhatIsCloudWatch.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; where appropriate. Useful signals include Registry search success and failure, selected record identifiers, client-construction errors, downstream invocation outcomes, end-to-end trace correlation, and application errors. The sample grants Amazon CloudWatch Logs permissions, but it doesn’t create custom metrics or dashboards. Production teams should define the traces, logs, and alerts required for their operating model. Production deployments should also apply &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; as a standard control on agent responses.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-teams-gain-from-governed-runtime-discovery"&gt;What teams gain from governed runtime discovery&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the discovery loop is in place, several capabilities that were previously coupled to orchestrator releases become independent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Decoupled publishing. Teams can deploy and register their own MCP servers, agents, and skills. Approval policy determines when a published record becomes available to consumers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;No orchestrator code change for newly approved records. In this pattern, adding a compatible, approved Registry record doesn’t require adding a tool-specific import or endpoint to the orchestrator. Changes to protocols, authentication setup, or orchestration policy can still require code or configuration updates.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Governed discovery. With manual approval, only reviewed and approved records become discoverable. IAM permissions can separate record publishers from approvers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Separated authentication. Registry authorization can use IAM or custom JWT. Capability invocation authentication is separate: the sample uses Cognito OAuth for Gateway and Boto3/IAM signing for AgentCore runtime. Search results don’t return those credentials.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Catalog search instead of prompt stuffing. With hybrid Registry search, the orchestrator retrieves a small candidate set instead of placing the full catalog in the model context. Search quality depends on descriptions, descriptor quality, query design, and evaluation.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="when-not-to-use-runtime-discovery"&gt;When not to use runtime discovery&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Runtime discovery is not necessary for every workload. Two patterns can still favor explicit build-time wiring:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Small, stable catalogs. If the complete capability set is small and changes rarely, explicit build-time wiring might be simpler and avoid an additional discovery request.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Fixed pipelines. When the same capabilities always run in a fixed order, implementing them as a deterministic flow is more straightforward and keeps tracing focused.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Discovery of assets from Registry at runtime makes the orchestrator less dependent on a fixed catalog. Against an AWS Agent Registry, it can search approved capabilities with hybrid semantic and keyword search, parse MCP or A2A descriptors, and instantiate request-scoped clients. Governance determines what is discoverable. Authentication remains an explicit concern for the Registry and each invoked capability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Agent Registry is &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/aws-agent-registry-generally-available" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;generally available&lt;/a&gt; with capabilities that extend beyond this single-account sample:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/organizations/latest/userguide/orgs_introduction.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Organization-wide discovery&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/ram/latest/userguide/what-is.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;cross-account sharing&lt;/a&gt; can expose governed records across account boundaries through supported &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/organizations/latest/userguide/orgs_introduction.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Organizations&lt;/a&gt; and sharing mechanisms.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Automatic capability synchronization&lt;/a&gt; can populate supported descriptors from a URL. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Credential-provider configuration&lt;/a&gt; on that synchronization request is used to fetch descriptor content. It isn’t returned to runtime consumers as invocation authentication.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Approval workflows can use Registry status APIs and IAM permissions to connect CI checks, security review, or human approval to the &lt;code&gt;DRAFT&lt;/code&gt; → &lt;code&gt;PENDING_APPROVAL&lt;/code&gt; → &lt;code&gt;APPROVED&lt;/code&gt; lifecycle.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Tagging, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSCloudFormation/latest/UserGuide/Welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudFormation&lt;/a&gt; support, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSCloudFormation/latest/UserGuide/Welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;infrastructure-as-code&lt;/a&gt; patterns can make Registry deployment and governance repeatable. Evaluation should verify search relevance and invocation behavior for the organization’s own catalog.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Run the &lt;a href="https://github.com/ach1ntya/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/discovery-and-invocation-at-runtime" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;runtime-discovery sample&lt;/a&gt;, then register one compatible MCP server or agent of your own. Confirm that its descriptor, approval state, authentication path, and search behavior match your production requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="learn-more"&gt;Learn more&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/aws-agent-registry-generally-available" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry general availability announcement&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry documentation&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/home" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore console (Registry)&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/manage-agents-tools-and-skills-at-scale-with-aws-agent-registry/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry general availability technical blog post&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Securing AI agent identity with X.509 PKI on AWS</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/securing-ai-agent-identity-with-x-509-pki-on-aws/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Deepakkumar Sharma]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 20 Oct 2026 16:03:48 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">05547fd2bd22554636c30cf4bb67047887b0d94a</guid>

					<description>Learn how to secure multi-agent AI systems with cryptographic identity using Keyfactor EJBCA and AWS services. This reference architecture combines signed request envelopes, user-signed delegation assertions, RFC 8693 audience-scoped tokens, and real-time OCSP revocation to prove agent identity and prevent request forgery across agent-to-agent and MCP tool calls.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;When AI agents access databases, APIs, and sensitive business operations, establishing cryptographic identity through X.509 public key infrastructure (PKI) becomes a critical requirement. The core challenge is proving which agent made a request, preventing forgery between agents, and confirming that each agent acts only on behalf of an authorized user. Traditional approaches such as sharing API keys between components or passing OAuth tokens through agent chains fall short of the zero-trust requirements that enterprise deployments demand.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Multi-agent AI frameworks increasingly use the Model Context Protocol (MCP) to connect agents to external tools and data sources. MCP defines how a large language model (LLM)-powered agent invokes tools such as database queries or email operations. However, MCP has no built-in authentication standard. It is transport-agnostic by design. This creates a critical gap: Without proper identity mechanisms, any component could impersonate an agent, replay intercepted credentials, or invoke tools without authorization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show you how to address this gap using Keyfactor EJBCA combined with AWS services. EJBCA is a PKI platform that supports high-availability deployment, REST API automation, and Online Certificate Status Protocol (OCSP) validation. The reference architecture uses four complementary security mechanisms. First, signed request envelopes provide cryptographic proof-of-possession on every agent call. Second, user-signed delegation assertions authorize agents without sharing credentials. Third, RFC 8693 audience-scoped tokens limit the scope of exposure from any single token disclosure. Fourth, real-time OCSP revocation checking propagates credential changes immediately across all agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; for LLM inference through the &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents/sdk-python" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt;. While this reference architecture uses a database as the protected resource, the same authentication pattern applies to any MCP-compatible tool server. Complete source code for all examples is available in the accompanying GitHub repository.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="about-keyfactor-ejbca"&gt;About Keyfactor EJBCA&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keyfactor provides a widely adopted machine identity management platform used by more than 1,500 enterprise customers worldwide, according to Keyfactor. These organizations span financial services, healthcare, manufacturing, and government. Keyfactor helps organizations establish and maintain cryptographic trust through certificate lifecycle management, PKI operations, and machine identity governance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;EJBCA Enterprise is Keyfactor’s Certificate Authority (CA) and PKI platform, designed for high-availability deployment and REST API automation. Available as an enterprise product, EJBCA provides X.509 certificate issuance, OCSP validation, certificate revocation list (CRL) distribution, and full certificate lifecycle management through both a web console and REST API. EJBCA supports deployment on-premises, in containers, or on Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The partnership between AWS and Keyfactor brings together AWS managed services (Amazon Bedrock, AWS Secrets Manager, Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS)) with PKI infrastructure that supports certificate lifecycle management, automated enrollment, and real-time revocation. This combination addresses a gap in the AI agent ecosystem. While AWS provides the compute, storage, and AI inference layers, Keyfactor provides the cryptographic identity layer that binds agents to verifiable, revocable, short-lived credentials. This joint reference architecture, developed collaboratively by the AWS and Keyfactor engineering teams, demonstrates how to integrate these technologies. You can adapt the patterns shown here to your own multi-agent deployments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture implements three authentication layers, each addressing a different trust boundary in the agent communication chain. These three layers use the four security mechanisms introduced earlier: signed envelopes for proof-of-possession, delegation assertions for credential-free authorization, audience-scoped tokens for exposure limitation, and OCSP for real-time revocation. The mechanisms combine across layers as shown in the following table.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Layer&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trust boundary&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Mechanism&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Validates&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1. User Auth&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;User to System&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;OAuth + X.509 cert + delegation minting&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;User identity, credential binding&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2. Agent-to-Agent&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent to Agent&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Signed envelopes (PoP + nonce + timestamp)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Caller identity, request freshness, no replay&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3. MCP Auth&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent to Tool Server&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Scoped token + envelope + delegation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;All identities, authorization chain, OCSP&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows the three authentication layers and how credentials flow between the user, agents, and the MCP tool server. The diagram illustrates the separation between user credentials (which stay in the login flow), agent credentials (scoped per agent), and the delegation chain that connects them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/16/ML-21717-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/16/ML-21717-1.png" alt="Three-layer PKI authentication architecture showing credential flow between the user, agents, and the MCP tool server" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Three-layer PKI authentication architecture for AI agent systems on AWS&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 2 illustrates the detailed traffic flow during a typical user session. It shows the sequence of authentication steps from login through certificate issuance, agent delegation, tool invocation, and session cleanup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 730px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/16/ML-21717-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/16/ML-21717-2.png" alt="Authentication traffic flow from login through certificate issuance, agent delegation, tool invocation, and session cleanup" width="720"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Authentication traffic flow from user login through agent delegation, tool invocation, and session cleanup&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="aws-services-used"&gt;AWS services used&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following AWS services form the foundation of the reference architecture.&lt;/p&gt;

 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;LLM inference through Strands Agents SDK&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon RDS for MySQL&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Application database accessed through MCP tools&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Simple Email Service (Amazon SES)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Email delivery for the Email Agent&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt;
