&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-walkthrough"&gt;Solution walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following steps walk through each component of the architecture, from initial user login through to session cleanup. Each step builds on the previous one, establishing a chain of trust that connects the human user to the final tool execution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An active AWS account with access to Amazon Bedrock, Amazon RDS for MySQL, Amazon SES, and AWS Secrets Manager.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Keycloak 26.2 or later (required for RFC 8693 standard token exchange. Previous versions use a legacy exchange that is incompatible).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Keyfactor EJBCA Enterprise with REST API V1 and an OCSP responder configured.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;EJBCA API administrator certificate (&lt;code&gt;api-admin.p12&lt;/code&gt;) for REST API authentication.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.9+ development environment.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Familiarity with X.509 certificates, OCSP, OAuth 2.0, RSA digital signatures, and PKCS#12 key stores.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-user-login-certificate-issuance-and-delegation-minting"&gt;Step 1: User login, certificate issuance, and delegation minting&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The login flow establishes the user’s identity through three mechanisms and then delegates authority to the AI agents without sharing the user’s credentials. This separation is critical: Agents receive proof of authorization without ever holding the user’s private key or full-session OAuth token.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First, the application authenticates the user against Keycloak using an OAuth 2.0 password grant, receiving a full-session access token and refresh token. Immediately after, the application performs an RFC 8693 token exchange to obtain a downscoped token. This token’s audience is restricted to the MCP server. Only this limited token is handed to agents. If it’s inadvertently exposed, it can’t access any other service in the organization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Second, the application generates an RSA 2048+ key pair locally and submits a Certificate Signing Request (CSR) to EJBCA through the &lt;code&gt;pkcs10enroll&lt;/code&gt; REST API endpoint. EJBCA signs the CSR and returns the certificate. The application bundles the certificate and private key into a PKCS12 container and stores it in AWS Secrets Manager. The private key never leaves the application boundary. It is not sent to EJBCA, not shared with agents, and not stored on the filesystem. Three agent certificates are issued using the same CSR-based flow, one per agent role.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Third, the user’s private key signs a delegation assertion. This is a JSON document listing the SHA-256 fingerprints of all three agent certificates, along with the user’s subject name, issuance time, and expiry. This delegation proves that the user authorized these specific agents to act on their behalf. Agents carry this delegation instead of the user’s certificate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# RFC 8693 Token Exchange - downscope to MCP audience
downscoped_token = keycloak.token_exchange(
    original_token, audience="mysql-mcp-server", scope="database:write"
)
# Client-side key generation (user key never leaves this flow)
private_key = rsa.generate_private_key(public_exponent=65537, key_size=2048)
csr = build_csr(private_key, f"CN={username},E={username}@org.com")
user_cert = ejbca.pkcs10enroll(csr, ca_name="UserCA")
# User signs delegation over agent cert fingerprints
delegation = mint_delegation(
    username=username,
    user_cert_pem=user_cert,
    user_key_pem=private_key_pem,
    agent_cert_pems=[orch_cert, mysql_cert, email_cert]
)
# Result: {sub, agent_fingerprints[], iat, exp, user_cert_pem, signature_b64}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-agent-to-agent-signed-envelopes"&gt;Step 2: Agent-to-agent signed envelopes&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the orchestrator agent routes a request to a sub-agent (such as the MySQL agent or Email agent), it does not simply pass its certificate for identification. Instead, it signs the entire request payload, including the tool name, arguments, a timestamp, and a cryptographically random nonce, using its private key with &lt;code&gt;RSA-PSS-SHA256&lt;/code&gt;. The resulting signed envelope provides proof-of-possession. The receiving agent can verify that the caller actually holds the private key corresponding to the presented certificate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The receiving agent performs six validation checks on the envelope before processing the request:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Signature verification:&lt;/strong&gt; Proves the sender possesses the private key (proof-of-possession).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Timestamp freshness:&lt;/strong&gt; The envelope must be within &lt;code&gt;ENVELOPE_MAX_AGE_SECONDS&lt;/code&gt; (default 60 seconds) of the current time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Nonce uniqueness:&lt;/strong&gt; The &lt;code&gt;ReplayCache&lt;/code&gt; rejects any previously seen nonce, preventing request replay.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CA chain validation:&lt;/strong&gt; The sender’s certificate must chain to the trusted CA (&lt;code&gt;UserCA.pem&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Role authorization:&lt;/strong&gt; The certificate CN’s role suffix must be in &lt;code&gt;ALLOWED_CALLER_ROLES&lt;/code&gt; for this agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;OCSP revocation check:&lt;/strong&gt; A real-time check against EJBCA confirms the certificate has not been revoked.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the orchestrator needs to re-send the same prompt (for example, after a transient failure), it must create a new envelope with a fresh nonce. Old envelopes can’t be replayed, even by the legitimate sender.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Orchestrator signs request to sub-agent
envelope = sign_envelope(
    payload={"tool": "query_database", "args": {"sql": "SELECT ..."}},
    cert_pem=orchestrator_cert_pem,
    private_key_pem=orchestrator_key_pem
)
# envelope: {payload_b64, cert_pem, timestamp, nonce, signature_b64, alg}
# Sub-agent verifies (six checks)
verified_payload = verify_envelope(
    envelope,
    allowed_role="orchestrator-agent",
    ca_cert_pem=ca_pem,
    replay_cache=cache
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-mcp-server-authentication"&gt;Step 3: MCP server authentication&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The MCP server (FastMCP) is the final trust boundary before any database operation runs. It performs four validation checks on every tool invocation, implementing defense-in-depth. Even if one credential is subject to unauthorized access, the remaining checks prevent unauthorized operations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The authentication sequence:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Token introspection:&lt;/strong&gt; A POST to the Keycloak &lt;code&gt;/token/introspect&lt;/code&gt; endpoint verifies the token is active and its audience contains “mcp-server”. A full-session token without the correct audience is rejected.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Envelope verification:&lt;/strong&gt; The agent’s signed envelope is validated for signature correctness, timestamp freshness, nonce uniqueness, CA chain trust, role match (&lt;code&gt;mysql-agent&lt;/code&gt;), and OCSP non-revocation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Delegation verification:&lt;/strong&gt; The user’s delegation assertion is validated. The signature proves the user’s key holder authorized this delegation, the calling agent’s certificate fingerprint appears in the authorized list, and the user’s certificate is checked through OCSP.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Identity cross-check:&lt;/strong&gt; The token username, the delegation subject, and the agent certificate CN prefix must all match. This confirms a single consistent identity chain from user through to tool execution.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# MCP Server - Four validation checks
# 1. Token: must be active with correct audience
info = introspect(token)
assert info["active"] and "mcp-server" in info["aud"]
# 2. Envelope: proof-of-possession + freshness + replay + CA + role + OCSP
payload = verify_envelope(envelope, "mysql-agent", ca_pem, replay_cache)
# 3. Delegation: user authorized this agent + user cert not revoked
claims = verify_delegation(delegation, agent_cert_pem, ca_pem)
# 4. Cross-check: consistent identity chain
assert token_username == claims["sub"] == agent_cn.rsplit("_", 1)[0]
# All checks passed - execute tool
result = execute_sql(payload["args"]["sql"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-replay-prevention"&gt;Step 4: Replay prevention&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every signed envelope carries a unique nonce, a 128-bit cryptographically random hex string generated with Python’s secrets module. The &lt;code&gt;ReplayCache&lt;/code&gt; is a thread-safe in-memory store that remembers every nonce until its freshness window expires (2x &lt;code&gt;ENVELOPE_MAX_AGE_SECONDS&lt;/code&gt;). If the same nonce appears in a second request, the envelope is immediately rejected, even if its signature is valid and the timestamp is fresh.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The timestamp validation complements the nonce check. An envelope’s timestamp must be within &lt;code&gt;ENVELOPE_MAX_AGE_SECONDS&lt;/code&gt; (default 60 seconds) of the server’s current time. Together, these mechanisms address four unauthorized access scenarios:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recorded replay:&lt;/strong&gt; An unauthorized user captures a valid envelope and resubmits it later. The nonce was already seen.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Delayed delivery:&lt;/strong&gt; An envelope intercepted in transit arrives after the freshness window. The timestamp check fails.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Stale request reuse:&lt;/strong&gt; An old envelope cached by a proxy is resubmitted. Both nonce and timestamp checks fail.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Precomputed forgery:&lt;/strong&gt; An unauthorized user cannot predict future nonces to pre-sign envelopes.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-client-side-key-generation"&gt;Step 5: Client-side key generation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A critical security property of this architecture is that private keys are generated locally and never transmitted to any external system. The application generates RSA 2048+ key pairs using the Python cryptography library. It constructs a CSR containing only the public key and subject information, and sends only the CSR to EJBCA through the &lt;code&gt;pkcs10enroll&lt;/code&gt; endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;EJBCA validates the CSR, applies the certificate profile (7-day validity, key usage constraints), signs it with the CA key, and returns the issued certificate. The application then bundles the locally held private key with the returned certificate into a PKCS12 container and stores it in AWS Secrets Manager.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This design provides several security properties:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The user’s private key is used only at login to sign the delegation assertion. It’s never shared with agents.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Each agent’s private key is used only by that agent to sign envelopes. It’s never shared between agents.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;EJBCA never sees or stores private keys. It operates purely as a certificate authority.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If Secrets Manager is subject to unauthorized access, the PKCS12 password (stored separately) adds an additional layer.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Key material can be rotated independently by re-enrolling with a new CSR at next login.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-6-complete-logout-and-credential-revocation"&gt;Step 6: Complete logout and credential revocation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Logout triggers a revocation cascade that immediately invalidates all outstanding credentials. The sequence results in no remaining valid credentials after completion:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Revoke user certificate in EJBCA:&lt;/strong&gt; This immediately invalidates all outstanding delegations, because delegation verification checks the user’s certificate through OCSP in real time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Revoke all three agent certificates:&lt;/strong&gt; This invalidates all outstanding signed envelopes, since envelope verification includes OCSP.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Delete all secrets from AWS Secrets Manager:&lt;/strong&gt; Using &lt;code&gt;ForceDeleteWithoutRecovery&lt;/code&gt; for immediate removal with no recovery window.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Terminate Keycloak session:&lt;/strong&gt; Invalidates the downscoped token through the session termination endpoint.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The revocation cascade is the key security property. Revoking a single user certificate at the PKI layer promptly revokes all downstream agent authority without needing to individually revoke tokens, delete sessions, or contact each agent. OCSP provides the propagation mechanism, and because every request checks OCSP, the effect is immediate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-considerations"&gt;Security considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The design choices in this architecture address several security properties that are important for production multi-agent deployments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Private key isolation. The user’s private key exists only during the login flow and is used solely to sign the delegation. Agent keys are scoped per-agent and never shared. No key material crosses trust boundaries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Delegation compared to credential sharing. Agents prove their authority through a cryptographic chain (delegation to agent fingerprint to envelope signature) without ever holding user credentials. If an agent key is exposed, only that agent’s key is revealed, not the user’s.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Audience-scoped tokens. RFC 8693 token exchange produces a token accepted only by the MCP server. An inadvertently disclosed downscoped token cannot authenticate against Keycloak admin APIs, other services, or even a different MCP server instance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Replay prevention. The combination of per-request nonces and a 60-second freshness window confirms that every request is single-use and time-bound. The &lt;code&gt;ReplayCache&lt;/code&gt; is thread-safe and self-cleaning.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Fail-closed design. Every validation failure results in immediate denial. OCSP unreachable: deny. Invalid signature: deny. Expired timestamp: deny. Replayed nonce: deny. Wrong audience: deny. There’s no graceful degradation for security checks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Revocation cascade. Revoking one user certificate promptly invalidates all delegations, all agent authority, and all outstanding envelopes. This is propagated through real-time OCSP without requiring individual credential cleanup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Forward-only trust chain. The &lt;code&gt;ALLOWED_CALLER_ROLES&lt;/code&gt; map enforces that only &lt;code&gt;orchestrator-agent&lt;/code&gt; can call &lt;code&gt;mysql-agent&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;email-agent&lt;/code&gt;. Sub-agents cannot call each other, can’t call upward to the orchestrator, and cannot impersonate the user.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="ai-governance-considerations"&gt;AI governance considerations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The PKI architecture provides built-in behavior boundaries for the AI agents in this system. Production deployments should use Amazon Bedrock Guardrails to enforce content filtering, topic avoidance, and grounding checks on agent responses, alongside the PKI-based access controls described in this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool boundaries&lt;/strong&gt;: The AI agent operates under strict tool constraints. It can only invoke pre-defined MCP tools (&lt;code&gt;execute_sql&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;describe_table&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;list_tables&lt;/code&gt;). The agent can’t execute arbitrary code or access resources outside its certificate scope.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Human oversight&lt;/strong&gt;: All agent actions are logged and auditable. AWS CloudTrail captures AWS API calls, and the MCP server logs every validated tool invocation with the full identity chain (user, agent, timestamp, and operation).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data handling&lt;/strong&gt;: User queries and results stay within the authenticated session boundary. The audience-scoped token confines data access to the specific MCP server instance.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Certificate-based behavior boundaries&lt;/strong&gt;: Agents can only invoke tools they hold valid certificates for. An agent without a &lt;code&gt;mysql-agent&lt;/code&gt; certificate cannot access the database, regardless of what the LLM might generate as instructions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="extending-the-architecture"&gt;Extending the architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can extend this architecture to support additional agent types, horizontal scaling, and multi-tenant isolation. The following paragraphs describe the most common extension points.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To add new agent types, you only need to add an entry in &lt;code&gt;ALLOWED_CALLER_ROLES&lt;/code&gt;. Certificates are automatically issued at login, and the delegation assertion dynamically includes the new agent’s fingerprint. No infrastructure changes are required.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For horizontal scaling, you can deploy EJBCA’s OCSP responder in high-availability mode behind a load balancer. AWS Secrets Manager supports high request rates suitable for this workload. The &lt;code&gt;ReplayCache&lt;/code&gt; can be backed by Amazon ElastiCache for distributed deployments.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To harden the architecture for production, you can use hardware security module (HSM)-backed EJBCA signing keys through AWS CloudHSM or an on-premises HSM. OCSP response stapling reduces validation latency, and Certificate Transparency logs provide a full audit trail of issued certificates.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For multi-tenant deployments, you can create per-tenant Certificate Authorities or per-tenant delegation authorities for cryptographic tenant isolation. Combined with per-tenant AWS accounts, this delivers strong isolation between tenants.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You can also complement PKI-based agent identity with AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles. Use PKI for agent-to-agent authentication and IAM for AWS service authorization. The two systems work independently, providing defense in depth.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, remove the resources created during this walkthrough:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Amazon RDS for MySQL instance (or the AWS CloudFormation stack if you used the scripted setup).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete all secrets from AWS Secrets Manager, including user certificates and agent certificates under the &lt;code&gt;mcp/&lt;/code&gt; prefix.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove the verified sender email address from Amazon SES.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Terminate the Amazon EC2 instances running Keycloak and EJBCA (if deployed for this demonstration).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Revoke and delete all test certificates in EJBCA by using the EJBCA admin console or REST API.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove the Keycloak realm (&lt;code&gt;mcp-realm&lt;/code&gt;) and associated clients.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If you used the scripted setup, run &lt;code&gt;./scripts/cleanup.sh&lt;/code&gt; (Linux) or &lt;code&gt;./scripts/cleanup.bat&lt;/code&gt; (Windows) to automate these steps.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we demonstrated a reference architecture for securing AI agent communications using X.509 PKI with Keyfactor EJBCA and AWS services. The architecture addresses the fundamental identity gap in multi-agent AI systems through four complementary mechanisms:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Signed request envelopes provide cryptographic proof-of-possession on every inter-agent call, making it significantly harder for an unauthorized party to forge an agent’s identity or replay its requests.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;User-signed delegation assertions decouple authorization from credential sharing. Agents prove they are authorized to act on the user’s behalf without holding the user’s private key or full-session token.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;RFC 8693 audience-scoped tokens limit the scope of exposure from any token disclosure to a single service boundary.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Real-time OCSP revocation checking confirms that credential revocation takes immediate effect across the entire system. Revoking one user certificate promptly ends all downstream agent authority.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This architecture is framework-agnostic. While we demonstrated it with the AWS Strands Agents SDK and FastMCP, the same PKI patterns apply to LangChain, CrewAI, AutoGen, or any other multi-agent framework that uses tool protocols. As AI agents proliferate and gain access to increasingly sensitive operations, agent identity infrastructure moves from optional to essential.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started with this reference architecture, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-Securing-AI-agent-identity-with-X-509-PKI-on-AWS" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;. For more information about the services used, see the following resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock User Guide&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.keyfactor.com/products/ejbca-enterprise/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Keyfactor EJBCA Enterprise&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MCP Specification&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents/sdk-python" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/opensource/introducing-strands-agents-an-open-source-ai-agents-sdk/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Introducing Strands Agents, an Open Source AI Agents SDK (AWS Open Source blog post)&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Eon built multi-cloud investigation with an MCP Gateway on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-eon-built-multi-cloud-investigation-with-an-mcp-gateway-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Dor Shaboy]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 20 Oct 2026 15:57:57 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">d3c3a7c789540007b485ac6f7137782fb5e6c556</guid>

					<description>Learn how Eon replaced per-tool credentials and local MCP processes across AWS, GCP, and Azure with a single authenticated Amazon Bedrock AgentCore gateway, giving more than 100 developers and autonomous agents unified access to production investigation and cutting new-engineer onboarding from two days to two minutes.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This is a guest post co-written with Roei Zavida from Eon.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Independent software vendors (ISVs) building AI-powered products need to connect agents and humans to dozens of operational tools across clouds, each with different authentication models, network boundaries, and access policies. When those tools span AWS, GCP, and Azure simultaneously, the integration surface multiplies.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post describes how Eon used Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. Eon used it to replace per-tool credentials and local Model Context Protocol (MCP) processes with a single authenticated gateway. The result gives developers and autonomous agents unified access to investigation tooling across three clouds. New engineers now reach all their investigation tools in about two minutes instead of the two days it previously took to configure credentials and local MCP servers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="about-eon"&gt;About Eon&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Eon provides the Autonomous Data Foundation for the AI era. The platform discovers and classifies cloud resources across AWS, Azure, and GCP. It provides continuous deduplicated backup that reduces storage costs by up to 50 percent in Eon’s internal measurements. It also detects ransomware and rogue-agent damage and delivers live grounded context for AI agents. All of this runs from a single read-only AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role, storing data in Apache Parquet and Apache Iceberg formats. Founded by former AWS engineers, Eon runs its software as a service (SaaS) infrastructure on AWS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge"&gt;The challenge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Eon’s internal engineering team investigates production incidents across multiple clouds and dozens of operational systems. A crashed Kubernetes pod requires checking container logs, Grafana metrics, the deployment’s GitHub pull request (PR), and cloud-provider health status within the same session.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Eon needed to address several key requirements:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Credential consolidation&lt;/strong&gt; — Each new tool added another integration to configure, secure, and rotate, which got harder to keep consistent as the toolset grew.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Eliminate local MCP processes&lt;/strong&gt; — Each developer ran several MCP servers locally, which was hard to standardize and keep current.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Identity separation&lt;/strong&gt; — Engineers accessed each system with role-based permissions, but there was no single place to decide which tools each person or agent could call.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cross-account access without networking overhead&lt;/strong&gt; — Hosting MCP servers across multiple AWS accounts required virtual private cloud (VPC) peering or AWS PrivateLink per account, multiplying operational cost and setup time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Developer onboarding&lt;/strong&gt; — New engineers spent roughly 2 days configuring credentials and local MCP servers before investigating their first incident.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent access&lt;/strong&gt; — Eon’s autonomous site reliability engineering (SRE) agent (running Claude on Amazon Bedrock) needed the same tool access as humans, but with different permissions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Growing engineering headcount combined with a fast-expanding set of AI tools and MCPs made maintaining credentials and local MCP servers increasingly costly. This pushed Eon toward a centralized solution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="approaches-considered"&gt;Approaches considered&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before building on AgentCore, Eon evaluated alternatives:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="option-1-centralized-proxy-with-custom-auth"&gt;Option 1: Centralized proxy with custom auth&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every harness had its own proxy configuration with all the targets. A single reverse proxy aggregating all tools, with a custom authentication layer. This handled credential consolidation but only supported remote MCP and required building and maintaining tool protocol translation, session management, and authorization logic from scratch. The implementation and maintenance burden was significant.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="option-2-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-gateway"&gt;Option 2: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, is a managed gateway with built-in identity federation, Cedar-based authorization policies, and Runtime hosting that converts local MCP servers to remote HTTP endpoints. This addressed all requirements without custom infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Eon chose Option 2 because it provided identity separation, cross-account connectivity, and policy enforcement as managed capabilities rather than code they would need to build and maintain. These were the only two options on the table.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Eon deployed the full Amazon Bedrock AgentCore stack: AgentCore Gateway, Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, AgentCore Runtime, and AgentCore Identity, all capabilities of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. The following diagram shows how client requests flow through the authentication layer to a single gateway. The gateway routes authorized tool calls to targets hosted on AgentCore Runtimes across multiple AWS accounts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21741-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/14/ML-21741-1-1.png" alt="Eon’s centralized gateway architecture: clients and an SRE agent reach one AgentCore gateway that routes tool calls to Runtimes across multiple AWS accounts" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Eon’s centralized gateway architecture on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture has five layers:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="clients"&gt;Clients&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Developers using Claude Code, Cursor, or OpenAI Codex connect to a single MCP URL. An autonomous SRE agent running Claude on Amazon Bedrock connects to the same gateway with a different identity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="identity-plane"&gt;Identity plane&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Corporate single sign-on (SSO) federates through Amazon Cognito, which mints the JSON Web Token (JWT) and hands it to the gateway. Amazon API Gateway sits in front as the OAuth entry point for humans, proxying the MCP session. Agents authenticate with Cognito &lt;code&gt;client_credentials&lt;/code&gt; grants directly against the gateway.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="one-gateway-least-privilege-access"&gt;One gateway, least-privilege access&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A single AgentCore MCP Gateway serves as the company-wide front door. Cedar policies in ENFORCE mode control which principals can invoke which tools. Read operations are available to selected groups, not every authenticated user. Write operations are limited to low-impact collaboration actions, such as commenting on a PR or ticket, and production tools are read-only.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="policy-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore"&gt;Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cedar policies map each principal to permitted tools. Write operations are limited to low-impact collaboration actions, such as commenting on a PR or ticket, and production tools are read-only. The gateway evaluates every &lt;code&gt;tools/call&lt;/code&gt; request against Cedar before forwarding to the target.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="targets"&gt;Targets&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Dozens of target MCP servers exposing hundreds of tools on the one gateway, served by AgentCore Runtimes distributed across multiple AWS accounts. Each Runtime converts local stdio-based MCP servers into Streamable HTTP endpoints accessible through the gateway.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A developer opens their integrated development environment (IDE), configures one MCP URL, authenticates through corporate SSO, and the gateway aggregates hundreds of tools into a single &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt; response. No local setup and no per-tool tokens.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="identity-and-access-separation"&gt;Identity and access separation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The core security decision was enforcing read and write boundaries through Cedar policy at the gateway level.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Single gateway, policy-enforced separation:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway accepts both JWT tokens (humans through corporate SSO and Cognito) and &lt;code&gt;client_credentials&lt;/code&gt; tokens (agents). Cedar policies determine what each principal can do. Humans in approved groups get read access. Write operations require explicit Cedar permits granted only to specific agent identities.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cedar reinforces per-tool granularity:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;// Agent "sre-bot" can add comments on GitHub PRs
permit(
    principal == Agent::"sre-bot",
    action == Action::"tools/call",
    resource == Tool::"github_add_pr_comment"
);

// No human principal can invoke mutation tools
forbid(
    principal in Group::"humans",
    action == Action::"tools/call",
    resource in ResourceGroup::"write-tools"
);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cedar policies are managed as code through GitHub PRs with review comments, giving the team version control and audit trail on every permission change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Within the gateway, Cedar policies scope tool access by SSO group, and groups map to job role. Engineers have access to development and testing environments by default. Production access follows Eon’s security and compliance controls: it is read-only, granted just-in-time through an approval process, and audited. A new team member’s group is scoped the same way, by role rather than by which service they happen to own. Because every agent action is checked against Cedar in ENFORCE mode and scoped to a least-privilege tool set, the autonomous agent operates within safe, bounded limits. It can only invoke the specific tools its identity is explicitly permitted to call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Before, our internal agent couldn’t reach the same investigation tools developers used. After, it uses the same Gateway with its own identity and a tighter tool set.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Roei Zavida, Senior DevOps Engineer, Eon&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cross-account-connectivity"&gt;Cross-account connectivity&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Eon runs Runtimes across multiple AWS accounts, organized by environment or service boundary. AgentCore Gateway reaches each Runtime through IAM cross-account role assumption. The Runtime in Account B trusts the AgentCore Gateway execution role in Account A through a standard IAM trust policy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;What this eliminated:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;VPC peering configuration and route table management across multiple AWS accounts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;PrivateLink endpoint costs per account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Security group coordination between accounts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Days of setup per new account (replaced by one IAM trust policy, deployable in minutes).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Target attachment patterns:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Depending on each tool’s authentication and hosting model, Eon uses two primary attachment patterns. The following table summarizes when to use each pattern and gives an example.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pattern&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;When to use&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Example&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Runtime + IAM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tool needs a cloud hop through IAM credentials&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Grafana queries, Kubernetes API calls&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Runtime wrapping remote MCP&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tool surface needs filtering or credential translation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Linear&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Most targets use Runtime + IAM. Eon only wraps a remote MCP to shrink its catalog or slot in a credential the gateway cannot otherwise supply.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="investigation-session-flow"&gt;Investigation session flow&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following flow traces a developer investigating a production incident:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Authentication.&lt;/strong&gt; The developer opens Claude Code (or Cursor/Codex) and connects to the gateway MCP URL. The IDE initiates OAuth through corporate SSO. Cognito issues a JWT carrying the developer’s identity and group memberships.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool discovery.&lt;/strong&gt; The gateway aggregates `tools/list` from dozens of connected targets and returns a unified manifest of hundreds of tools to the IDE. The developer sees all available investigation tools in one list.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool invocation.&lt;/strong&gt; The developer asks: “Show me the error rate spike for the payments service in the last hour.” The IDE’s agent selects the Grafana query tool and sends a `tools/call` request.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Policy evaluation.&lt;/strong&gt; The gateway evaluates the request against Cedar. The developer’s group membership grants access to the Grafana tool. The request passes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Runtime execution.&lt;/strong&gt; The gateway forwards the request to the Runtime hosting the Grafana MCP server (running in a separate AWS account). The Runtime authenticates to Grafana using a service credential stored in the AgentCore Identity vault, executes the query, and returns results.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cross-tool correlation.&lt;/strong&gt; The agent follows up by checking the Kubernetes deployment status and the related GitHub PR, each routed to different Runtimes. The developer sees correlated results without switching tools or re-authenticating.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="lessons-learned-at-scale"&gt;Lessons learned at scale&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploying to 100+ developers and approximately 10,000 sessions surfaced production challenges:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="managing-cedar-policy-size"&gt;Managing Cedar policy size&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every tool schema that the gateway pre-indexes contributes to Cedar policy size. With dozens of targets exposing extensive tool manifests, Eon’s policy grew toward the size quota. The fix: DYNAMIC listing mode for high-tool-count targets. In DYNAMIC mode, the gateway forwards &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt; calls to the MCP server at request time instead of pre-indexing schemas, adding zero bytes to the policy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The trade-off is that semantic tool search is unavailable for DYNAMIC targets. Eon runs a hybrid configuration: DEFAULT mode for targets requiring per-user OAuth (smaller tool counts, need pre-indexed schemas for the consent flow) and DYNAMIC mode for large-catalog targets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="oauth-session-binding-for-standard-mcp-clients"&gt;OAuth session binding for standard MCP clients&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Claude Code, Cursor, and Codex expect the OAuth &lt;code&gt;state&lt;/code&gt; parameter for cross-site request forgery (CSRF) protection. AgentCore uses session-URI binding. Eon built an Amazon API Gateway shim that translates between the two protocols so standard MCP clients can complete the OAuth flow without modification.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="filtering-tool-surfaces"&gt;Filtering tool surfaces&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Some MCP servers expose hundreds of tools. Individual developers need only a subset for their service. Eon built a Runtime wrapper that filters the tool manifest at the Runtime layer, reducing noise in &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt; responses and preventing accidental invocation of irrelevant operations. This approach also keeps those targets on DEFAULT mode, preserving semantic search and embedded features that DYNAMIC mode sacrifices.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Before AgentCore&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;After AgentCore&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Credential sets per developer&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One per system/MCP server (GitHub, Grafana, cloud accounts, etc.)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0 (one SSO login)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Local MCP processes per machine&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;10&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Developer onboarding time&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2 days&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cross-account networking configs&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;VPC peering per account&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0 (IAM-only)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tool authorization&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Configured per tool&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One Cedar policy for humans and agents&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Developers on the platform&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;—&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;100+&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tools accessible from one URL&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;—&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Hundreds (across dozens of targets)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Sessions processed&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;—&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~10,000&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The single-gateway architecture has run in production for Eon’s internal engineering team for several months. The clearest outcome is developer onboarding. Setting up access to the full tool set dropped from roughly two days of configuring credentials and local MCP servers to about two minutes. A new engineer connects one gateway URL, authenticates through corporate SSO, and immediately sees the tools their role allows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The internal agent accounts for most of the time saved, because it gains access to more context through the gateway, improving investigation accuracy. Engineers save the time previously spent on AI tool setup, because they only need to connect to the gateway URL.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Eon is exploring how the same patterns can support AI capabilities in its own product.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“AgentCore gave us one authenticated MCP Gateway instead of per-tool tokens and local MCP processes. Developers and our internal agent now reach Kubernetes, Grafana, GitHub, and our cloud accounts from a single URL.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Roei Zavida, Senior DevOps Engineer, Eon&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post described how Eon replaced per-tool credentials and local MCP processes with a single Amazon Bedrock AgentCore MCP Gateway. The result is Cedar-based identity separation, cross-account connectivity without VPC peering, and unified tool access for more than 100 developers and autonomous agents across three clouds. The single-gateway architecture, Cedar least-privilege enforcement, and hybrid DEFAULT and DYNAMIC policy mode are reusable patterns for ISVs connecting agents and developers to tools across multiple clouds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about building on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, review these resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agents-tools-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Runtime documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://catalog.workshops.aws/agentcore-getting-started/en-US" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting Started with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Workshop&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

  &lt;h3 class="lb-h4"&gt;Dor Shaboy&lt;/h3&gt; 
  &lt;p style="overflow: hidden"&gt;Dor is a Solutions Architect at AWS with over a decade of experience in cloud architecture and security engineering. Dor works closely with ISVs, helping them scale and adopt AWS services. For the past year, he has been partnering with Eon on their AI-powered investigation platform built on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Outside of work, Dor enjoys hiking and tennis.&lt;/p&gt;
