		<item>
		<title>Developers can now access SageMaker Studio through the new AWS experience</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/developers-can-now-access-sagemaker-studio-through-the-new-aws-experience/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Hazim Qudah]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 19 Oct 2026 22:39:24 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker Studio]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">d50fc3895579c09e9db14324517982a638f3877e</guid>

					<description>New Amazon SageMaker AI customers can now land in a fully configured SageMaker Studio environment in only a few steps. Sign up with an identity you already have, such as Amazon, Apple, GitHub, or Google, with no advanced AWS account or credit card required for most customers, plus $100 in AWS Free Tier credits.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Today, we’re excited to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/10/sagemaker-studio-social-sign-in/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;announce&lt;/a&gt; that new Amazon SageMaker AI customers can now land inside a fully configured Amazon SageMaker Studio environment in only a few steps after signing up for AWS. You can use a sign-in method that you already have, such as Amazon, Apple, GitHub, Google, or an email address. You don’t need to set up an advanced AWS account, enter a credit card (for most customers), or configure an environment manually. New customers also receive $100 in AWS Free Tier credits that work across SageMaker AI, including model customization, training jobs, Studio notebooks, and endpoint deployments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post follows on from our previous post, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/from-hugging-face-to-amazon-sagemaker-studio-in-one-click-2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;From Hugging Face to Amazon SageMaker Studio in One Click&lt;/a&gt;, which introduced deep links from Hugging Face model pages directly into SageMaker Studio workflows. Earlier this year, we also sped up Studio environment creation time from 2 minutes to about 20 seconds and pre-configured model customization permissions in every new Studio domain. Together, these changes removed much of the friction after a developer reached Studio. One major hurdle remained before they got there: creating an AWS account. New customers had to verify an email address, enter contact information, verify a phone number, provide payment details, and select a support plan before they could start building. For individual developers and small startup teams used to signing in and building within a few minutes, that was still a significant barrier.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-new"&gt;What’s new&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With this launch, SageMaker Studio is now integrated with the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/aws-reimagines-the-getting-started-experience/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;updated AWS sign-in and sign-up experience&lt;/a&gt;, which introduces new options for creating and managing AWS accounts along with simplified collaboration. New customers can create an account using an identity that they already have and start building in SageMaker Studio right away.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="new-sign-in-experience"&gt;New sign-in experience&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;New customers can now sign up using their Google, GitHub, Apple, or Amazon account, or with an email address, in 2–3 steps. You don’t need to complete the advanced AWS account sign-up, verify a phone number, or enter a credit card (for most customers):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22115-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22115-1-1.png" alt="The Sign up for AWS page with an email field and sign-in options for Google, GitHub, Apple, and Amazon" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The Sign up for AWS page with social sign-on options&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After you sign up, SageMaker AI automatically creates your Studio environment in about 20 seconds. You then land on the page that matches where you started. For example, you might discover and select a model from Hugging Face for deployment through &lt;strong&gt;Deploy on SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt;. Studio then opens the Deployment page with the model pre-configured for endpoint creation:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22115-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22115-2-1.png" alt="Hugging Face deploy dialog for the Qwen3.5-9B model with Deploy on SageMaker AI and Customize on SageMaker AI options" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Deploying a Hugging Face model on Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22115-3-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22115-3-1.png" alt="The Deploy model to endpoint page in SageMaker Studio showing endpoint name, instance type, and instance count settings" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Configuring endpoint settings in SageMaker Studio&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="up-to-200-in-free-tier-credits-no-credit-card-required-for-most-customers"&gt;Up to $200 in Free Tier credits, no credit card required for most customers&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;New customers receive $100 in AWS Free Tier credits that work across SageMaker AI, including endpoint deployments, model customization, Studio notebooks, and model training. You also have the chance to earn an additional $100 worth of credits. Credits are applied automatically to your usage, and you receive an email notification as you approach your limit. The Free Tier period ends after 6 months or when you’ve used all your credits, whichever comes first. At that point, resource usage stops automatically, so you won’t be surprised by unexpected charges. You can upgrade to a paid plan at any time by adding payment details.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="built-on-the-foundations-of-previous-launches"&gt;Built on the foundations of previous launches&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;New environments created through the new sign-in experience include the improvements from earlier launches. Permissions for model customization, training, evaluation, and deployment are pre-configured, so you don’t need to create AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles. Eligible accounts get default access to G5, G6, and G4dn GPU instances for endpoints, training jobs, and Studio notebooks. Additionally, the instance selection menu in Studio shows quota availability, so you can quickly see which instance types are available for use.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can try the new sign-in experience today:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Browse models on &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/models" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Hugging Face&lt;/a&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;, select &lt;strong&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt; and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Customize on SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt; or &lt;strong&gt;Deploy on SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;on a supported model. Alternatively, visit the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/studio/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Studio&lt;/a&gt; page and choose &lt;strong&gt;Get started with Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the AWS sign-in page, choose &lt;strong&gt;Google&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;GitHub&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;Apple&lt;/strong&gt;, or &lt;strong&gt;Amazon&lt;/strong&gt;, or enter your email address, and follow the prompts to create your account in a few steps.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Land in your fully configured SageMaker Studio environment and start building with your Free Tier credits.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The new sign-in experience is available for new AWS accounts. If you already have an AWS account, there’s no change to how you sign in.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The new sign-in experience removes the last major hurdle between discovering and building with Amazon SageMaker AI. Combined with faster environment creation, pre-configured permissions, deep links from Hugging Face, and default GPU access, individual developers can get started quickly. Startup teams can now go from finding a model to customizing or deploying it in minutes, using an identity they already have.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, visit &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/studio/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/studio&lt;/a&gt; or explore models on &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/models" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Hugging Face&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Amazon SageMaker JumpStart: 2026 year-to-date in review</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart-2026-year-to-date-in-review/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Vamsi Goparaju]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 19 Oct 2026 21:14:44 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker JumpStart]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">caf8c9cd8fb47f7d05ebc18e6193092824fb5c28</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker JumpStart onboarded over 100 new models from 30 providers in the first three quarters of 2026. This recap covers the model-onboarding highlights and JumpStart enhancements, including the simplified Model Hub, pre-configured deployment options, and Hugging Face deep link integration.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is a machine learning (ML) hub within Amazon SageMaker AI that provides access to a curated catalog of AI models, solution templates, and example notebooks. It reduces the time it takes to go from model selection to deployment. You can use it to evaluate open source large language models (LLMs), deploy a vision model for document understanding, or fine-tune a compact model on your own data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker JumpStart serves three primary use cases:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model discovery and evaluation — Browse hundreds of foundation models (FMs) from providers like Alibaba (Qwen), NVIDIA, Google, Mistral, Cohere, IBM, and more. Each model card includes architecture details, benchmarks, licensing information, and sample notebooks.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;One-click deployment — Deploy your choice of model to a SageMaker real-time inference endpoint with pre-configured settings optimized for your workload (low cost, low latency, throughput, or balanced). No manual container or instance configuration required.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model customization — Fine-tune models directly from the console or SDK using techniques like low-rank adaptation (LoRA), supervised fine-tuning (SFT), direct preference optimization (DPO), and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Serverless customization options reduce infrastructure management.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can access SageMaker JumpStart through the SageMaker Studio console (Models → JumpStart models), programmatically through the SageMaker Python SDK, or directly from Hugging Face using the recently launched deep link integration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="onboarding-at-a-glance"&gt;2026 onboarding at a glance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From January through September 2026, SageMaker JumpStart onboarded over 100 new models across 30 distinct providers. This represents a significant expansion of the catalog, driven by the accelerating pace of open source model releases and growing customer demand for choice across model architectures, sizes, and capabilities.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here’s a breakdown of the leading contributors and a link to our &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart page&lt;/a&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Alibaba (Qwen):&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/qwen3-family-of-reasoning-models-now-available-in-amazon-bedrock-marketplace-and-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;29 models&lt;/a&gt; spanning reasoning (Qwen 3.8-27B), large-scale mixture-of-experts (Qwen3.5-122B-A10B), agentic workflows (Qwen-AgentWorld-35B-A3B), code generation (Qwen3-Coder-Next, Qwen 3 Coder 30B), speech recognition (Qwen3-ASR-1.7B), text-to-speech (Qwen3-TTS), embeddings (Qwen3-VL-Embedding-2B), and reranking (Qwen3-Reranker-4B).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;NVIDIA:&lt;/strong&gt; 17 models including the Cosmos3 world foundation model family (Super, Edge, Nano), Cosmos Reason 7B, MSA-Search, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/nvidia-nemotron-3-5-lightning-now-available-in-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Nemotron 3.5 Lightning&lt;/a&gt;, Nemotron-3-Super-120B (FP8 and BF16), Nemotron3 Ultra, Nemotron-Nano-12B-v2, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/nvidia-nemotron-3-nano-30b-is-now-available-in-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Nemotron 3 Nano Omni (multimodal)&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/01/nvidia-nemotron-3.5-super-vl-on-sagemaker-jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Nemotron 3.5 Super VL&lt;/a&gt; for vision-language.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Mistral:&lt;/strong&gt; 7 models including the Ministral-3 family (3B, 8B, 14B in standard and BF16) and Voxtral-Mini-4B-Realtime for speech.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Google:&lt;/strong&gt; 6 Gemma 4 variants, including gemma-4-31B-it, E2B-it, E4B-it, 26B-A4B-it, assistant, and NVFP4 quantized.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;IBM:&lt;/strong&gt; 4 models including Granite 4.0 h-micro/small/tiny and granite-speech-4.1-2b for multilingual automatic speech recognition (ASR) across 6 languages.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cohere:&lt;/strong&gt; 4 models including Command A (A100) and Rerank v3.5, v4.0 Fast, and v4.0 Pro for retrieval augmentation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Others:&lt;/strong&gt; 33 models from MiniMax, Zhipu AI (GLM), DeepSeek, Moonshot AI, Allen AI, Microsoft, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/01/muse-spark-open-on-sagemaker-jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Meta (Muse Spark)&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/01/reflection-beam-on-sagemaker-jumpstart-and-aws-trainium/"&gt;Reflection (Beam)&lt;/a&gt;, Black Forest Labs, JetBrains, OpenBMB, BharatGenAI, Kakao, OpenFold, and more.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="model-onboarding-highlights"&gt;Model onboarding highlights&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The models onboarded in 2026 reflect several focused priorities in how we expand the SageMaker JumpStart catalog. These aren’t only industry trends. They represent the areas where we see strong customer demand and significant opportunity to help customers build production AI applications on SageMaker.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="expanding-the-nvidia-open-model-portfolio"&gt;Expanding the NVIDIA open-model portfolio&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NVIDIA is one of our significant model providers, and in 2026 their contribution extended well beyond language models. The Cosmos3 world foundation models, available in Super, Edge, and Nano tiers, bring video generation, physics simulation, and action planning into a single architecture for physical AI applications such as robotics, autonomous systems, and digital twins. Combined with Nemotron models spanning from efficient edge inference (Nano-12B) to large-scale reasoning (Super-120B), SageMaker JumpStart now offers a broad portfolio of NVIDIA open models. If you build on NVIDIA hardware, you can deploy these models on SageMaker with optimized containers and pre-configured endpoints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="closing-the-multimodal-gap"&gt;Closing the multimodal gap&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Customer workloads increasingly require models that process more than text. In 2026, we prioritized onboarding models that handle vision, speech, and document understanding natively. NVIDIA Nemotron 3 Nano Omni processes text, vision, and speech in a single model. Google Gemma 4 adds vision-language capabilities across multiple sizes. IBM granite-speech-4.1-2b handles speech recognition and translation across six languages. Mistral Voxtral-Mini supports real-time speech understanding. These additions mean customers can avoid chaining separate preprocessing models for multimodal workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="making-creative-ai-accessible"&gt;Making creative AI accessible&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Image and video generation moved from niche to mainstream in 2026. We onboarded FLUX.2 Small Decoder and FLUX.2-klein-base-4B from Black Forest Labs for fast text-to-image generation, Meta Muse-Glimmer-30B for multimodal agentic workflows, and NVIDIA Cosmos3 for video generation. With these models, you can access creative AI capabilities with the same one-click deployment experience you use for language models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prioritizing-efficient-and-quantized-models"&gt;Prioritizing efficient and quantized models&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Not every workload requires a frontier-scale model, and many customers are focused on optimizing inference costs as they move to production. We onboarded multiple quantized and compact models: Zhipu AI GLM-5.2 FP8 delivers strong performance with a reduced memory footprint. JetBrains Mellum2-12B uses a mixture-of-experts architecture with only 2.5B active parameters for code generation. OpenBMB MiniCPM5-1B packs multimodal capabilities into 1 billion parameters. Microsoft Phi-4-mini-instruct and IBM Granite 4.0 h-tiny demonstrate that compact models serve production workloads on instance types like ml.g5.xlarge. These models can optimize latency and total cost of ownership (TCO) for targeted use cases.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="onboarding-purpose-built-models-for-targeted-tasks"&gt;Onboarding purpose-built models for targeted tasks&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;General-purpose LLMs are versatile, but purpose-built models are optimized for specific tasks. In 2026, we onboarded Qwen-AgentWorld-35B-A3B for autonomous AI workflows with native tool-calling. GLM-OCR, LightOnOCR, and DeepSeek-OCR handle document text extraction. Qwen3-Reranker-4B and Cohere Rerank v4.0 improve retrieval precision in Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines. langcache-embed-v3-small provides lightweight embedding for semantic search. Qwen3-ASR and Qwen3-TTS bring speech recognition and synthesis. OpenFold3 supports protein structure prediction for scientific computing. With these models, you can build pipelines where each stage uses the specialized tool for the job, rather than forcing a single model to do everything.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="broadening-provider-diversity"&gt;Broadening provider diversity&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reducing concentration risk in model selection is important for enterprise customers. In 2026, we expanded the catalog to include 30 providers, up from a more concentrated set in prior years. Alibaba Qwen is the single largest contributor with 29 models spanning reasoning, coding, speech, and retrieval. Zhipu AI and DeepSeek add competitive LLMs and specialized OCR. MiniMax and Moonshot AI contribute emerging architectures. Cohere brings reliable reranking. BharatGenAI and Kakao expand regional model coverage. This breadth gives customers more options for performance, cost, licensing, and language support.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="sagemaker-jumpstart-enhancements-in-2026"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart enhancements in 2026&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Beyond model onboarding, SageMaker JumpStart also shipped significant improvements in 2026 that reduce friction across the model lifecycle, from discovery to deployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="simplified-model-hub-on-the-sagemaker-ai-console"&gt;Simplified Model Hub on the SageMaker AI console&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Previously, discovering SageMaker JumpStart models required navigating into SageMaker Studio and finding the SageMaker JumpStart section in the left navigation. The new Model Hub experience surfaces the full SageMaker JumpStart catalog directly on the SageMaker AI console landing page. You can browse models by category and filter by input/output modality, publisher, model size, license, and framework. You can also view sample notebooks and open models inside Studio with a single click. This avoids the multi-step navigation that slowed initial exploration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pre-configured-endpoint-deployment-options"&gt;Pre-configured endpoint deployment options&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choosing the right instance type, serving container, and optimization settings for a model deployment typically required manual benchmarking across dozens of combinations. SageMaker JumpStart now offers named deployment configurations, with optimization options for:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Balanced&lt;/strong&gt; — An even tradeoff between cost, latency, and throughput for general-purpose workloads.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput optimized&lt;/strong&gt; — Maximum tokens per second using techniques like Speculative Decoding, ideal for high-volume batch processing.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Latency optimized&lt;/strong&gt; — Lowest Time to First Token (TTFT), prioritizing speed for interactive applications.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost optimized&lt;/strong&gt; — Lowest infrastructure cost per request, suitable for workloads that can accept higher latency.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These configurations include Neuron-optimized setups for AWS Inferentia and AWS Trainium instances, reducing deployment complexity and infrastructure costs. You can deploy a model with a single click and the right optimization profile, rather than spending weeks benchmarking configurations manually.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="hugging-face-deep-link-integration"&gt;Hugging Face deep link integration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A new integration with Hugging Face connects model discovery directly to SageMaker Studio, for onboarded models. When browsing models on Hugging Face, you can choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy on SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt; or &lt;strong&gt;Customize and Deploy on SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt;. You land directly in a configured Studio environment with the model pre-loaded, permissions pre-configured, and GPU quota visibility built in. This avoids the need to set up AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles, create a domain, or configure permissions before experimenting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are three ways to get started with SageMaker JumpStart:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;SageMaker Studio console — Open SageMaker Studio, navigate to Models → JumpStart models, and browse the catalog. Choose a model to view its details, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt; or &lt;strong&gt;Customize&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;SageMaker Python SDK — Deploy models programmatically using the SageMaker Python SDK. Visit the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-jumpstart.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart model catalog&lt;/a&gt; for SDK examples and deployment guides.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Hugging Face deep link — Browse the &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/models" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HuggingFace Model Hub&lt;/a&gt; and choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy on SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;to land directly in Studio for SageMaker JumpStart onboarded models.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each model on SageMaker JumpStart includes deployment configurations, sample notebooks, and documentation. For a hands-on walkthrough, see our related blog post on “&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deploy-and-evaluate-models-with-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deploy and evaluate models with Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;”.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first three quarters of 2026 have been a period of significant progress for Amazon SageMaker JumpStart. With over 100 new models onboarded from 30 providers, the catalog now spans a wide range of capabilities. These include frontier reasoning models, multimodal vision-language models, creative AI for image and video generation, speech recognition and synthesis, optical character recognition (OCR) and document understanding, embedding and retrieval models for RAG, agentic workflow models, and specialized scientific computing models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These onboarding priorities reflect where we see the strongest customer demand and the greatest opportunity to accelerate AI adoption on AWS. They include expanding the portfolio, closing the multimodal gap, making creative AI accessible, prioritizing efficiency and quantization, onboarding purpose-built models, and broadening provider diversity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Combined with the enhancements like simplified Model Hub, pre-configured deployment options, and the Hugging Face deep link integration, SageMaker JumpStart continues to lower the barrier between model discovery and production deployment. Visit the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart model catalog&lt;/a&gt; to explore the full lineup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="next-steps"&gt;Next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are many ways to get started with SageMaker JumpStart. Try one of these out for yourself!&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Evaluate models and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deploy-and-evaluate-models-with-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;choose the right one for you&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Learn how to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/from-hugging-face-to-amazon-sagemaker-studio-in-one-click-2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deploy from HuggingFace to SageMaker Studio&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use pretrained models in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/use-case-based-deployments-on-sagemaker-jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;case-based deployments&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Design a &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-physical-ai-model-factory-with-nvidia-cosmos-3-on-sagemaker-hyperpod/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Physical AI model factory on SageMaker HyperPod&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Pegasus 1.5 from TwelveLabs is now available in Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/pegasus-1-5-from-twelvelabs-is-now-available-in-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ron Tal]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 19 Oct 2026 16:49:19 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundational (100)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">09c6ed66cdab4e2162f30a6ad1493b4fd9f71589</guid>

					<description>TwelveLabs Pegasus 1.5 is now available in Amazon Bedrock. The video language model turns long-form video into structured, timestamped metadata: define the moments that matter and get every matching segment back as JSON, with new video segmentation, multimodal prompting with reference images, and on-the-fly processing of up to two hours of video.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;On Oct 9, 2026 we announced the availability of &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/10/twelve-labs-pegasus-1-5-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;TwelveLabs’ Pegasus 1.5 on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. Pegasus 1.5 is a video language model that turns long-form video into structured, timestamped metadata. You describe the moments that matter to your workflow, and the model returns every matching segment as JSON.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Video represents a significant and complex category of unstructured data, yet most of it remains unsearchable and underutilized. Extracting structured, actionable insights from that content has historically required labor-intensive manual review or fragmented toolchains.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With earlier versions of Pegasus, you can point the model at a clip, ask a question, and get an answer. Now, Pegasus 1.5 analyzes the entire video, finds every segment that matches user-defined criteria, and returns structured, timestamped metadata. It also introduces video segmentation, multimodal prompting with reference images, and on-the-fly processing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Pegasus 1.5 on Amazon Bedrock, you can analyze videos directly from an Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) URI or a base64 string with no pre-indexing required, and your data remains within the AWS data boundary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we cover what’s new in Pegasus 1.5, how the model is packaged on Amazon Bedrock, and how to send your first request. We then walk through video segmentation, structured JSON output, and multimodal prompting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-new-in-pegasus-1.5"&gt;What’s new in Pegasus 1.5&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pegasus 1.5 has the following changes compared to previous versions:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Time-based metadata generation.&lt;/strong&gt; You define the segments, events, or domain-specific cues that matter to your workflow, such as editorial segments, sports plays, speaker changes, or brand appearances. Pegasus 1.5 returns every matching segment as JSON, with start and end times and the requested fields populated.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multimodal prompting.&lt;/strong&gt; A segment definition can combine a text description with a visual reference. This makes it possible to locate a specific product, logo, or person, along with what they are doing on screen.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;On-the-fly processing.&lt;/strong&gt; No ingestion pipeline or pre-indexing step is required. Videos are sent directly from Amazon S3 and results are returned in the response.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Extended processing capacity.&lt;/strong&gt; Pegasus 1.5 analyzes videos up to two hours long in a single request, up from one hour in Pegasus 1.2.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pegasus 1.5 retains and enhances the core capabilities that made Pegasus 1.2 a powerful tool for video understanding, including:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Video-to-text generation&lt;/strong&gt;: Creates detailed textual descriptions based on video content.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Granular visual comprehension&lt;/strong&gt;: Analyzes objects, on-screen text, and numerical content within video frames.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Temporal grounding&lt;/strong&gt;: Accurately identifies timestamps of specific events within videos.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multimodal understanding&lt;/strong&gt;: Combines visual, audio, and textual information for comprehensive analysis.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multilingual support&lt;/strong&gt;: Full support for English, with partial support for Arabic, Chinese, French, German, Italian, Japanese, Korean, Portuguese, Russian, Spanish, Thai, and Vietnamese.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-pegasus-1.5-works-on-amazon-bedrock"&gt;How Pegasus 1.5 works on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pegasus 1.5 takes a video and either a text prompt or a set of segment definitions, and returns text or JSON. You call it on the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; endpoint with the &lt;code&gt;InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt; API, or with &lt;code&gt;InvokeModelWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt; when you want the response streamed as it’s generated.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Setting&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Value&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Model ID&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;twelvelabs.pegasus-1-5-v1:0&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Region&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;US West (Oregon), &lt;code&gt;us-west-2&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Inference&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;On-demand&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;APIs&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;InvokeModelWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each request runs in one of two analysis modes, chosen with the &lt;code&gt;analysisMode&lt;/code&gt; field:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Analysis mode&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;analysisMode&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;You send&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;You get back&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;General&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;general&lt;/code&gt; (default)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A text prompt in &lt;code&gt;inputPrompt&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A text answer in &lt;code&gt;message&lt;/code&gt;, or JSON when you supply a schema&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Time-based metadata&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;time_based_metadata&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Segment definitions in &lt;code&gt;responseFormat&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A &lt;code&gt;segments&lt;/code&gt; list with start and end times and the fields you asked for&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can point the model at video in two ways. An S3 location (&lt;code&gt;s3Location&lt;/code&gt;) takes the object’s &lt;code&gt;uri&lt;/code&gt; and the &lt;code&gt;bucketOwner&lt;/code&gt; account ID, and suits long-form video. A base64 string (&lt;code&gt;base64String&lt;/code&gt;) embeds the video in the request body, up to 25 MB, and suits short clips.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To track cost by application, create an application inference profile from the Pegasus 1.5 model, tag it, and call it by its Amazon Resource Name (ARN) in place of the model ID.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started-with-pegasus-1.5"&gt;Getting started with Pegasus 1.5&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following section outlines how to get started with Pegasus 1.5.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, make sure you have:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An active AWS account with Amazon Bedrock access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.10 or later, with the AWS SDK for Python (Boto3) installed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions for &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model access enabled for TwelveLabs Pegasus 1.5 in the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock console&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Videos stored in an Amazon S3 bucket, or short clips you can send inline.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following policy grants both invoke actions on Pegasus 1.5.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "bedrock:InvokeModel",
                "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"
            ],
            "Resource": [
                "arn:aws:bedrock:us-west-2::foundation-model/twelvelabs.pegasus-1-5-v1:0"
            ]
        }
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Boto3 signs requests with your usual AWS credentials. You can also authenticate with an Amazon Bedrock API key by setting it in the &lt;code&gt;AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK&lt;/code&gt; environment variable. For production, prefer a short-term key generated from your IAM credentials with the &lt;code&gt;aws-bedrock-token-generator&lt;/code&gt; package, because it expires automatically. Bearer-token authentication also requires the &lt;code&gt;bedrock:CallWithBearerToken&lt;/code&gt; permission.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="send-your-first-request"&gt;Send your first request&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Install Boto3:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;pip install boto3&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example analyzes a video stored in Amazon S3 in general mode. This example uses the global Cross Region Inference (CRIS) endpoint. To target the US geographic endpoint instead, replace “global.” with “us.” in the endpoint URL.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3
import json
from botocore.config import Config

AWS_REGION = "us-west-2"
MODEL_ID = "global.twelvelabs.pegasus-1-5-v1:0"

bedrock_runtime = boto3.client(
    service_name="bedrock-runtime",
    region_name=AWS_REGION,
    config=Config(
        # keep a longer read_timeout for lengthier invocations such as video segmentation
        read_timeout=900,
        retries={"max_attempts": 0},
        connect_timeout=10,
    ),
)

# Analyze a video stored in Amazon S3
payload = {
    "inputPrompt": "Summarize this video and identify the key topics discussed.",
    "mediaSource": {
        "s3Location": {
            "uri": "s3://amzn-s3-demo-pegasus-15/your-video.mp4",
            "bucketOwner": "your-account-id"
        }
    },
    "temperature": 0.2,
    "maxOutputTokens": 4096,
    "includeMetering": True
}

response = bedrock_runtime.invoke_model(
    modelId=MODEL_ID,
    body=json.dumps(payload)
)
result = json.loads(response["body"].read())
print(result["message"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The response carries the answer in &lt;code&gt;message&lt;/code&gt;, the token counts in &lt;code&gt;input_tokens&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;output_tokens&lt;/code&gt;, and the reason generation ended in &lt;code&gt;stopReason&lt;/code&gt;. A &lt;code&gt;stopReason&lt;/code&gt; of &lt;code&gt;length&lt;/code&gt; means the answer reached &lt;code&gt;maxOutputTokens&lt;/code&gt; before it finished, so check it before you rely on the output:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;if result["stopReason"] == "length":
    print("The answer was cut off. Raise maxOutputTokens and try again.")
print("Tokens in:", result["input_tokens"], "out:", result["output_tokens"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Request fields for general mode:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Field&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Required&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Description&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;inputPrompt&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The question or instruction about the video&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;mediaSource.s3Location.uri&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One video source is required&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;S3 URI of the video&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;mediaSource.s3Location.bucketOwner&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;With &lt;code&gt;uri&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS account ID that owns the bucket&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;mediaSource.base64String&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One video source is required&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Base64-encoded video, up to 25 MB&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;temperature&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Randomness of the output, from 0 to 1. Default 0.2&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;maxOutputTokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Maximum tokens to generate, from 512 to 98,304&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;responseFormat&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A JSON Schema the answer must follow&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;analysisMode&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;general&lt;/code&gt; (default) or &lt;code&gt;time_based_metadata&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a short clip, you can send the video inline instead of from S3:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import base64

with open("your-video.mp4", "rb") as f:
    video_b64 = base64.b64encode(f.read()).decode("utf-8")

payload["mediaSource"] = {"base64String": video_b64}
response = bedrock_runtime.invoke_model(modelId=MODEL_ID, body=json.dumps(payload))
print(json.loads(response["body"].read())["message"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Note that the preceding example uses the optional &lt;code&gt;includeMetering&lt;/code&gt; attribute. &lt;code&gt;includeMetering: True&lt;/code&gt; makes Pegasus 1.5 add two billing-usage fields to the non-streaming response: &lt;code&gt;outputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;inputVideoSecond&lt;/code&gt; (the analyzed video’s duration in seconds).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="stream-the-response"&gt;Stream the response&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For interactive applications, &lt;code&gt;InvokeModelWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt; returns the answer in chunks as it’s generated. Each chunk carries the next piece of text in &lt;code&gt;message&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = bedrock_runtime.invoke_model_with_response_stream(
    modelId=MODEL_ID,
    body=json.dumps(payload)
)
for event in response["body"]:
    chunk = json.loads(event["chunk"]["bytes"])
    print(chunk.get("message", ""), end="", flush=True)
print()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="return-structured-json"&gt;Return structured JSON&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In general mode, you can constrain the answer to a JSON Schema so downstream code can parse it directly. Set &lt;code&gt;responseFormat.type&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;json_schema&lt;/code&gt; and put the schema under &lt;code&gt;jsonSchema&lt;/code&gt;. The answer arrives in &lt;code&gt;message&lt;/code&gt; as a JSON string:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;payload = {
    "inputPrompt": "Analyze this video and return a structured summary.",
    "mediaSource": {
        "s3Location": {
            "uri": "s3://amzn-s3-demo-pegasus-15/your-video.mp4",
            "bucketOwner": "your-account-id"
        }
    },
    "temperature": 0,
    "maxOutputTokens": 2048,
    "includeMetering": True,
    "responseFormat": {
        "type": "json_schema",
        "jsonSchema": {
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {
                "summary": {"type": "string"},
                "key_scenes": {"type": "array", "items": {"type": "string"}},
                "setting": {"type": "string"}
            },
            "required": ["summary", "key_scenes", "setting"]
        }
    }
}
response = bedrock_runtime.invoke_model(modelId=MODEL_ID, body=json.dumps(payload))
summary = json.loads(json.loads(response["body"].read())["message"])
print(summary["summary"])
for scene in summary["key_scenes"]:
    print(" -", scene)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="video-segmentation-turning-video-into-structured-data"&gt;Video segmentation: Turning video into structured data&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Time-based metadata generation is where Pegasus 1.5 differs most from earlier versions. Instead of a prompt, you send one or more segment definitions, and the model returns every segment of the video that matches each one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set &lt;code&gt;analysisMode&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;time_based_metadata&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;responseFormat.type&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;segment_definitions&lt;/code&gt;. The definitions take the place of &lt;code&gt;inputPrompt&lt;/code&gt;, so leave that field out. Each definition has:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;id&lt;/code&gt;: a name for the definition, returned on every segment it matches.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;description&lt;/code&gt;: what a matching segment looks like.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;fields&lt;/code&gt;: the values to extract for each segment, each with a &lt;code&gt;name&lt;/code&gt;, a &lt;code&gt;type&lt;/code&gt; (&lt;code&gt;string&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;boolean&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;number&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;integer&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;array&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;timestamp&lt;/code&gt;), and a &lt;code&gt;description&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example identifies each editorial section in a news broadcast and returns a title, summary, and key topics for each one:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;payload = {
    "analysisMode": "time_based_metadata",
    "mediaSource": {
        "s3Location": {
            "uri": "s3://amzn-s3-demo-pegasus-15/news-broadcast.mp4",
            "bucketOwner": "your-account-id"
        }
    },
    "responseFormat": {
        "type": "segment_definitions",
        "segmentTimeFormat": "hh:mm:ss",
        "segmentDefinitions": [
            {
                "id": "editorial_section",
                "description": "A distinct editorial section of the broadcast, "
                "such as a new story, topic, or segment of the program.",
                "fields": [
                    {"name": "segment_title", "type": "string",
                     "description": "A descriptive title"},
                    {"name": "summary", "type": "string",
                     "description": "A brief summary of the segment content"},
                    {"name": "key_topics", "type": "array", "items": {"type": "string"},
                     "description": "Main topics discussed"}
                ]
            }
        ]
    },
    "temperature": 0,
    "maxOutputTokens": 32768,
    "includeMetering": True
}
response = bedrock_runtime.invoke_model(
    modelId=MODEL_ID,
    contentType="application/json",
    accept="application/json",
    body=json.dumps(payload)
)
result = json.loads(response["body"].read())
for segment in result["segments"]:
    print(segment["start_sec"], "-", segment["end_sec"], segment["segment_title"])
    print(" ", segment["summary"])
    print(" topics:", ", ".join(segment["key_topics"]))&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The previous sample code when tested against a sports clip may produce an output like the following sample.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "segments": [
        {
            "start_sec": "00:04:24",
            "end_sec": "00:05:22",
            "segment_title": "Substitutions and Added Time",
            "summary": "The referee announces three minutes of added time, and both teams make substitutions in preparation for a potential penalty shootout.",
            "key_topics": ["Substitutions", "Added time", "Penalty shootout preparation", "Tactical changes"]
        },
        {
            "start_sec": "00:05:22",
            "end_sec": "00:08:57",
            "segment_title": "Last Gasp Attempts and Final Whistle",
            "summary": "The final minutes of the match see intense action from both sides, with several close calls before the referee blows the final whistle, ending the game in a 3-3 draw.",
            "key_topics": ["Final whistle", "Draw"]
        },
        {
            "start_sec": "00:08:57",
            "end_sec": "00:09:29",
            "segment_title": "Match Conclusion and Penalty Shootout Setup",
            "summary": "The match concludes with a 3-3 draw, leading to a penalty shootout. Players from both teams shake hands and prepare for the decisive moments.",
            "key_topics": ["Match conclusion", "Penalty shootout", "Player handshakes", "Final"]
        }
    ],
    "definition_errors": [],
    "input_tokens": 249408,
    "output_tokens": 802,
    "stopReason": "stop",
    "finish_reason": "stop"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each segment carries &lt;code&gt;start_sec&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;end_sec&lt;/code&gt;, and the &lt;code&gt;fields&lt;/code&gt; you defined. &lt;code&gt;segmentTimeFormat&lt;/code&gt; controls how the times are written: &lt;code&gt;seconds&lt;/code&gt; returns numbers you can do arithmetic on, while &lt;code&gt;hh:mm:ss&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;hh:mm:ss.fff&lt;/code&gt; return readable timestamps as strings. The response also includes a &lt;code&gt;definition_errors&lt;/code&gt; list, which is empty when every definition was processed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A single request can include multiple segment definitions, for example one for editorial sections and another for on-screen brand appearances. Pegasus 1.5 returns the matches for all of them in one &lt;code&gt;segments&lt;/code&gt; list, and tags each segment with &lt;code&gt;_definition_id&lt;/code&gt; so you can tell them apart. The following example adds a second definition to the previous request and groups the results:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from collections import defaultdict

payload["responseFormat"]["segmentDefinitions"].append({
    "id": "brand_appearance",
    "description": "A shot where a company logo or branded product "
    "is clearly visible on screen.",
    "fields": [
        {"name": "brand", "type": "string", "description": "The brand shown"},
        {"name": "is_prominent", "type": "boolean",
         "description": "Whether the brand is the main focus of the shot"}
    ]
})
response = bedrock_runtime.invoke_model(modelId=MODEL_ID, body=json.dumps(payload))
result = json.loads(response["body"].read())
by_definition = defaultdict(list)
for segment in result["segments"]:
    by_definition[segment["_definition_id"]].append(segment)
for definition_id, segments in by_definition.items():
    print(definition_id, len(segments), "segments")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the schema is defined once, the same definitions can be applied across an entire video library, producing consistent metadata for every asset.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="multimodal-prompting-adding-visual-context-to-your-queries"&gt;Multimodal prompting: Adding visual context to your queries&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pegasus 1.5 introduces multimodal prompting, so you can include reference images alongside your text prompts. This is useful when you want to ask the model about specific objects, people, or scenes that are easier to show than describe.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For example, you can provide a product image and ask the model to identify every moment where that product appears in a video. Or you can provide a reference image of a specific logo and ask the model to detect brand appearances and their timestamps. This capability enhances the precision of video analysis, and opens up use cases such as visual brand monitoring, product placement analysis, and image-guided content search.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reference images attach differently depending on the mode. In general mode, put them in &lt;code&gt;promptV2.mediaSources&lt;/code&gt;, alongside the question in &lt;code&gt;promptV2.inputText&lt;/code&gt;. In segmentation mode (&lt;code&gt;analysisMode: time_based_metadata&lt;/code&gt;), attach them to the &lt;code&gt;mediaSources&lt;/code&gt; of each segment definition inside &lt;code&gt;responseFormat.segmentDefinitions&lt;/code&gt;. A definition can then match a visual reference as well as a text description. Each image needs a &lt;code&gt;name&lt;/code&gt;, a &lt;code&gt;media_type&lt;/code&gt; of &lt;code&gt;image&lt;/code&gt;, and exactly one source: either an inline &lt;code&gt;base64String&lt;/code&gt; or an &lt;code&gt;s3Location&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;uri&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;bucketOwner&lt;/code&gt;. A single request can carry up to 30 reference images.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A general-mode request asks the model about an object shown in a reference image. The following sample payload and response provide an example in general mode.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;payload = {
    "analysisMode": "general",
    "mediaSource": {"s3Location": {"uri": "s3://...", "bucketOwner": "..."}},
    "promptV2": {
        "inputText": "Does the object in the reference image appear in this video?",
        "mediaSources": [
            {"name": "ref1", "media_type": "image", "base64String": "..."}
        ]
    }
    …
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "input_tokens": 144,
    "output_tokens": 11,
    "message": "Yes, the green canoe appears in the video.",
    "stopReason": "stop"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In segmentation mode, the reference image lives inside the definition, so Pegasus returns only the segments where that product appears. The following sample payload and response provide an example in segmentation mode.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;payload = {
    "analysisMode": "time_based_metadata",
    "mediaSource": {"s3Location": {"uri": "s3://...", "bucketOwner": "..."}},
    "responseFormat": {
        "type": "segment_definitions",
        "segmentTimeFormat": "hh:mm:ss",
        "segmentDefinitions": [
            {
                "id": "product_appearance",
                "description": "A shot showing the product in the reference image",
                "fields": [
                    {"name": "what", "type": "string", "description": "what is on screen"}
                ],
                "mediaSources": [
                    {"name": "ref1", "media_type": "image",
                     "s3Location": {"uri": "s3://...", "bucketOwner": "..."}}
                ]
            }
        ]
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "segments": [
        {"start_sec": "00:00:00", "end_sec": "00:00:02",
         "what": "A family of four in a green canoe, paddling on a calm river surrounded by towering red rock formations."},
        {"start_sec": "00:00:09", "end_sec": "00:00:10",
         "what": "The family back in the canoe, paddling away from the shore, with the children smiling and enjoying the serene surroundings."}
    ],
    "definition_errors": [],
    "input_tokens": 4498,
    "output_tokens": 292,
    "stopReason": "stop",
    "finish_reason": "stop"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="monitoring-usage"&gt;Monitoring usage&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pegasus 1.5 publishes Amazon CloudWatch metrics in the &lt;code&gt;AWS/Bedrock&lt;/code&gt; namespace under its model ID, including &lt;code&gt;Invocations&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;OutputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;InvocationLatency&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;InvocationClientErrors&lt;/code&gt;. Use them to track volume and latency. Pair them with application inference profiles when you need usage broken down by application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-cases"&gt;Use cases&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pegasus 1.5 is designed for organizations that manage large volumes of long-form video:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;News and broadcast archives&lt;/strong&gt;: Teams can define editorial segments, topics, and speaker changes once, then catalog years of footage that was previously searchable only by transcript keywords.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sports broadcasters and leagues&lt;/strong&gt;: Detect plays, scoring events, and other key moments across full-length game footage, speeding up highlight production.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Streaming applications&lt;/strong&gt;: Scene-level metadata supports moment-based recommendations, contextual ad placement, and segment-level brand safety review.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In each case, people remain part of the workflow. Their work shifts from finding and tagging every moment by hand to reviewing and refining the metadata generated by the model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="input-requirements"&gt;Input requirements&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pegasus 1.5 supports the following video specifications:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Duration&lt;/strong&gt;: Up to 2 hours.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;File size&lt;/strong&gt;: Up to 2 GB from Amazon S3, or up to 25 MB as a base64 string.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resolution&lt;/strong&gt;: 360×360 to 5184×2160.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Aspect ratio&lt;/strong&gt;: Between 1:1 and 2.4:1, or their inverses.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Formats&lt;/strong&gt;: FFmpeg-supported formats.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

Pegasus 1.5 is billed through AWS Marketplace, so its charges appear on your AWS bill and in AWS Cost Explorer under TwelveLabs rather than Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Deepgram optimized voice AI at scale with AWS Trainium</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-deepgram-optimized-voice-ai-at-scale-with-aws-trainium/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Pierre Lienhart]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 19 Oct 2026 16:23:22 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Trainium]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">0b1e8b84bdaaccec8fd8f6adeda3c1b7cbd14074</guid>

					<description>Deepgram worked with the AWS Forward Deployed Engineering team to port its Nova-3 speech-to-text model to AWS Trainium2 for offline batch inference. This post covers porting to the AWS Neuron stack, kernel-level tuning, a data-parallel Rust runtime, and the throughput and cost results on batch transcription.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;As voice becomes a first-class modality for AI, Deepgram’s models span speech-to-text, text-to-speech, and voice agents. They now process quadrillions of audio tokens a year for more than 200,000 developers and 1,400 organizations. Customers use them through the Deepgram API or run them in their own environments, including on Amazon SageMaker AI. &lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Deepgram, serving efficiency determines how much audio the system can process for a given infrastructure budget. High-volume batch workloads put heavy pressure on that budget, so the more efficiently each accelerator hour is used, the more audio Deepgram can serve on the same fleet. That made inference efficiency a natural target and Deepgram turned to AWS AI chips to improve it. They worked with AWS to bring its Nova-3 speech-to-text model to AWS Trainium2 for offline batch workloads such as contact-center analytics and medical transcription.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The engagement began with Deepgram delivering Nova-3 as standard PyTorch code. Over several months, Deepgram and the AWS Forward Deployed Engineering (FDE) team ported Nova-3 to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/trainium/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Trainium2&lt;/a&gt; in PyTorch reconstruction and tuned it for the new hardware. The final configuration runs eight model replicas on a single Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) trn2.3xlarge instance. On Deepgram’s offline, throughput-oriented batch workloads, that setup processed 11–70 percent more audio per dollar than the compute options Deepgram had been running. We measured this using instance pricing at the time of the evaluation. These gains help Deepgram scale efficiently, keep shipping better models, and bring them to customers at lower prices. These benefits can extend to customers who self-host Deepgram models in their own virtual private cloud (VPC) on Amazon SageMaker AI. There, Deepgram and AWS continue to improve ease of setup, reliability, and observability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through the full inference optimization effort on Trainium: porting Nova-3 to the AWS Neuron software stack, the kernel-level tuning that followed, the data-parallel Rust runtime that lets Deepgram’s own model server call Trainium, and the throughput and cost results we measured on batch transcription.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="optimizing-nova-3-to-trainium2-with-native-pytorch"&gt;Optimizing Nova-3 to Trainium2 with native PyTorch&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Starting with standard PyTorch code, we used TorchNeuron, the native PyTorch integration of the Neuron Compiler, which supports both eager execution and &lt;code&gt;torch.compile&lt;/code&gt;. We ran Nova-3 in eager mode on Trainium2 first and checked which operations fell back to the CPU, then compiled with &lt;code&gt;torch.compile&lt;/code&gt;. The compiled path ran every model operation on Trainium2 with no CPU fallbacks detected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We compiled the encoder and decoder as separate static-shape graphs using &lt;code&gt;torch.compile&lt;/code&gt;. Throughout, we checked numerical accuracy at two levels: layer-level output consistency against CPU, and the end-to-end Word Error Rate (WER), which had to stay in line with Deepgram’s reference values and target.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From that working baseline, we kicked off the optimization work by replacing several layers with fused kernels from the public NKI Library (NKILib), a collection of pre-built reference kernels written with the Neuron Kernel Interface (NKI). The kernels covered operations such as fused QKV projections, fused feed-forward network (FFN) layers, and depthwise 1D convolution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We optimized for throughput and, by extension, cost efficiency, targeting Deepgram’s offline inference workloads. Throughput is reported as a relative gain over a baseline configuration rather than as absolute audio processed per second, and we swept batch sizes 1–128 to find the throughput-maximizing batch size.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="kernel-level-tuning-with-neuron-explorer-and-nad"&gt;Kernel-level tuning with Neuron Explorer and NAD&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enabling kernels is only the first step of an optimization flywheel: enable an NKI kernel (custom or from NKILib), profile the workload, then either tune the existing kernel or replace it with a new one based on the profiling insights. This cycle repeats until the performance target is met. At each step, a different part of the Neuron stack accelerates the work: NKILib provides drop-in replacement kernels, Neuron Explorer is the go-to tool for profiling Neuron workloads, and the Neuron Agentic Development (NAD) toolkit speeds up profiling analysis and kernel tuning through its profiling and kernel-writing skills.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In our case, the NKILib kernels got us most of the way, and profiling showed where a hand-written kernel would do better. Each time, the NAD profiling skills took us from a Neuron Explorer profile to a root cause, and the NAD kernel-writing skills helped produce the replacement kernels.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Layer&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What we found and changed&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Gain&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;FFN&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The reference kernel’s tiling schedule reloaded the full set of projection weights from off-chip memory for every token tile, so memory bandwidth limited throughput as the token count grew. Our hand-tuned kernel loads each weight tile once, reuses it across multiple token tiles, and pipelines weight reads with on-chip compute.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2.0x lower latency compared to the reference kernel at batch size 96&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Depthwise conv1d&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The experimental NKILib kernel framed the operation as an implicit general matrix multiply (GEMM), which left Tensor Engine utilization low. We rewrote it as a broadcast multiply-accumulate loop on the Vector Engine, reaching full engine utilization, enough to more than offset the Vector Engine’s lower peak throughput.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1.5x speedup compared to the reference kernel at batch size 96&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Conv2d&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The model’s conv2d layers hit a compilation bottleneck: beyond batch size 32, these layers prevented the model from compiling as a single graph. Since NKILib had no conv2d kernel, we wrote one from scratch in NKI. The kernel went through several profiler-informed iterations following the flywheel described earlier, unlocking batch sizes above 32.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Up to 50x on the convolution module at batch sizes ≤32&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="data-parallel-runtime-in-rust"&gt;Data-parallel runtime in Rust&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each Trainium2 device features eight physical NeuronCores, exposed by default as four logical NeuronCores (LNC2). Because each Nova-3 model instance fits comfortably on a single physical core, we reduced the number of physical cores per logical core to one (LNC1). This configuration lets us host eight model replicas (DP8) per Trainium2 device. Inputs are processed concurrently while maximizing per-device throughput. The data-parallel runtime is driven by a work-stealing queue with one thread per replica.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deepgram’s production serving stack is written in Rust, so porting Nova-3 to Trainium2 required more than compiling the model: it also had to run without Python or PyTorch in the serving path. We therefore built a lightweight runtime in which Rust coordinates model replicas through a shared work-stealing queue. A C layer loads and executes ahead-of-time-compiled Neuron Executable File Format (NEFFs) directly through the Neuron Runtime API (&lt;code&gt;libnrt&lt;/code&gt;). The runtime integrates directly with Deepgram’s serving architecture and reproduced the reference token sequences exactly at every tested batch size.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We benchmarked it against multithreaded (MT) and multiprocessing (MP) Python data-parallel runtimes. Compared with the MP baseline, the Rust runtime removes the overhead of the Python interpreter entirely. Compared with the MT baseline, it further alleviates global interpreter lock (GIL) serialization, so dispatch calls from concurrent worker threads are no longer contended within a single process. These gains matter because Nova-3 performs a sequence of small decoder calls for each audio chunk, making host-side dispatch the bottleneck at low batch sizes. The throughput improvement is therefore largest there, and remains meaningful even at the throughput-maximizing batch size. There, the Rust runtime delivered a 7.5% throughput gain over the multiprocessing Python baseline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-21906-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-21906-1-1.png" alt="Nova-3 encoder and decoder compiled ahead of time to NEFFs and served by a data-parallel Rust runtime on AWS Trainium2" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Deepgram Nova-3 on AWS Trainium2, compiled ahead of time and served from Rust&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cost-saving-impact"&gt;Cost saving impact&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We measured price-performance as audio processed per dollar. On Deepgram’s offline, throughput-oriented batch workloads, the single-device Trainium2 setup (trn2.3xlarge) processed 11–70 percent more audio per dollar than the compute options Deepgram had been running.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we demonstrated how Deepgram and the AWS FDE ported the Nova-3 speech-to-text PyTorch model to Trainium hardware using TorchNeuron, which provides native PyTorch framework integration for AWS Trainium. We improved performance by replacing layers directly with pre-built kernels from NKILib. We then assessed performance using Neuron Explorer and used the Neuron Agentic Development (NAD) profiling skills to query profiling data and accelerate performance troubleshooting. Building on these insights, we adapted kernel source code using the NAD kernel-writing skills. Finally, we built a lightweight Rust data-parallel runtime for AWS Trainium, calling the Neuron Runtime API from Rust. Our setup demonstrated cost savings in offline, throughput-oriented scenarios, validating AWS Trainium as a viable and efficient option for Deepgram’s inference workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To try this approach on your own models, start with the &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Neuron documentation&lt;/a&gt;, which covers the compiler, the runtime, and the supported frameworks. The &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/general/nki/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Neuron Kernel Interface (NKI) guide&lt;/a&gt; explains how to write the kernels described in this post, and the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-neuron/nki-samples" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NKI samples repository&lt;/a&gt; holds the reference kernels we started from. The &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/tools/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Neuron developer tools documentation&lt;/a&gt; covers the profiling and agentic tooling we used here, including Neuron Explorer and Neuron Agentic Development. For instance sizes and pricing, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/instance-types/trn2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EC2 Trn2 Instances&lt;/a&gt;. For help scoping a similar port, contact the AWS Forward Deployed Engineering.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

With nearly a decade at Amazon, he has contributed to machine learning initiatives that significantly impact Amazon’s retail catalog. Rohit holds an MBA from The University of Chicago Booth School of Business and a Master’s degree from Carnegie Mellon University.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
	</channel>
</rss>
